PREFACE

This Report has been prepared for submission to the Governor of the State
of Jammu and Kashmir under Article 151 of the Constitution of India.
Chapter-1 of this Report relating to Introduction on Social, General,
Revenue and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs) covers profiles of audited
entities, authority for audit, planning and conduct of audit and responses of
the departments to audit.

Chapter-2 of this Report deals with the audit findings of Performance Audit,
Thematic Audit, Chief Controlling Officer based Audit of a Government
Department and Transaction audit paragraphs of Social, General, Revenue
and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs).

Chapter-3 of this Report gives a broad overview of the revenue receipts of
the State Government viz., trends of revenue of the State Government,
analysis of collection of tax and non-tax revenue, analysis of arrears of
revenue, etc. during the period 2007-12.

Chapter-4 of this Report deals with the findings of performance audit on
Taxes on Services and Assessment and collection of water usage charges
besides transaction audit paragraphs of Commercial Taxes Department.

The Report containing audit observations on matters arising from
examination of Finance Accounts and Appropriation Accounts of the
Government of Jammu and Kashmir for the Year ended 31 March 2012 is
presented separately under the heading Report on State Finances for the year
ended 31 March 2012.

The Report containing audit findings of Public Sector Undertakings of the
Government of Jammu and Kashmir for the year ended 31 March 2012 is
also presented separately under the heading Report on Social, General and
Economic Sectors (Public Sector Undertakings) for the year ended 31
March 2012.

The cases mentioned in the Report are among those which came to notice in
the course of test audit of accounts during the year 2011-12 as well as those
which had come to notice in earlier years but could not be dealt with in
previous Reports; matters relating to the period subsequent to 2011-12 have
also been included, wherever necessary.

The Audit has been conducted in conformity with the Auditing Standards
issued (March 2002) by the Comptroller and Auditor General of India.

Introduction

on Social, General and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs)



CHAPTER-I
Introduction on Social, General and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs)
11 Introduction
Chapter-1 of this Report provides a broad overview of the profile of the Audited entities
of the State Government Departments and non-corporatized agencies viz., autonomous
bodies, budgetary allocations under different sectors to these departments/ bodies during
the last five years (2007-12) and the coverage thereof by Audit during 2011-12.

Chapter-2 of this Report deals with the findings of performance audit, thematic audit,
observations on audit of transactions in Government Departments and Autonomous
Bodies and results of Chief Controlling Officer (CCO) based audit of the Industries and
Commerce Department.

Chapter-3 of this Report gives a broad overview of the revenue receipts of the State
Government viz., trends of revenue of the State Government, analysis of collection of tax
and non-tax revenue, analysis of arrears of revenue, etc. during the period 2007-12.

Chapter-4 of this Report deals with the findings of performance audit on Taxes on
Services and Assessment and collection of water usage charges besides transaction audit
paragraphs of Commercial Taxes Department.

The primary purpose of this Report is to bring to the notice of the State Legislature, the
important results of audit. Auditing standards require that the materiality level of
reporting should be commensurate with the nature, volume and magnitude of
transactions. The findings of audit are expected to enable the Executives of the
Government to take corrective actions as also to frame policies and directives that result
in improved financial management of the organizations, thus contributing to better
governance of the State.

Compliance audit refers to examination of the transactions relating to expenditure,
receipts, assets and liabilities of the audited entities to ascertain whether the provisions of
the Constitution of India, the Constitution of Jammu and Kashmir, applicable laws, rules,
regulations and various orders and instructions issued by the competent authorities are
being complied with.

Performance audit is an independent assessment or examination of the extent to which an
organization, programme or scheme operates economically, efficiently and effectively.
1.2. Profile of audited entities

The Government departments have been grouped in Social Sector, General Sector,
Revenue Sector and Economic Sector (Non-PSUs) and Public Sector Undertakings of all
Sectors for the purpose of audit. This Report covers audit observations on Social Sector,
General Sector, Revenue Sector and Economic Sector (Non-PSUs).



Audit Report on Social, General, Revenue and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs) for the year ended 31 March 2012

There are 29 departments at the Secretariat level, headed by Chief Secretary/Financial
Commissioner/ Principal Secretaries/ Secretaries who are assisted by the Special
Secretaries, Deputy Secretaries, Directors and Subordinates officers under them for 5549
units and 38 Autonomous Bodies of the State which are under the audit jurisdiction of the
Principal Accountant General (PAG) (Audit), Jammu and Kashmir on behalf of the CAG.

During 2011-12, a total number of 825 units of 28 departments were covered in Audit.

1.4 Authority for audit

Authority for audit by the CAG is derived from the Articles 149 and 151 of the
Constitution of India and the Comptroller and Auditor General’s (Duties, Powers and
Conditions of Service) Act, 1971. The CAG conducts audit of expenditure of the
departments of the Government of Jammu and Kashmir under Section 13’ of the CAG’s
(DPC) Act. The CAG is the sole auditor in respect of eight Autonomous Bodies which
are audited under Sections 1903)? and 20(1)° of the CAG’s (DPC) Act. In addition, the
CAG also conducts audit of 30 other Autonomous Bodies, which are substantially funded
by the Government, under Section 141 of the CAG’s (DPC) Act. The principles and
methodologies for various audits are prescribed in the Auditing Standards (March 2002)
and the Regulations on Audit and Accounts issued by the CAG in 2007.

Audit of (i) all transactions from the Consolidated Fund of the State, (ii) all transactions relating to
the Contingency Fund and Public Accounts and (iii) all trading, manufacturing, profit and loss
accounts, balance sheets and other subsidiary accounts.

Audit of the accounts of Corporations (not being Companies) established by or under law made by
the State Legislature in accordance with the provisions of the respective legislations.

Audit of accounts of any body or authority on the request of the Governor, on such terms and
conditions as may be agreed upon between the CAG and the Government.

Audit of all (i) receipts and expenditure of a body/ authority substantially financed by grants or
loans from the Consolidated Fund of the State and (ii) all receipts and expenditure of any body or
authority where the grants or loans to such body or authority from the Consolidated Fund of the
State in a financial year is not less than € one crore.

3



Audit Report on Social, General, Revenue and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs) for the year ended 31 March 2012

1.5 Organisational structure of the office of the PAG (Audit), Jammu & Kashmir

Under the directions of the CAG, the Principal Accountant General (Audit), Jammu and.
Kashmir conducts audit of the State Government departments, Autonomous Bodies and
Government Companies. The Principal Accountant General (Audit) is assisted by three
Deputy Accountants General, Audit Officers, Assistant Audit Officers and supporting
staff in the discharge of his official duties.

During the year 2011-12, 42 audit parties comprising of 101 audit personnel and
supervised by Deputy Accountants General conducted audit of the selected units from
various departments of the State Government, autonomous bodies and externally aided
projects, etc.

1.6 Planning and conduct of audit

Audit process commences with the assessment of risk of various Government
departments/organizations/autonomous bodies and schemes/projects, etc., based on
expenditure, criticality/ complexity of activities, level of delegated financial powers,
assessment of internal controls and the concerns of stakeholders. The previous audit
findings are also considered as an input to this exercise.

After completion of audit of each unit, Audit Inspection Reports containing audit findings
are issued to the Head of the unit/ Department. The units are requested to furnish replies
to the audit findings within one month of receipt of the Audit Inspection Report.
Whenever replies are received, audit findings are either settled or further action for
compliance is advised. The important audit observations arising out of these Audit
Inspection Reports are processed for inclusion in the CAG’s Audit Report.

During 2011-12, 5525 party-days were utilised to carry out compliance audit of 825 units
out of 5905 units of various departments/organisations. The Audit Plan covered those
units/ entities which were vulnerable to significant risk, as per the assessment.

1.7 Significant audit observations

A synopsis of the important audit findings relating to the performance audits; Sheri-i-
Kashmir Institute of Medical Sciences (SKIMS), Mahatma Gandhi National Rural
Employment Guarantee scheme (MGNREGS), Power Transmission Utilities, Thematic
Audit on Functioning of Jammu and Kashmir Building and Other Construction Workers
Welfare Board, CCO Based audit of the Industries and Commerce Department and
transaction audit paragraphs included in Chapter-2 are given as under:

A. PERFORMANCE AUDIT

Sher-i-Kashmir Institute of Medical Sciences

Sher-i-Kashmir Institute of Medical Science (SKIMS) Srinagar was established in
December 1982 to provide specialized medical care, need based education in medical and
health services and clinical research and to develop referral linkage between primary,
secondary and tertiary health care in the State. The performance review revealed the
following financial and operational deficiencies:



Chapter-1-Intraduction

Financial deficiencies: The financial management was deficient as cases of under-
utilisation of funds, irregular withdrawals for crediting of funds to the Civil Deposit
heads and unauthorised retention and utilisation of hospital receipts were noticed in audit.
Operational deficiencies: The delivery of patient care services was affected by longer
waiting periods for diagnostic tests/ investigations and surgeries besides
mis-utilisation of ambulances meant for patients. The medical research activities had not
been attended to and the capacity of medical courses was not fully utilised. Audit also
noticed that the procurement system for purchase of drugs and equipment was deficient.
The quality and efficacy of drugs is sought to be ensured through higher priced
procurements and the funds released for setting up an Advanced Quality Assurance
Laboratory were lying unspent in a Civil Deposit. The hospital administration reported
that there were shortages of medical, paramedical and nursing staff and faculty though
the manpower requirements were not properly assessed/ benchmarked.

RURAL DEVELOPMENT DEPARTMENT
Mahatma Gandhi National Rural Employment Guarantee Scheme

National Rural Employment Guarantee Act (NREGA) was notified by the Government of
India (GOD in September 2005 with the objective of enhancing livelihood security in
rural areas by providing at least 100 days of guaranteed wage employment in a financial
year to every household whose adult members volunteer to do unskilled manual labour.
The Act was extended to the State of Jammu and Kashmir with effect from May 2007.
The scheme was launched in the State in phases and gradually extended to all districts of
the State from April 2008. Out of the total number of 27.69 lakh Households in the State,
number of Households provided employment ranged between 1.44 lakh (five per cent)
and 5.67 lakh (20 per cent) during 2007-12. A performance review of the scheme by
Audit brought to fore several areas of concern such as non-observance of programme
guidelines in terms of nature of works to be taken up, unauthorized retention of funds,
delayed release and diversion of funds, execution of unapproved works, abandonment of
works, preparation of faulty estimates, delays in payment of wages to the workers,
shortfall in engagement of staff and their inadequate training, inadequate inspection of
works and social audit for proper monitoring of scope, extent and quality of works
executed and retention of undisbursed balances in the State Employment Guarantee Fund.

POWER DEVELOPMENT DEPARTMENT
Power Transmission Utilities

The National Electricity Policy recognized the need for continued development of
National Grid for providing adequate infrastructure for inter-state transmission of power.
Both the Central Transmission Utility (CTU) and the State Transmission Utility (STU)
have the key responsibility of network planning and development based on the National
Electricity Plan in coordination with all the concerned agencies. The transmission of
electricity and Grid operations in the State are managed and controlled by the Power



Audit Report on Social, General, Revenue and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs) for the year ended 31 March 2012

Development Department. The Performance Audit of Transmission utilities in the State
revealed under-utilisation of plan funds and non-achievement of targets in capacity
additions in respect of grid sub-stations and transmission lines. Almost all the Grid sub-
stations and Transmission Line projects witnessed a time over-run due to inadequate
planning, faulty designs etc. Some of the completed projects could not be operationalised
due to incompletion of related works, with the result that the infrastructure created was
lying idle. The transmission losses in the State exceeded the Central Electricity Authority
(CEA) norm of four per cent during the period 2008-12. Due to in-efficient Grid
management, the State Government had to pay the unscheduled Interchange charges. The
disaster management setup did not exist in the Department for immediate restoration of
transmission system in the event of major failures.

B. THEMATIC AUDIT
LABOUR AND EMPLOYMENT DEPARTMENT

Functioning of J&K Building and Other Construction Workers Welfare Board

Government of India (GOT) enacted (August 1996)> the Building and Other Construction
Workers (Regulation of Employment and Conditions of Service) Act, 1996 (BOCW Act)
for constitution of a Welfare Board in each State to undertake social security schemes’.
The GOI also enacted (August 1996)’ the Building and Other Construction Workers
Welfare Cess Act, 1996 (Cess Act). Section 14 of the Cess Act stipulates that rule
making power under the Act vests only with the GOI. The GOI framed (March 1998) the
Building and other Construction Workers’ Welfare Cess Rules, 1998 (Cess Rules). To
augment the resources of the Board, Section 3 of the Cess Act as amended in
September 1996 provided for levy and collection of labour welfare cess at a uniform rate
of one per cent of the cost of construction incurred by the employer. For the safety,
health and welfare of the workers, the State Government in pursuance of sections 40 and
62 of the BOCW Act also framed (July 2006) the Jammu and Kashmir Building and
Other Construction Workers (Regulation of Employment and Conditions of Service)
Rules, 2006 (J&KBOCW Rules) as amended in November 2012. The State Government
constituted (July 2007) the Jammu and Kashmir Building and Other Construction
Workers Welfare Board (Board) to carry out welfare schemes for construction workers.
Audit scrutiny revealed delay in constitution of Board resulting in non-enforcement of the
pro’ ms of the Act for the social security and other welfare measures of workers
besides loss of cess to the Board. Against the available funds of हैं 104.52 crore, the Board
utilized हैं 3.90 crore (four per cent) during 2010-12, leaving an unspent balance of
& 100.62 crore as at the end of 31 March 2012. Besides, cases of delay in submission of
cheques valuing of ~ 95.60 lakh on account of cess money, expenditure on administrative
expenses in violation of the provisions of the Act and non preparation of annual reports
for the financial years 2010-11 and 2011-12 were also noticed in audit.

Effective from 1 March 1996.
Provide loans and advances to beneficiaries for construction of houses, financial assistance for
education of children, medical assistance to beneficiaries, disability pension, financial assistance
for marriages, personal accident insurance scheme, etc

D. CHIEF CONTROLLING OFFICER BASED AUDIT OF A GOVERNMENT DEPARTMENT
INDUSTRIES AND COMMERCE DEPARTMENT

The Industries and Commerce Department is responsible for promotion of growth of
industries, creation of new employment opportunities and development of new designs in
the Handloom and Handicraft sectors in the State, besides optimum and sustainable
usage of state mineral resources. A Chief Controlling Officer-based audit of the
Department brought to fore several areas of concern: (a) Departmental failure of non-
existent oversight resulting in low industrial output, low employment generation, low-
end and low value-addition manufacturing despite fiscal burden of about % 8340 crore on

the State and Central exchequer during 2007-12, thus, defeating the objectives of the
Central/ State Governments’ industrial promotion policies (b) under-utilisation of Central
assistance (c) unplanned execution of industrial estates/ growth centres, leading to
unproductive investments (d) inadmissible and irregular payment of subsidies, VAT
reimbursement and irregular exemption of toll tax (e) under-utilisation of training
centres. Audit also noticed that performance of implementation of schemes in
Handicrafts and Handloom sector was poor and the purpose of imparting training to
unemployed persons for generating employment through formation of co-operative
societies was not achieved. The Department had no mechanism in place to check the
quantities of minerals extracted and lifted by the lessees from the mineral sites.

1.9 Lack of response to Audit

The Hand Book of Instructions for speedy settlement of Audit observations/ Inspection

Reports (IRs), etc., issued by the Government (Finance Department) provides for prompt
response by the executive to the IRs issued by the Principal Accountant General (Audit)

to ensure remedial/ rectification action in compliance with the prescribed rules and
procedures and accountability for the deficiencies, lapses, etc. brought out in the IRs. The
Heads of offices and next higher authorities are required to comply with the observations
contained in the IRs and rectify the defects promptly and report their compliance to the
Principal Accountant General (Audit).

Eleven (11) Audit Committee meetings were held during the year 2011-12 in respect of
paragraphs contained in IRs pertaining to seven departments wherein 322 transaction
audit paragraphs were discussed, out of which 95 audit paragraphs were settled.

1.10 Response of departments to Audit Report material

The draft audit paragraphs proposed for inclusion in the Report of the Comptroller and
Auditor General of India are forwarded by the Principal Accountant General (Audit) to
the Principal Secretaries/ Secretaries of the Department concerned, drawing their
attention to audit findings and requesting them to send their response within six weeks.
The fact of non-receipt of replies from the departments/ Government is invariably
indicated at the end of such paragraphs included in the Audit Report.

Seventeen (17) draft paragraphs including three performance reviews, CCO based audit
and a thematic audit paragraph, proposed to be included in the Report of the Comptroller
and Auditor General of India for the year ended 31 March 2012 were sent to the Principal
Secretaries/ Secretaries of the respective departments by name between March and
December 2012. The Principal Secretaries/ Secretaries of the departments did not send
replies in respect of 15 paragraphs, two performance reviews and one Thematic audit
paragraph. These paragraphs have been included in Chapter-2 of the Report without the
response of the Principal Secretaries/ Secretaries of the department concerned.



Audit Report on Social, General, Revenue and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs) for the year ended 31 March 2012

1.11 Follow-up on Audit Reports
1.11.1 Non-submission of suo-motu Action Taken Notes

To ensure accountability of the executives to the issues dealt with in various Audit
Reports, the State Government (Finance Department) issued instructions in June 1997 to
the administrative departments to furnish to Public Accounts Committee (PAC)/
Committee on Public Undertakings (COPU), swo-motu Action Taken Notes (ATNs) on
all the audit paragraphs featuring in the Audit Reports irrespective of the fact that these
are taken up for discussion by these Committees or not. These ATNs are to be submitted
to these Committees duly vetted by the Principal Accountant General (Audit) within a
period of three months from the date of presentation of Audit Reports in the State
Legislature.

It was, however, noticed that out of 395 audit paragraphs featuring in the Civil Chapters
of Audit Reports from 2000-01 to 2010-11, suo-motu ATNs in respect of 203 audit
paragraphs had not been received upto 31 July 2012.

1.11.2 Action taken on recommendations of the PAC/ COPU

Action Taken Notes, duly vetted by the Principal Accountant General (Audit) on the
observations/ recommendations made by the PAC/ COPU in respect of the audit
paragraphs discussed by them are to be furnished to these Committees within six months
from the date of such observations/ recommendations. Out of 375 audit paragraphs
featuring in the Civil chapters of Audit Reports for the years from 2000-01 to 2009-10
(excluding Audit Reports presented in the Jammu and Kashmir State Legislature on
04 April 2012), only 133 audit paragraphs have been discussed by the PAC up to 31
March 2012. Recommendations in respect of 128 audit paragraphs have been made by
the PAC, however, ATNs on the recommendations of the Committees is pending from
the State Government in respect of 102 paragraphs.

A. Performance Audit
2.1 Sher-i-Kashmir Institute of Medical Sciences

Sher-i-Kashmir Institute of Medical Science (SKIMS) Srinagar was established
in December 1982 to provide specialised medical care, need based education in
medical and health services and clinical research and to develop referral linkage
between primary, secondary and tertiary health care in the State. The
Performance review of SKIMS revealed the following financial and operational
deficiencies:
Financial deficiencies: There were cases of under-utilisation of funds, irregular
withdrawals for crediting of funds to the Civil Deposit heads and unauthorised
retention and utilisation of hospital receipts.
Operational deficiencies: The delivery of patient care services was affected by
longer waiting periods for diagnostic tests/ investigations and surgeries besides
misutilisation of ambulances meant for patients. The medical research activities
had not been attended to and the capacity of medical courses was not fully
utilised. Audit also noticed that the procurement system for purchase of drugs
and equipment was deficient. The quality and efficacy of drugs is sought to be
ensured through higher priced procurements and the funds released for setting
up an Advanced Quality Assurance Laboratory were lying unspent in a Civil
Deposit. The hospital administration reported that there were shortages of
medical, paramedical and nursing staff and faculty though the manpower
requirements were not properly assessed/ benchmarked.
Highlights
« The Institute did not have any approved long term master/ perspective Plan
for phased and planned infrastructure development and for purchase of
medical equipment or civil infrastructure.

(Paragraph: 2.1.8)

e The project and financial management in the Institute was lax as manifested

in persistent under-utilisation of allotted budget ranging between eight to 28

per cent, irregular withdrawal of funds ranging from % 0.73 crore to = 14.42

crore for crediting to Civil Deposits to avoid the lapse of budget, irregular

utilization of upto 61 per cent of sundry hospital receipts for unapproved
purposes and irregular personal claims.

(Paragraphs: 2.1.9, 2.1.9.1 and 2.1.9.3)

e¢ The number of patients who attended the Out Patient Department (OPD) of

32 clinical departments including super-specialty centres has steadily risen by

28 per cent from 5,16,860 in 2007-08 to 6,60,482 in 2011-12 though there was

a significant dip in 2010-11. The patients had to wait for surgeries from one

month to 12 months and for diagnostic tests/ investigations for the period

ranging between 10 days to 524 days. The Institute has not carried out any

study of the patient profile to ascertain whether this rising trend indicated

any deficiency on the part of the primary health care service providers



Audit Report on Social, General, Revenue and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs) for the year ended 31 March 2012

leading to indiscriminate rush at the Institute’s OPD. The tertiary character
of the Institute was thus getting diluted.
(Paragraph: 2.1.10.1, 2.1.10.2 and 2.1.10.3)
The growth in the number of hospitalized patients was relatively stable
barring a peak in the year 2009-10 as it rose from 45,654 in 2007-08 to 50,075
in 2011-12. The number of patients awaiting surgeries as on 31 March 2012
in seven In-Patient Departments ranged between 20 in Surgical

ward) was four per cent of the total deaths.
(Paragraph: 2.1.10.4)
The Emergency Medicine Department of the Institute had not been fully

injuries including orthopaedic injuries. Consequently, such patients were
shifted to Bone and Joint Hospital Srinagar.

just to payment of monthly clinical research allowance of हैं 2500 to the

(Paragraph 2.1.11.2)
The procurement system for purchase of equipment and drugs needs to be

The system of drawing of samples of drugs for testing to ensure procurement

Control Laboratory of the Institute had stopped functioning since November
2003 and % 1.40 crore released during 2009-11 to set up an Advanced Quality
Assurance Laboratory were lying unspent in a Civil Deposit. Physical
verification of drugs and surgical instruments in the Institute had not been
conducted since June 2008.
(Paragraphs: 2.1.12.2)
No effort was made to open JAN AUSHADHI sale outlet in the Institute or
procure generic medicines under the Government of India campaign to make
available unbranded quality generic medicines at lower prices, though
equivalent in potency to branded expensive drugs.
(Paragraphs: 2.1.12.2)

21.1 Introduction.

Sher-i-Kashmir Institute of Medical Sciences, Srinagar (Institute) was established in
December 1982 as a premier institute designed to serve all the three tiers’ of the
health delivery system. The Institute was designed to have 500 general and 100
paying beds. In April 1984, the Institute adopted the Health Block at Hajan,
Bandipora for experimenting new modalities in maternity and child care while the
Medical College, Bemina (erstwhile Jehlum Valley College) was taken over by the
Institute in April 1998 for conducting undergraduate cour: The objectives
envisaged by the State Government in setting up the Institute were as under:-

. To provide facilities of specialised medical care, need oriented education in
medical, health sciences and clinical research;

Primary, Secondary and Tertiary



Audit Report on Social, General, Revenue and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs) for the year ended 31 March 2012

. To develop a referral linkage between the primary, secondary and tertiary
health care institutions of the State in order to achieve a regionalization of
health care;

. To provide for instruction and research in such branches of sciences of modern
medicine and other allied sciences including physical and biological sciences;

. To hold examinations and grant degrees, diplomas and other academic
distinctions.
2.1.2 Organizational set-up

The organizational chart is shown in Chart-I below:-

2.1.3 Governing structure of the Institute

The functioning of the Institute is overseen by a high level Governing Body (GB)
ired by the Hon’ble Chief Minister of the State. The GB has some Cabinet
members, with the Director of the Institute as Member Secretary. The
Chief Secretary and other Administrative Secretaries act as advisors. The GB is
assisted by Apical Selection Committee (ASC) chaired by the Chief Secretary to
handle appointment/ promotion of faculty and Standing Finance Committee (SFC)
chaired by the Financial Commissioner to look after financial matters. Besides, there
are Senior Selection Committee (SSC) and Junior Selection Committee (JSC) chaired
by the Director of the Institute to look after appointment/ promotion of Gazetted and
Non-Gazetted staff, respectively. Other committees include Standing Academic
Committee, Purchase Committee, Works Contract Committee, Technical Committee
for purchase of drugs, etc. The Director is designated as ex-officio Secretary to
Government of the State for matters related to the Institute. The decisions taken by the
Governing Body are deemed to have approval of the Cabinet, conveyed by issuing
Government orders by the Director in his capacity of ex-officio Secretary to the
Government. The Institute was granted statutory status to function as a deemed
university to award degrees in medical and allied specialties from August 1983 under
the provisions of the Sher-i-Kashmir Institute of Medical Sciences (Grant of Degrees)

16


Chapter-2-Audit Findings an Social, General and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs)

Act, 1983. The Institute’s medical education/ research activities are also covered by
the regulatory mechanism of the Indian Council of Medical Research. Thus, the
Institute has attributes of an autonomous statutory body, a deemed university and an
entity having the powers of a Government Department without the usual system of
checks and balances applicable to a Government Department. The unique governing
structure, while designed to facilitate its functioning, casts special responsibilities on
the administration of the Institute.

2.1.4 Audit Objectives
The performance audit was conducted to ascertain whether:

न the planning for infrastructure development and purchase of equipment was
efficient;

. allotment of funds was adequate and fund management in the Institute was
effective and efficient;

. patient care including diagnosis and treatment in the Institute was adequate
and effective;

. procurement of machinery and equipment was done economically and
effectively;

. there was adequate faculty and health personnel;

. adequate and effective monitoring and internal control system was in existence
in the Institute.

2.1.5. Audit Criteria

Audit findings were benchmarked against the following criteria:

© Budget documents, Annual Action Plans/ Project Reports/ Scheme guidelines
etc. as applicable to the Institute having a unique governance structure;

© Financial and Civil Service rules of the State Government and orders issued
thereunder, as applicable to the Institute having a unique governance structure;

. State Public Works Account Code and Schedule of Rates, as applicable to the
Institute having a unique governance structure.
2.1.6 Audit Methodology

An entry conference was held on 8 March 2012 to discuss the objectives, scope and
criteria of audit with the Director of the Institute. Thereafter, an examination of
records including inspection of the premises and interaction with various Heads of
Departments was undertaken by a team of officers from the office of the Principal
Accountant General (Audit), Jammu & Kashmir. The exit conference was held on 21
November 2012 with the Director, SKIMS in which audit observations were
discussed. Replies thereof have been incorporated in the review at appropriate places.

2.1.7 Scope of Audit

The performance review of working of the Institute during the period from 2007-08 to
2011-12 was conducted by test-check of records of the Director, Joint Director, Dean,
Chief of Material Management, Medical Superintendent, Financial Advisor/ Chief
Accounts Officer, Chief Engineer, Executive Engineers Civil/ Electrical/ Mechanical/

17


Audit Report on Social, General, Revenue and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs) for the year ended 31 March 2012

Information Technology, Deputy Director Planning and Heads of Departments
(HODs) of nineteen’ medical departments of the Institute between December 2011
and August 2012. The selection of the Departments was made on judgemental basis.

Audit findings

2.1.8 Planning

Strategic planning is necessary for attaining the long term goals of an entity in a time-
bound frame work. It forms the core of an implementation process. A properly
planned approach for infrastructure development and purchase of medical equipment
taking into account the needs and preferences of professionals and end users for
retaining consistency and control is needed for ensuring value for money. This may be
achieved by preparing a long term perspective plan for implementation in a phased
manner.

Audit noticed that the Institute did not have any approved long term master/
perspective Plan for phased and planned infrastructure development and purchase of
medical equipment or civil infrastructure. Thus, build-up of infrastructure (Civil
Works and Equipment) and matching manpower profile was based on requirements
projected from time to time. Even the draft Annual Plans prepared by the Institute
were not adhered to and % 24.09 crore was incurred on unplanned works during
2007-12 as brought out in Paragraph 2.1.9 below.

The Institute had prepared 11th Five Year Plan period (2007-12) proposals amounting
to = 214.05 crore which was subsequently reduced to हैं 146.14 crore on the basis of
fresh ceiling conveyed by the Planning & Development Department, Government of
Jammu and Kashmir. The supporting statements in respect of plan proposals under
infrastructure development and equipment were not available with the Planning Wing
of the Institute. The annual plans prepared could not thus be correlated with the 11th
Five Year Plan projections.

toto as 39 works falling outside the purview of the Plan were taken up for execution
and expenditure of % 24.09 crore was incurred during 2007-12 on such unplanned
works.

2.1.9.1 Withdrawal of funds in anticipation of requirements

Rule 2.33 of the State Financial Code envisage that funds should be withdrawn from
treasury only if required for immediate disbursement. The practice of withdrawing
funds with a view to avoid lapsing of budget grant is forbidden. The Finance
Department had prohibited (August 1999) the Drawing and Disbursing Officers
(DDOs) from parking funds at the close of the financial year in the Major Head 8443-
Civil deposits to avoid lapsing of funds.

Audit noticed that हैं 41.61 crore were withdrawn by the Institute for purchase of
Machinery and Equipment from the Consolidated Fund of the State in contravention
of the rules and credited to the Civil Deposit head during the period 2007-12 to avoid
lapse of budget. Full utilization of these funds had not been ensured during the
subsequent years and the unspent balances in the Deposit head accumulated to हैं 28.19
crore as of March 2012 as indicated in Table 2.1.2 below:

Table-2.1.2

The Chief Accounts Officer (CAO) of the Institute stated (July 2012) that non-
utilisation of funds was because of delays in the process of procurement of equipment
and construction works, and the funds were kept in Civil Deposits for subsequent
utilisation. The withdrawal of funds in anticipation of requirements and their
subsequent parking in civil deposits was irregular. The Institute did not seek any
relaxation from the Governing Body, which has been vested with the powers of the
Cabinet, either before or after resorting to irregular financial transactions. The
Director while admitting the fact stated (November 2012) that the parking of funds in
civil deposits had been minimized during the year 2011-12 and the efforts would
continue during 2012-13 as well.

Audit further noticed that हैं 5.22 crore were advanced by the Institute during the years

Joinery Mills (₹ 47.20 lakh) for procurement of key construction material. Against
this, material worth = 2.29 crore had been lifted by the Institute from these agencies
during 2007-08 to 2011-12 resulting in blockade of Government money to the extent
of & 2.93 crore with these procurement agencies as of March 2012.

The Executive Engineer (Civil), of the Institute stated (July 2012) that the material
was lifted on need basis and the balance material would be lifted after reconciliation
with the supplying agencies. The reply is indicative of the fact that the funds had been
advanced without ascertaining the actual requirement of the construction material of
the Institute.

be disbursed during the years 2007-08 to 2011-12 pending submission of attendance
certificates, adjustment account of T.A advance etc. of the employees of the Institute
as indicated in Table 2.1.3 below:

Scrutiny of records revealed that the Institute had irregularly utilized
हैं 16.17 crore representing 61 per cent of the total revenue realized during the years

Institute. The reply is not acceptable as the method adopted was contrary to the
financial and Budget procedures. The Director of the Institute informed (November

payments, the authorities draw and advance amount to these agencies, which is
recouped at a later stage. The Director further stated that non-recoupment of हैं 16.17

the Government Account. The Accounts Officer (Budget) stated (July 2012) that the
matter would be looked into.

The details of revenue earned/ retained and unspent funds in CD accounts was not
intimated to the Finance Department resulting in excess budgeting as evidenced from
persistent inability to utilize the approved budget.

2.1.9.4 Rush of expenditure

Financial Rules provide that expenditure should be evenly distributed over the year.
Audit noticed rush of expenditure in the month of March of each year which ranged
between 49 and 76 per cent during the period from 2007-08 to 2011-12. The rush of
expenditure in the last month of financial year indicated lack of financial discipline.

2.1.9.5 Non-maintenance of Cash Book and incomplete reconciliation of
accounts

The Cash Book had not been maintained from July 2011 onwards and, therefore,

Audit could not verify whether the cash accounts were fully reconciled with the Bank

and Treasury Accounts.

Operational deficiencies

2.1.10 Delivery of patient care services

The Institute provides consultancy for various diseases and medical care facilities to

the patients either through OPD-clinics or by admitting them in wards. There are 32
clinical departments including super-specialty centres for managing different types of

health promotion activities, improve the capacity of healthcare, involve all
professionals and the patient in improving the quality of care and to improve the

hospital manual. This manual had, however, not been formulated (August 2012). The
following significant points with regard to patient care were noticed in audit

2.1.10.1 Out-patient services

The norms for deployment of specialists to ensure satisfactory patient care and to
bring down the waiting time of patients in the OPD had not been fixed by the

and the huge rush of patients attending the Institute is basically due to non-availability
of sufficient health care facilities in the peripheral institutions of the valley. In case
proper referral system is in vogue in Kashmir, the referred patients only would attend
the Institute, which can ultimately improve the patient care at the Institute to a greater
extent.

tertiary care institution, the Institute received many patients with advanced stage of
complications and even with best efforts, small percentage of deaths was
unfortunately unavoidable. Although no consolation by itself, the Director compared
the mortality rate in SKIMS with Post Graduate Institute (PGI) Chandigarh (Gross
death rate at PGI Chandigarh is 7.3 per cent compared to SKIMS) and stated that
mortality was slightly higher than All India Institute of Medical Sciences (AIIMS)
(net death rate of AIIMS three per cent compared to SKIMS)

2.1.10.5 Non-establishment of Trauma Centres

The Emergency Medicine Department of the Institute had not been fully equipped to
deal with cases of road traffic accidents having multiple organ injuries including
orthopaedic injuries and such patients were shifted to Bone and Joint Hospital,
Srinagar. The proposal for creation of Trauma Centres at the Institute and SKIMS
Medical College, Bemina approved (July 2011) by the Governing Body was sent
(September 2011) to the State Government for taking up the matter with the Ministry
of Health and Family Welfare, Government of India for assistance. Further progress
in the matter was awaited (August 2012). The Director of the Institute stated
(December 2012) that the SKIMS was armed with all the specialties necessary for
managing all trauma patients and that no full fledged dedicated building was available
within the Institute as was available at AIIMS or PGI Chandigarh.

2.1.10.6 Ambulance services

The availability of adequate ambulance services and emergency medical technicians,
for salvaging the patients during medical emergencies is very essential for patient
care. The timely availability of ambulance with adequate basic facilities is very vital
in case of critical patients. The Institute had a fleet of 18 ambulances (March 2012)
which were not equipped with the essential medical equipment viz. oxygen cylinders,
suction pumps, blood pressure instruments, stethoscope, first aid bags for providing

the intake capacity for various courses and the M.Sc Nursing course approved by the

deficient infrastructure/ manpower in the Institute.

2.1.11.2 Medical research activities

The Institute was conceived as a Centre of Excellence in the area of medical
education while serving as a hospital for specialised medical services. Audit observed
that no roadmap to achieve excellence in medical research had been prepared by the
Institute and that no agenda in this regard was presented in any of the Governing
Body meetings for attaining this vital objective of the Institute. The research activity
had been restricted just to payment of monthly clinical research allowance of % 2,500
to the faculty members (without verifying the eligibility conditions for the allowance)
and meager allocation of research grant. The Institute had not laid emphasis on
medical research, as against the total budget allotment of = 813.31 crore during the
years 2007-08 to 2011-12, an amount of % 0.39 crore (0.04 per cent) only had been
allocated for medical research under research grant. Out of this allotment, हैं 0.31 crore
had been advanced to 80 faculty members out of 181 faculty members for undertaking
research activities during the period 2007-12. Audit scrutiny of records showed that
74 faculty members had not submitted adjustment accounts of these advances. The
review for ascertaining progress of research projects had not been conducted by the
Director and Dean (Academics) of the Institute during the years 2007-08 to 2011-12
and the results of the research activities carried out, if any, by the faculty members
were not intimated to audit. The Director stated (December 2012) that the policy

Board of Professional Entrance Examination

Audit Report on Social, General, Revenue and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs) for the year ended 31 March 2012

guidelines enabling the Principal investigator to procure equipment, kits and reagents
for research projects had been approved (January 2012) and by virtue of this new
policy it would be easy for scientists to utilize the funds. Audit further noticed that a
double storey animal house building costing ~ 8.99 crore constructed for research
purposes continued to remain under the occupation of security forces since May 1998.
The Sr. Administrative Officer (Policy) of the Institute stated (May 2012) that the
matter in this regard had been taken up with the concerned authorities. The Director
of the Institute stated (December 2012) that instructions had been issued by the
Chairman, Governing Body of the Institute to the Home Department for vacation of
the building.

The Indian Council of Medical Research, Department of Biotechnology, Department
of Science and Technology etc. have been providing grants for conducting research in
different areas under extra mural funding programme. At the end of March 2012, an
amount of हैं 76.84 lakh was unspent balance out of रैं 1.64 crore provided during
2008-12 which includes an opening balance of % 70.24 lakh of such grant lying with
the Institute. The Institute did not have a system of watching the recovery of
institutional overheads from various funding agencies for use of Institute’s manpower
and other resources for sponsored research. The information relating to completion of
research projects, submission of final reports and number of research findings got
patented/ commercialized was not furnished to audit. Further, no periodic review of
the research projects regarding progress made, expenditure incurred, time spent and
likely time and expenditure required to achieve the desired results was conducted by
the Director and Dean. Thus, the objective to make the Institute a Centre of
Excellence could not be achieved. The Director of the Institute agreed (November
2012) that meager allotment was made for medical research and that sufficient
allotment of funds for carrying out research could not be obtained despite pleading the
case with the Government. The Director further stated (December 2012) that for
monitoring of the research projects, the Department of Clinical Research had been
created from this year by the Institute.

The Institute was recognized as Regional Cancer Centre (RCC) by the Ministry of
Health and Family Welfare (GOI) in November 2005. For this purpose grant-in-aid of
% 6,13 crore was released in December 2005 (₹ 4.99 crore) and April 2007 (® 1.14
crore) to the Institute. The Centre had not been established (November 2012).

2.1.12 Defective procurement system

The medical equipment is of paramount importance in carrying out diagnosis and
treatment of patients in a hospital. Non-availability of medical equipment can result in
denial of timely treatment to patients and at times prove fatal. The Governing Body
noticed (May 2009) deficiency in procurement process of medical equipment and
decided to engage a reputed consultant for suggesting measures for its improvement.
No steps were, however, taken in this regard.

Audit scrutiny of records revealed that despite availability of funds there was
abnormal delay in invitation of tenders and placing of supply orders for purchase of
medical equipment. Due to delay in the process of procurement of medical equipment,
most of the funds allotted for the purpose had been kept in the civil deposit head at the
close of each financial year during 2007-12 as indicated in Table 2.1.12 below:

2.1121 Non-procurement/ installation of medical equipment

To overcome difficulties faced by the Department of Radiation Oncology due to lack
of proper and sophisticated medical equipment for treatment of patients suffering
from various malignant diseases, the Head of the Department proposed (April 2008)
procurement of Linear Accelerator which was approved (October 2008) by the
Governing Body of the Institute. For this purpose, = 10 crore was released (November
2008) by the State Planning and Development Department and the global tenders
were invited (January 2009) by the Sr. Material Management Officer (Imports) in
response to which two firms’ quoted rates of % 6.18 crore and हैं 9.89 crore
respectively. Due to inadequate response to the tender and infirmities in the techno-
financial bids submitted by the firms, fresh tenders were invited (March 2010) for
purchase of the equipment. The three firms including previous two firms quoted rates
for supply of the equipment. However, the firm which had previously quoted lowest
rate for the equipment was not selected on technical grounds. The order for supply of
equipment was placed (March 2012) with the firm which had quoted lower rate of
% 12.32 crore. The letter of credit for procurement of the equipment could not be
opened (June 2012) as balance of हैं 11.07 crore only was available under Deposit
head of the Institute. The matter regarding requirement of additional amount of & 1.25
crore for procurement of the equipment had been taken up with the Government and
further progress in the matter was awaited (August 2012). Thus, delay in finalisation
of procurement process had caused extra burden on the State exchequer due to
increase in cost of equipment and consequent denial of intended benefits to the
patients. After this being pointed out in audit, the Material Management Officer stated
(August 2012) that fresh tenders were invited due to inadequate response to tenders
and infirmities in techno-financial bids of the firms. The Director of the Institute
stated (December 2012) that due to inadequate response and technical reasons the
tenders could not be finalized despite engagement of two eminent experts and as a
result the equipment could not be procured. The reply should be viewed in light of the
fact that even after invitation of fresh tenders in March 2010 the receipt of equipment
by the Institute was nowhere in sight (September 2012). The Head of Department,
Radiation Oncology had expressed that due to shortage of the equipment the patients
had to wait for two to three weeks to receive the treatment.

The order for import, supply, installation, testing and commissioning of C-Arm
system’? for Radio Diagnosis and Gastroenterology Departments was placed
(February 2011) by the Chief of Material Management of the Institute with an Italy
based firm at a cost of = 1.12 crore. The equipment received (October 2011) at a cost
of & 1.53 crore in the stores of the Institute were found wet/ water stained during its
inspection by the engineer of the firm in November 2011. Further, the image
intensifier and mono block parts of the equipment were found totally damaged and
due to these reasons the engineer of the firm refused to install the equipment. The
Material Management Officer (Imports) requested (November 2011) the firm to
replace the equipment with new ones. However, no further progress for replacement

2.1.12.2 Purchase of drugs

The requirement of drugs are assessed annually on the basis of the requisitions of
various medical departments. The quantities of different kinds of medicines to be
procured are determined after taking into account the available balance of
unconsumed drugs in central stores of the Institute. A health care system can ill-afford
to purchase drugs mentioned under different proprietary brands at widely varying
prices. A limited list of essential drugs also referred to as drug formulary defines
which drugs will be regularly purchased. At the core of the concept is the objective
that procurement should be limited to drugs that are economically priced but safe and
effective.

It was noted that a formal system of pre and post-qualification of prospective
suppliers to ensure procurement from suppliers of quality products was not in place.

The SKIMS Purchase Committee in its meeting held on 16% July 2007 had desired
that instead of open tender system for drugs, the Institute should come up with a
proposal for purchase on the pattern of ATIMS, Delhi. The Finance Department
conveyed (February 2008) approval of the Chairman Governing Body to the proposal.
There was delay in implementing the new purchase policy and the earlier contract was
extended upto December 2008. Under the new system, the manufacturing companies
were to be short listed in consultation with Heads of Departments and rates invited
from them for a two year rate contract effective from February 2009, later extended
upto March 2012. The rates achieved under the new purchase policy were generally
lower both in respect of Sale Counter Drugs and Hospital drugs. However, the
purchase of 29 drugs for Sale Counter Drugs from April 2008 to December 2008 on
old rate contract resulted in excess expenditure of = 10.91 lakh.

The pharmaceutical procurement procedure should normally include, wherever
practicable, identification of generic over proprietary drugs. The Government of India
in association with the Central Pharma Public Sector Undertakings has launched a
campaign for opening Jan Aushadhi stores where unbranded quality generic
medicines would be sold at lower prices, though equivalent in potency to branded
expensive drugs. Thus, with the same cost, ten times more persons could be treated
with same efficacy and cure. No effort was, however, made to open Jan Aushadhi sale
outlet in the Institute or procure generic medicines under the programme. The
Director of the Institute stated (December 2012) that the Institute has its own Drug
Sales Counter which sells medicines at cheaper and economical rates and that the
opening of Jan Aushadhi outlet in SKIMS was subject to authorization of the
Governing Body

The system of drawing of samples of drugs for testing to ensure procurement of
quality medicines and drugs is not in vogue in the Institute. For exercising quality
control, both the options of having an in-house quality control set up and buying from

31


Audit Report on Social, General, Revenue and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs) for the year ended 31 March 2012

reputed sources at higher prices entail costs. A cost benefit analysis is needed to arrive
at a proper balance. It was noticed that the Drug Quality Control Laboratory
established in Drug and Pharmacy Department of the Institute had ceased to function
since November 2003. The Institute proposed establishment of Advanced Quality
Assurance Laboratory for drug pharmacy at a cost of % two crore against which
& one crore was released during 2009-10 and हैं 40 lakh during 2010-11. No work was,
however, done on the project and instead money was lying unspent in a Civil Deposit
account. The physical verification of drugs and surgical instruments in the Institute
had not been conducted since June 2008. This in turn may directly affect the quality
and efficiency of drugs in the Institute.

2.1.13 Improper planning in infrastructure development

To overcome patient load and shortage of space, it was incumbent to augment the
existing facilities of the Institute. On the basis of projections made in the 117 Five
Year Plan period (2007-12), the Institute proposed to execute and complete the
ongoing 10 major civil works for providing better facilities at the Institute and in
SKIMS Medical College, Bemina, Srinagar. Test-check of records revealed that one
work viz. married doctors quarters out of four works'! taken up for execution during
the year 2006-07 had been completed at a cost of € 5.20 crore during the year 2011-12
after a delay of about three years. The remaining three works taken up for execution
during the years 2001-02 to 2008-09 at an estimated cost of ¥ 28.70 crore and targeted
to be completed during 2002-03 to 2010-11 had not been completed despite incurring
an expenditure of & 15.90 crore up to March 2012. The urgency of the requirement of
the quarters could be gauged from the fact that the married doctors quarters had been
instantly occupied on their completion. It is pertinent to note in this context that house
rent allowance to the extent of = 1.43 crore was paid by the Institute during the period
of delay.

The delay in execution of works was attributed (July 2012) by the Executive Engineer
(Civil) to delays in processing cases, issuance of allotment orders by the Chief
Hospital Engineer and execution of works by the contractors. The reply is not
convincing as the executing agencies failed to complete the works despite availability
of funds. The Director of the Institute stated (December 2012) that the infrastructural
development programmes had been taken up on a war footing but due to procedural
delays these works got prolonged.

2.1.13.2 Execution of works without technical sanction

Rule 9.3 of the Jammu and Kashmir Financial code stipulates that no work should be
commenced unless administrative approval and technical sanction of the competent
authority is accorded. Audit noticed that ten civil works had been taken up for
execution during 2007-08 to 2011-12 without accord of technical sanction. The
Executive Engineer (Civil) informed that these schemes could not be technically
sanctioned as the powers were not vested with the Chief Hospital Engineer. The reply
is not convincing in view of the fact that the contract committee in its meeting
(February 2010) had directed that the cases should be referred to the concerned
territorial Chief Engineer for accord of Technical Sanction.

2.1.13.3 Advanced centre for human genetics

To check susceptible risks of carrying genetically inherited diseases due to
consanguinity, the Government in the Science and Technology Department, approved
(November 2006) up-gradation of Human Genetics Research cum Counseling Centre
in the Institute at a cost of = 5.28 crore for completion in three years. The project
included provision for construction of building (€ 2.18 crore), machinery and
equipment (हैं 2.28 crore) and allied components (% 82 lakh). The administrative
approval for construction of a double storeyed building was accorded (December
2006) by the State Planning and Development Department at a cost of हैं 2.50 crore.
The construction of the building though envisaged to be completed in one year was
allotted in parts to three different contractors between November 2006 and January

33


Audit Report on Social, General, Revenue and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs) for the year ended 31 March 2012

2009. The work on construction of the building was taken up in February 2007 and
expenditure of % 2.09 crore was incurred on part construction of the building. An
amount of & 1.01 crore was kept in Civil Deposit head and the balance = 2.18 crore,
out of the released amount of % 5.28 crore got lapsed (March 2009). Thereafter it was
decided to raise the building upto third storey. The project was, however, not
completed within the targeted time of three years by November 2009. The Governing
Body of the Institute decided (May 2009) to adopt the centre renamed as ‘Advanced
Centre for Human Genetics’ and to complete the balance works of the building under
State Plan funds. The construction of the building had not been completed though an
expenditure of हैं 5.28 crore’? had been incurred on it (August 2012). Further,
equipment and furniture valuing = 1.97 crore’* procured for the centre between

Thus, improper planning in execution resulted in non-establishment of ‘Advance
Centre for Human Genetics’ and unfruitful investment of हैं 7.25 crore besides non-
achievement of intended benefits of the project. After this was pointed out by Audit,
the Coordinator for the Project stated (July 2012) that he was not in a position to
comment on utilisation of funds/ building as the project was monitored by ex-Head of
the Department. The Director of the Institute stated (December 2012) that the
Advanced Centre for Human Genetics had been completed in all respects except some
minor works in mechanical side and that the most of the equipment for the project had
been procured.

2.1.13.4 Non-establishment of local area networking system

The Project ‘Computerization and campus wide local area networking’ (LAN)
approved (December 2004) at a cost of हैं 1.75 crore was completed at an expenditure
of € 4.87 crore in May 2010 after the cost of project was revised to = 4.62 crore. The
Project among other things was intended to streamline the functioning, better work
output, easy administrative monitoring, better patient care and accountability, analysis
of research data etc. of the Institute by way of providing equipment/ material for
cabling internet facilities, installation of 466 computers, software etc. Audit noticed
that against the requirement of 466 computer systems for the project, only 368
computers were purchased between December 2006 and March 2012 out of which
only 198 computers were installed and the remaining 170 computers were lying idle
since October 2011 in the central stores of the Institute. Audit also noticed that = 0.37
crore had been diverted towards the expenditure on items like procurement of
cartridges, stationery and repairs/ renovation of residential quarters which was outside
the purview of the project. Thus due to improper planning, in execution of the project
envisaged objectives of the project could not be achieved. The Executive Engineer
(IT) stated (June 2012) that poor implementation of the scheme was due to resistance
shown by the user departments. The reply is not convincing as no such matter was
brought to the notice of Governing Body. The Director of the Institute stated
(December 2012) that computers planned for different sections were based on
requirement at various locations and that 170 computers were issued to various offices
after proper scrutiny.

2.1.13.5 Inadequate fire safety measures

Scrutiny of records revealed that no fire safety measures existed in the Institute. For
ensuring fire safety, the Institute had an automatic Fire Alarm System consisting of 45
Zone Alarm panels which had gone obsolete. However, no steps had been taken either
to repair or get it replaced with the latest technology to safeguard the lives of patients
and staff. The Security Officer of the Institute stated (February 2012) that steps were
being taken to replace the system with latest technology. The Director of the Institute
stated (December 2012) that a meeting in this regard was held with the Fire Services
Department.

2.1.14 Inadequate Manpower Management

The efficiency of medical care depends upon availability of adequate quality medical
and paramedical personnel and other support staff. Audit noticed that the cadre
strength of various departments and services in the Institute was revised in May 1998.
However, there was no staff statistics and records maintained to indicate the criteria
adopted for assessing staff requirement of the Institute. As a result, the benchmark
used to determine the staff requirement could not be ascertained by Audit. The Sr.
Administrative Officer (Policy) stated (August 2012) that the creation of staff had
been made on the basis of recommendations of the concerned Heads of the
Departments of the Institute depending upon increase in the work load etc.

2.1.14.1 Medical and para medical staff
The position of sanctioned and effective strength of medical/ paramedical, nursing

and administrative staff in the Institute and SKIMS Medical College as of March
2012 is indicated in Table 2.1.15 below:

The vacancy of medical/ para-medical/ nursing and administrative staff was 32 per
cent and 34 per cent in the Institute and 37 per cent and 54 per cent in SKIMS

Care Department of the Institute having bed strength of 12, which was raised to 60 in
January 2012 due to increased incidents of critical care patients. As a result, this
Department remained non-functional (August 2012). Also, 15 posts of technical staff
sanctioned for the Burn Unit and Burn Ward of the Plastic Surgery Department in
March 2008 had not been filled up (August 2012) indicating inadequate manpower for
the Burn Unit. The effective strength of medical/ paramedical/ nursing staff and
administrative staff in the Institute was in the ratio of 53:47. The Governing Body
realized (October 2011) that the staff structure in the Institute was lopsided and there
was dearth of staff in areas of nursing and technical professionals whereas there was
excess staff in areas of supportive services. The Governing Body further felt that need
based reorganization of all cadres in the Institute could be achieved in a scientific
manner after taking into consideration bed strength of the hospital, volume of OPD
and IPD activities on campus and type of research conducted by various departments
during the last three decades. The Governing Body accordingly decided to create a

Human Resource Development Cell by hiring an agency for the purpose. However, no
agency had been hired by the Institute (August 2012). The Director (SKIMS) during
exit conference agreed (November 2012) that there was excess administrative/
supporting staff in comparison to medical/ para-medical staff in the Institute.

It is thus seen that the sanctioned strength of the Institute has been incrementally
augmented from time to time without any holistic norm-based appraisal and no
comparative study has been carried out to benchmark the manpower requirements in
various categories, except the norms referred to in Paragraph 2.1.14.2 below for
nursing staff. Hence, it is difficult to appreciate the shortages depicted in the above
table in proper perspective.

2.1.14,.2 Inadequate nursing staff

According to the prescribed norms of Indian Nursing Council, 723 staff nurses and 75
nursing supervisors were required in the hospital on the basis of strength of ICU high
dependency and general beds. However, the actual strength of staff nurses and nursing
supervisors was 385 and 45, respectively as of March 2012 revealing shortage of 338
staff nurses (47 per cent) and 30 nursing supervisors (40 per cent). The shortage of
nurses affected the patient care and also created extra burden on existing staff. After
this was pointed out by Audit, the Administrative Officer (Hospital Administration)
stated (July 2012) that 197 posts of staff nurses had been advertised and selection was
under process.

2.1.14.3 Academic staff

The Institute having the status of deemed University, imparts education and training
in various disciplines of medicine. SKIMS Medical College, Bemina was recognized
by the Medical Council of India for 50 admissions to MBBS course annually. The
up-gradation of SKIMS Medical College, OPD and IPD as per norms and guidelines
prescribed by the Medical Council of India was approved in May 2009 by the
Governing Body of the Institute. However, the requisite infrastructure to comply with
MCI norms was to be fully created. The position of sanctioned and effective strength
of faculty in the Institute and Medical college as of March 2012 is indicated in Table
2.1.16 below:

candidates despite advertisement for these posts. Audit further noticed that the posts
of Registrar-cum-Controller of Examination, Deputy Registrar Academics, Deputy
Controller of Examination and Assistant Controller of Examinations in the Academics
Department had not been filled up since their retirement between October 2002 and
September 2011 and these posts were being managed by contractual consultant and
two administrative officers. The Director of the Institute stated that the posts could not
be filled up due to non-possessing of the requisite service by the feeder cadre of the
Department and that the personnel of requisite experience could not be obtained on
deputation basis from the University despite efforts in this behalf.

2.1.14.4 Irregularities in establishment matters

According to Jammu and Kashmir Civil Services Rules (CSR), a Government servant
promoted to higher post in officiating capacity is entitled to charge allowance till
regularisation of his promotion. Audit observed that pay of 118 employees promoted
to higher posts in officiating capacity during the period from January 1992 to March
2012 was fixed straight away in the promoted posts from the dates of their officiating
promotion under Article 77 (b) of J&K CSR without the approval of regularisation of
their promotion. This resulted in payment of excess pay and allowances of हैं 10.83
lakh noticed in six test-checked cases alone in the matter. After this was pointed out
by Audit, the Accounts Officer stated (July 2012) that recovery would be effected
from the salaries of the concerned employees.

Rule 67 (1) of the Jammu and Kashmir Civil Services (Leave) Rules, 1979 provide
that study leave shall be granted to a Government servant as leave without allowance.
Test-check of records (April 2012) revealed that study leave was sanctioned in favour
of 14 staff members, for acquiring higher studies between November 2005 and May
2010 and allowances of हैं 26.47 lakh had been paid irregularly to these employees
during the period of study leave in violation of these rules. After this was pointed out
in audit, it was stated (July 2012) that recovery would be affected from the salaries of
the concerned employees.

During the period April 2007 to March 2012, the Institute had irregularly booked an
amount of % 1.60 crore to head of account ‘salaries’ meant for regular employees for
payment on outsourcing of sanitation work. This misrepresented the expenditure on
salary of regular employees.

In one case, the Selection Committee were misinformed about the performance
appraisal reports and approval for promotion of senior staff member was secured on
the basis of misreported performance as ‘excellent’ whereas the actual recorded
performance was merely ‘satisfactory’. The importance of providing truthful inputs to
the high level committees whose members are not expected to have the benefit of
independent examination of material placed before them for securing their approvals
on various matters can hardly be over emphasized.

2.1.15 Management of sanitation in the premises

The sanitation of various areas of the Institute was opened (January 2001) for
privatisation to the extent of the budget for vacant posts of sanitary attendants in the
Housing and Sanitation Department of the Institute. The sanitation of the remaining
areas of the Institute was to be carried out by the available sanitation staff of the
Institute. However, 14 sanitary attendants, out of the available staff, were attached
with different departments of the Institute between April 2006 to November 2008 due
to which the sanitation work suffered in the Institute. The Officer in-charge Sanitation
stated (August 2012) that after deployment of these 14 sanitary attendants, to other
wings, the strength of work force got depleted thereby forcing the Institute to
outsource sanitation work of additional areas of the Institute to private contractors.
This led to extra expenditure on account of sanitation through private contractors.

2.1.16 Non-disposal of unserviceable stock

The Institute had not conducted auction of condemned store/ stock items valuing
% 2.31 crore since 2004. After this was pointed out by Audit, a survey committee was
constituted (July 2012) for ascertaining the position of unserviceable stock. The

37


Audit Report on Social, General, Revenue and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs) for the year ended 31 March 2012

Estates Officer, however, stated (July 2012) that the matter regarding convening
meeting of condemnation committee for disposal of unserviceable stock had been
taken up with the concerned authorities of the Institute. Further progress in the matter
was awaited (December 2012).

For biomedical waste management, a proposal to install one incinerator with capacity
of around 400 kgs/ hour was initiated in August 2009 which was not implemented
(May 2012).

2.1.17 Outstanding library books

The books valued at % three lakh were outstanding against the doctors, technologists
and other staff including migrant employees who had left the Institute. No steps to
recover the outstanding books or cost thereof had been taken by the Institute. The
Chief Librarian of the Institute stated (March 2012) that the matter regarding recovery
of the cost of the books was pending with the Joint Director (Administration) since
April 2010. Further progress in the matter was awaited (December 2012).

2.1.18 Monitoring

The functioning of the Institute has not been subjected to any formal, structured
review by any external or internal agency though Governing Body meetings do
provide an opportunity for a general overview. Medical research is one of the primary
objectives of the Institute but there is no documented vision, policy, agenda or action
plan to guide the research activities in different fields. Effective monitoring of
execution of all approved schemes, procurements of equipment, civil works and
patient care profile is essential for mid-term correction and over all progress in the
intended direction.

2.1.19 Internal Control mechanism

A proper internal control mechanism in an organisation is essential for smooth
running of various activities and implementation of programmes undertaken by it. The
existence or otherwise thereof adversely affects day to day activities of the
organisation. Audit noticed certain inadequacies in internal controls of the Institute as
brought out below:

¢ The Asset register of movable and immovable property to indicate the description
of assets, cost and date of acquisition of asset had not been maintained as a result
the position of assets available with the Institute could not be ascertained.

e The irregularities in personal claims mentioned above also indicate lack of
adequate internal control.

2.1.20 Conclusion

The performance review of the Institute revealed the following financial and
operational deficiencies:

Financial deficiencies: The financial management was deficient as cases of persistent
under-utilization of allotted budget grants, irregular withdrawal of funds and their
subsequent crediting to Civil Deposits, irregular utilization of sundry hospital
receipts, etc. were noticed in audit.

Operational deficiencies: The number of patients attending OPD including super
specialty centres has risen by 28 per cent from 2007-08 to 2011-12 and patients had to
wait for surgeries, diagnostic and investigation for conducting these tests. The number

38


Chapter-2-Audit Findings an Social, General and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs)

of patients awaiting surgeries in seven In-patient Department was in the range of 20 to
241. The Emergency Medicine Department had not been fully equipped to deal with
cases of road traffic accidents. The shortage of Faculty, Medical, Paramedical and
Administrative staff had not been reviewed in a formal structured manner and
lopsided position of the staff though realized by the Governing Body had not been
corrected. Audit noticed abnormal delays in the procurement of medical equipment
which resulted in locking up of funds. The infrastructure development was not
planned properly as the works targeted for completion by the year 2008 were
incomplete. The roadmap for achieving excellence in medical research had not been
prepared and advanced centre for human genetics and local area networking system
had also not been established by the Institute due to improper planning

2.1.21 Recommendations
The Government may consider to:

* Frame perspective/ long term plans and assess the infrastructure /manpower
requirements based on approved norms, benchmarked with other medical
institutions of comparable size and mandate.

e Ensure proper utilization of funds and avoid persistent savings for efficient
management of financial resources.

© Streamline the system of procurement of equipment and medicines to cut down
delays for better patient care.

RURAL DEVELOPMENT DEPARTMENT

2.2 Mahatma Gandhi National Rural Employment Guarantee
Scheme

National Rural Employment Guarantee Act (NREGA) was notified by the
Government of India (GOD in September 2005 with the objective of enhancing
livelihood security in rural areas by providing at least 100 days of guaranteed
wage employment in a financial year to every rural household whose adult
members volunteer to do unskilled manual labour. The Act was extended to the
State of Jammu and Kashmir with effect from May 2007. The scheme was
launched in the State in phases and gradually extended to all districts of the
State from April 2008. A performance review of the scheme by Audit brought to
fore several areas of concern such as unauthorized retention of funds, delayed
release and diversion of funds, execution of unapproved works, abandonment of
works, preparation of faulty estimates, delays in payment of wages to the
workers, shortfall in engagement of staff and their inadequate training,
inadequate inspection of works and social audit for proper monitoring of scope,
extent and quality of works executed and retention of undisbursed balances in
the State Employment Guarantee Fund. During the period the audit was
conducted, there were no ongoing works that could be inspected by Audit teams.
A limited beneficiary survey covering 793 beneficiaries was carried out. The
Audit findings are based primarily on the examination of the records maintained
in the offices of the Department.

Highlights

¢ No Annual Report on the state of affairs of the scheme had been brought out
and tabled in the State legislature as required under programme guidelines.

(Paragraph: 2.2.

e¢ State Employment Guarantee Fund, though established, has not been
operated as intended and as on March 2012, there was undisbursed balance
of = 15.81 crore in the Fund, mainly representing arrears of undisbursed
difference in wages.
(Paragraph: 2.2.6.2)
e Perspective plans had not been formulated in the initial years of
implementation of the scheme and the Labour Budget had been submitted to
the Government of India belatedly.

(Paragraphs: 2.2.7.1 and 2.2.7.2)

« Unspent balances had increased from % 7.07 crore as of April 2007 to
% 127.66 crore at the close of March 2012. Cases of retention, delayed release
and diversion of funds were noticed in audit.
