2.2.1 Introduction

The National Rural Employment Guarantee Act (NREGA) was notified by the
Government of India (GOI) in September 2005 with the objective of enhancing
livelihood security in rural areas by providing at least 100 days of guaranteed wage
employment in a financial year to every household whose adult members volunteer to
do unskilled manual work. The Act is a right based framework for adult members of a
tural household willing to do unskilled manual work. It prescribes time bound
guarantee wherein if employment is not provided within 15 days from the date of
application for work, the State Government has to give unemployment allowance. The
Act was extended to the State of Jammu and Kashmir with effect from May 2007. The
programme which became effective from February 2006 was initially launched in
three’ districts of the State in the first phase and extended to two” more districts in
the year 2007-08 and to all the districts of the State from April 2008. The programme
was rechristened as Mahatma Gandhi National Rural Employment Guarantee Scheme
(MGNREGS) in October 2009.

During the period from 2007-08 to 2011-12, fund amounting to हैं 1635.35 crore was
released under the programme in the State against which, an amount of हैं 1501.58
crore (92 per cent) was spent by the department on the implementation of the scheme
as detailed in Table 2.2.1 below:

“ Doda, Kupwara and Poonch

Anantnag and Jammu

41


Audit Report on Social, General, Revenue and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs) for the year ended 31 March 2012

Table-2.2.1
(®in crore)

Year Opening Balance Receipt Expenditure
2007-08 7.07 85.73 65.26
2008-09 29.09 117.43 86.90
2009-10 46.01 162.78 194.42
2010-11 12.88 411.96 378.28
2011-12 46.93 857.45 776.72

Total 1635.35 | 35058 |

The Act fosters conditions for inclusive growth ranging from basic wage security and
recharging rural economy to a transformative empowerment process of democracy.
The Act provides opportunities to develop rural infrastructure through water-shed
development, restoration of water bodies, activities aimed at forestry, land
development and soil erosion, flood control, construction of roads and institutional
facilities. The main objectives of the programme are:

e Enhancing the livelihood security of people in rural areas by guarantecing
hundred days of wage-employment in a financial year to a rural household.

© Creation of a strong social safety net for vulnerable groups by providing a fall-
back employment source, when other employment alternatives are scarce or
inadequate.

e Strengthening of the natural resource base of rural livelihood and create durable
assets in rural areas through the process of providing employment on works that
addresses causes of chronic poverty.

¢ Empowerment of rural poor through the process of a rights-based law, and

e Exercising transparency and grass-root democracy in governance.

2.2.2 Organisational set-up

In the State of Jammu and Kashmir, a State Employment Guarantee Council (SEGC)
aids and advises the State Government in implementation of the programme. The
activities of the scheme at the State level are carried out by the SEGC through its
Member Secretary (SEGC) functioning under the overall control of the Commissioner
Secretary, Rural Development Department. The State Government designated (March
2007) Assistant Commissioner Development as District Project Coordinator, District
Panchayat Officer as Additional District Project Coordinator and Block Development
Officer as Programme Officer under the Scheme.

2.2.3 Scope of Audit and methodology

The performance Audit of the scheme covering the period from 2007-08 to 2011-12
was carried out between March 2012 and June 2012 through test-check of records of
the Member Secretary (SEGC), six’® Assistant Commissioners Development (ACDs),
127 Block Development Officers (BDOs) and 117 Gram Panchayats.

The selection of the Districts was made by statistical sampling method using ‘Simple
Random Sampling without Replacement’ (SRSWOR) which was based on the
number of job cards issued in a District. The selection of Blocks was done through
‘Simple Random Sampling with Replacement’ (SRSWR) by putting the Blocks in an
alphabetical order. Twenty per cent of the Gram Panchayats were selected on ‘Risk
Analysis’ and the balance through ‘Population Proportional to Size with replacement,
(PPSWR) method as provided in the guidelines. The selection of works was, however
made on ‘Random Sampling Method’ (RSM)

Besides, the Audit interacted with 793 beneficiaries and conducted on the spot check
of as many works on the basis of one beneficiary per work. As against the available
fund of % 1642.42 crore, हैं 1501.58 crore was spent by the department on the
implementation of the scheme. Out of this, an expenditure of € 102.56 crore incurred
in the six test-checked districts constituting seven per cent of the total expenditure of
हैँ 1501.58 crore incurred by the Department under the programme during the period
of review was covered in Audit

An entry conference was held on 02 March 2012 by the Principal Accountant General
with the Commissioner Secretary, Rural Development Department wherein
objectives, scope, criteria and methodology of audit were discussed. An exit

2.2.4 Audit objectives

The audit objectives of the performance audit were to assess whether:

Structural mechanisms were put in place for the implementation of the
programme.

Planning process (including convergence of other schemes) for implementation of
the programme was efficient and effective.

Financial management (fund allocation, utilization and proper accountal) was
efficient and effective.

Implementation of programme was carried out economically, efficiently and
effectively in providing works to the needy persons.

Maintenance of data and records as well as Management Information System
generation was efficient and effective.

Human Resource Management for implementation of the programme was
efficient.

Monitoring mechanism (Social Audit, Gram Sabha meeting and Inspections by
higher authorities at Block, District, State and Central levels) was in place and

effective.
2.2.5 Audit criteria
Audit criteria has been derived from the following sources:
e Provisions of the NREG Act 2005 and amendments thereto

e Guidelines/Circular instructions issued on the scheme by the Ministry of Rural
Development, Government of India (GOI)/ State Government.

e Jammu and Kashmir Rural Employment Guarantee Scheme 2007, J&K Financial
Code and Book of Financial powers.

2.2.6 Structural mechanisms

For implementation of the programme in accordance with provisions of the Act,
structural mechanism had to be put in place and adequate capacity building
measures to be taken both by the GOI and the State Government. The State
Government had to formulate rules and the Gram Panchayats at the Village level had
to be made pivotal bodies for implementation of the programme from planning to
execution and monitoring level. The responsibility of formulation of plans and
Labour Budget for monitoring and supervision of the programme at the District level
was to be entrusted to District Panchayats, to be formed at District level, with a view
to ensuring public participation in developmental works.

For implementation of the programme, the State Government notified the Jammu
and Kashmir Rural Employment Guarantee Scheme in March 2007. The Panchayats
in the State came into being with effect from June 2011 after the Panchayat elections
conducted during the period March-June 2011. Till then the role of the Panchayats
for identification of the works for execution in a village was taken over by the
Blocks through Village Level Workers (VLWs). No Intermediate Panchayats at
Block level and no District Panchayats at District level were in existence in the State
(March 2012). The Annual Action Plans (AAPs) formulated at the Panchayat/ Block

व्‌


Chapter-2-Audit Findings an Social, General and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs)

level were forwarded to the Assistant Commissioners Development of the District
for incorporation in the composite District Plan,

2.2.6.1 Constitution of State Employment Guarantee Council

For the purpose of regular monitoring and reviewing the implementation of the Act
at the State level, the State Government was required to constitute a Jammu and
Kashmir State Employment Guarantee Council which was to meet at least twice in a
year. The State Employment Guarantee Council (SEGC) was constituted by the
Government in March 2006 with Hon’ble Chief Minister of the State as Chairperson
of the Council. However, from September 2008 Minister-in-charge for Rural
Development was nominated as Chairperson ex-officio of SEGC. The SEGC held
only three meetings (March 2008, January 2010 and April 2011) during the period
2007-12 against the requirement of 10 meetings. It was observed that the decisions
taken by the SEGC in the meetings had been implemented partially. The decisions
regarding strengthening of State MGNREGS Cell, appointment of requisite staff
under the scheme and of consultants at the State/ District level for quality
monitoring, routing of funds through State Employment Guarantee Fund etc. had not
been implemented as of March 2012. Due to non-appointment of consultants at the
State level, the MGNREGs Cell which was to provide technical support in the key
areas like Information Education and Communication strategies, training needs,
planning, monitoring and evaluation, social audit and financial management under
the scheme could not look into these key areas. Further, the Annual Report on the
state of affairs of implementation of the programme in the State could not be
brought out and tabled in the State Legislature. The Member Secretary (SEGC)
stated (October 2012) that the Annual Reports for the period from 2007-08 to
2010-11 would be printed out in a consolidated form to be presented in the next
session of the Assembly.

2.2.6.2 Constitution of State Employment Guarantee Fund (SEGF)

For the purpose of implementation of the scheme, the State Government had to
establish a State Employment Guarantee Fund (SEGF). The State Government
accordingly established (October 2008) a separate Bank account for the scheme in
Jammu and Kashmir Bank Limited, Civil Secretariat but the SEGF had not been made
fully operational and the particulars of the fund were not communicated to the GOI.
As a result, the funds were released by the GOI directly to the districts for
implementation of the programme. This defeated the very purpose of establishment of
SEGF. An amount of हैं 17.20 lakh released by the GOI in June 2008 had been
deposited in the fund account. Further, = 15 crore released by the State Government
on account of difference in the wages of the beneficiaries due to enhancement in wage
rates retrospectively had been deposited in the fund account in July 2010. As the
wages had not been paid to the beneficiaries, the amount alongwith interest of = 15.81
crore continued to remain unutilized as of March 2012. The Commissioner-Secretary
during exit conference stated (November 2012) that proposal to convert undisbursed
balance of % 15.81 crore as State’s matching share under the programme with the
concurrence of the Planning and Development Department was under active
consideration of the Department.

in the districts where DRDAs are non-existent

2.2.7 Planning

Planning is the main tool for successful implementation of a programme. The
Programme guidelines provide for preparation of perspective plans (PP) by the
districts to facilitate advance planning and to provide a development perspective for
the district. A district perspective plan for five years would have the advantage of
facilitating the annual labour budgets as a framework of long term planning and
would enable the adoption of a project approach to works. The Annual Development
Plans (ADP) which would flow from the PPs would be working plans that contain
the nature, number and nomenclature of the developmental works to be executed in
a year. For this purpose, the involvement of the Gram Panchayats was to be made
mandatory for identification of works. Based on such identification, the ADPs had to
be drawn, which would be converted into Labour Budget (LB) in financial terms at
the Block, District and the State level for transmission to the GOI for release of
funds. Timely submission of LBs to the GOI was, as such, necessary for release of
funds in time. Following points were observed in audit.

2.2.7.1 Non-formulation of Perspective Plans

Audit observed that none of the test-checked districts had formulated PPs during the
period of review except for three districts (Udhampur, Rajouri and Leh) where the
PPs had been formulated belatedly from the year 2010-11. The MS (SEGC) stated
(November 2012) that PPs had been formulated by all the districts. The reply is quite
contrary to the factual position of formulation of PPs by the test-checked districts. In
the absence of PPs, the advance planning to provide developmental perspective for
the district could not be achieved.

2.2.7.2 Delayed/Non-submission of Labour Budget

As per the programme guidelines, the Gram Panchayats have to identify and
recommend the works in the Gram Sabha to be held on 2 October for execution in
the ensuing year and forward the development plan to the Programme Officer (PO)
by 15 October every year. The PO has to scrutinize, re-refer the plan to Gram
Panchayats, if necessary, and consolidate the plan and submit the same to
Intermediate Panchayat (at Block level) by 15 November which has to approve the
Block Plan within 15 days for submission to the District Programme Coordinator
(DPC) by 30 November. The DPC has to submit the Block-wise shelf of projects
and the Labour Budget (LB) thereon to the District Panchayat Officer (DPO) by 15
December. After getting it approved by the District Panchayat by 31 December, the
DPC has to forward the LB to the State Government, which will forward it
alongwith its recommendations to the GOI by 31 January.

Audit, however, observed that records regarding submission of the plans by the
Panchayats to the blocks were not available in any of the test-checked Panchayats.
Test-checked blocks in two districts (Rajouri and Udhampur) had submitted the LBs
to the DPC after delays of two to three months. The details/records of submission of
LBs were not available in other blocks of four test-checked districts. Similarly no
records/details regarding submission of LBs during the period 2007-10 to the GOT
were available with the MS (SEGC). However, Audit noticed that submission of
LBs to the GOI by the State Government had been delayed by two months during
each year 2010-11 and 2011-12. The MS (SEGC) stated (October 2012) that the

46


Chapter-2-Audit Findings an Social, General and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs)

Annual LB was submitted to the GOI on the basis of which, the State was able to
seek the Central assistance under the scheme.

2.2.7.3 Convergence of scheme funds

The programme guidelines provide for convergence of scheme funds with the funds
from other sources such as Finance Commission, Centrally Sponsored Schemes like
Swaranjayanti Gram Swarozgar Yogna (SGSY), Drought Prone Area Programme
(DPAP), Desert Development Programme (DDP), etc. for construction of durable
community assets/works permissible under the scheme. Special guidelines for this
purpose had to be issued by the State Government. Audit did not observe any action
having been taken at any level in this direction by the Department. It was, however,
seen that a Committee had been constituted (March 2012) at the State level to look
into this aspect. The Commissioner-Secretary stated (November 2012) that the
mechanism of convergence of various schemes was not followed by the Department.

2.2.8 Financial Management

According to the provisions of the Act, the GOI has to bear the expenditure under
the scheme towards entire cost of wages of unskilled manual workers, 75 per cent of
the cost of material/wages of skilled and semi-skilled workers, administrative
expenses to include inter alia salary and allowances of the Programme Officer and
his supporting staff and work site facilities. The State Government has to bear the
expenditure towards 25 per cent of the cost of material/wages of skilled and semi
skilled workers, unemployment allowance and administrative expenses of the
SEGC. The position of funds released by the GOI, State Government under the
scheme and the expenditure incurred thereagainst during the period from 2007-08 to
2011-12 is detailed in Table 2.2.2 below:

2.2.8.1 Retention of funds

The funds received under the scheme by the State Government was to be credited to
the SEGF wherefrom these were to be released to the Assistant Commissioners
Development (ACDs). The ACDs had to release the funds to the Blocks for further
release to the Panchayats for meeting expenditure on account of wages of the labour
and material component under the Scheme. However, due to non-operation of the
SEGF the money was released by the GOI to the districts directly. Audit observed
that in three districts (Udhampur, Rajouri and Leh), funds were received by the
District Rural Development Agency (DRDA) from the GOI for further release to the
ACDs at the District level. Audit noticed retention of 83.77 crore by the DRDA
during 2007-12 for periods ranging from 20 days to over five months. Reasons
thereof, though called for from the MS (SEGC), were awaited (December 2012).

2.2.8.2 Delay in release of funds

The delay in release of funds during the period 2007-12 by the ACDs to the Blocks
in four”? test-checked districts ranged between one month and nine months.
Similarly, the delay in release of funds by eight test-checked Blocks of these
districts to the panchayats was also in the range of one month to nine months beyond
the permissible limit of 15 days. The ACDs/BDOs stated (June 2012) that the delay
was procedural and that a portion of the funds had to be retained till the actual
demand arose. The reply was not acceptable as the funds were required to be
released immediately on their receipt.

2.2.8.3 Diversion of funds

The programme guidelines provide that funds allocated for the scheme should not be
used for other purposes under any circumstances. Audit observed that the scheme
funds to the extent of = 1.06 crore had been diverted by the Director, RDD, Jammu,
three ACDs (Leh, Rajouri and Poonch) and BDO Rajouri on the activities not
related to the scheme. It was seen that the Director, RDD, Jammu had got transferred
% 38.62 lakh from his subordinate agencies (10 ACDs and 13 BDOs) during the
period 2008-12 to his official Bank Account and spent हैं 19.55 lakh on payment of
compensation, purchase of diesel generator and other items in violation of the
guidelines. Audit further noticed that हैं 31.68 lakh of the SEGF had been utilised on
hiring of vehicles, purchase of stationery items, POL, travelling bills of the officers
and State Quality Monitors, etc. during the period from December 2009 to March
2012 by the MS (SEGC). This tantamount to diversion of scheme funds. The MS

प्र Leh, Poonch, Rajouri and Udhampur

48


Chapter-2-Audit Findings an Social, General and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs)

(SEGC) stated (April 2012) that the amount had been utilized on activities
permissible under the scheme. The reply was not acceptable as the expenditure on
these activities had to be met out of the funds meant for administrative expenses.

2.2.8.4 Under-statement/ over-statement of expenditure figures

Accounts of a unit should depict a true and fair picture of all the transactions having
taken place with proper summary/remarks, wherever necessary. Notwithstanding
this, it was seen that three BDOs (Poonch, Rajouri and Doongi) and ACD, Poonch
had not accounted for 15.98 lakh earned as interest from the banks on programme
funds during the period 2010-12 in the financial statement forwarded to GOT thereby
understating the available balances under the scheme. In reply, the BDOs stated
(June 2012) that necessary correction would be incorporated.

As per programme guidelines, the State would become eligible for the release of the
second instalment only after 60 per cent of the carlier releases including the opening
balance, if any, have been utilized. It was, however, seen that ACDs Udhampur and
Rajouri inflated the expenditure figures by % 79.69 lakh and % 11.18 crore
respectively through the utilisation certificates submitted (October 2011, February
2012) to the GOI to get further funds released under the scheme. The BDOs stated
(June 2012) that the figures of expenditure had been communicated by the ACDs to
the GOI without taking cognizance of the figures furnished in the expenditure
statements by them. This was indicative of the fact that the ACDs had taken recourse
to wrong reporting to get the funds released.

2.2.9 Programme implementation

Under the programme guidelines, door to door survey had to be conducted by the
panchayats for registration of households willing to register themselves under the
provisions of the scheme. Records relating thereto had to be maintained at the Gram
Panchayats. NGOs/CSOs had to be engaged for motivating the rural population for
demanding 100 days of work under the programme. This aspect had hardly been
looked into at any level and the shortcomings observed are brought out in the
succeeding paragraphs:

2.2.9.1 Provision of guaranteed employment

The adult members of every household (HH) residing in any rural area and willing
to do unskilled manual labour have to get themselves registered with the GPs/Blocks
and it shall be the duty of the GP/Blocks to register the household and issue a job
card containing details of adult members of the household. Further, Jammu and
Kashmir Rural Employment Guarantee Scheme provide that a job card with unique
ID number was to be issued by the Panchayat to the registered household. The
position of HHs issued job cards, HHs which demanded employment and those
provided employment in the State during the period from 2007-08 to 2011-12 is
indicated in Table 2.2.3 below:

Out of the total number of 27.69 lakh”! Households in the State, number of
Households provided employment ranged between 1.44 lakh (five per cent) and 5.67
lakh (20 per cent) during 2007-12. The increase in issuance of job cards was a
positive sign showing that more and more people are approaching the implementing
authorities to derive the benefits under the programme. While as the number of HHs
which demanded employment had increased from 1.44 lakh in 2007-08 to 6.78 lakh
in 2011-12, the HHs actually provided employment also increased from 1.44 lakh to
5.68 lakh during the period. The HHs which had actually been provided employment
was less than the number of HHs which demanded employment. 1,56,183 HHs (nine
per cent) were not provided employment during the period 2008-12. Under the
provisions of the Act, these HHs were entitled to unemployment allowance which
had not been paid as no provision of the allowance at the rate two per cent of annual
allocation was kept in SEGF.

Audit also observed that the number of HHs provided 100 days of employment
increased from two per cent (2007-08) to 14 per cent (2011-12) of the total HHs
provided employment. Non-providing of 100 days of employment to the bulk of the
HHs ranging from 86 to 98 per cent was attributable to delayed start of works by the
Blocks/Panchayats in the villages. This defeated the basic tenets of the programme
which call for providing assured employment to the rural population to ameliorate
their lot. The Member Secretary (SEGC) stated (October 2012) that no incidence of
not providing employment to the job seekers had been noticed by the Department.

2.2.9.2 Inadequate coverage of women beneficiaries

Under the provisions of the Act, the priority for providing unskilled manual work
shall be given to women in such a way that at least one-third of the beneficiaries
shall be women who have been registered and requested for work. The overall
representation of women was observed to be poor being in the range of zero to 19
per cent in five test-checked districts of the State. It ranged from two to four per cent
in Udhampur, zero to four per cent in Rajouri, zero to 11 per cent (2007-08) in
Poonch, zero to four per cent (2008-09) in Baramulla and one to 19 per cent in
Pulwama during the period from 2007-08 to 2011-12. However, it was
comparatively high in Leh where it ranged between 26 and 42 per cent. The BDOs
stated (May-June 2012) that the women were reluctant to come forward to seek jobs
under the scheme.

Chapter-2-Audit Findings an Social, General and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs)

2.2.9.3 Non-engagement of beneficiaries as mates

For supervision of work and recording attendance at worksite, a Mate was to be
designated for each work. It was however, seen in audit that mates had not been
engaged in any of the test-checked blocks. Reasons therefor, though called for, were
not assigned. Further the non-employment of women as mates defeated the very aim
of women empowerment.

2.2.9.4 Delay in payment of wages

As per the Act, it is essential to ensure that the wages are paid in time on weekly
basis and in any case not later than a fortnight after the date on which the work was
done. In the event of any delay in payment of wages the workers are entitled to
compensation as per the provisions of the payment of wages Act, 1936 to be borne
by the State Government. The programme guidelines provide that when the amount
is paid, a wage slip had to be generated for issuance to the worker stating therein the
quantum of wages credited to their bank accounts against the work done by the
workers, Audit scrutiny revealed that in nine test-checked Blocks, the BDOs had not
issued wage slips to the workers in token of having made such payments. However,
in remaining three test-checked blocks, delay in payment of wages of हैं 38.58 lakh
ranging between three and 547 days was noticed in 245 cases during the period
2008-12. The BDOs attributed (June 2012) the delay to banks, delayed release of
funds to Panchayats and delays in payments of wages by Panchayats.

Execution of works

The number of works taken up for execution each year had been very high and the
number of incomplete works increased from 3929 at the end of March 2008 to
34886 at the close of March 2012. Taking up execution of new works in bulk
without ensuring completion of incomplete works, delay in accord of approval to
District Plans and shortage of staff resulted in swelling up of incomplete works at
the close of each year. This was indicative of defective planning by the District level
authorities.

The position was no different in the test-checked districts as the incomplete works
showed a quantum jump from 1986 works in 2007-08 to 10,333 at the end of March
2012. The highest increase of 900 per cent was observed in Baramulla District
where the incomplete works increased from 365 at the end of 2009-10 to 2889 at the
close of 2011-12. The position was, however, comparatively better in Leh district.

Audit Report on Social, General, Revenue and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs) for the year ended 31 March 2012

2.2.9.6 Non-identification of works

Under the programme guidelines, every work approved for execution under the
scheme is to be provided a unique identification number which had to be indicated
in each and every document related to the work. Audit observed that the said code
had neither been indicated in the Annual Action Plans nor in the progress reports in
the test-checked districts. In absence thereof, the possibility of taking up the same
work for execution more than once cannot be ruled out. The BDOs attributed (June
2012) this to shortage of manpower or lack of knowledge regarding recording of the
identification number against the works.

2.2.9.7 Execution of unapproved works

As per the Act, all works had to be identified for execution and a shelf of the works
got approved. Works had to be taken up from the approved shelf of projects which
had been incorporated in the AAPs. Audit noticed that in seven” blocks of three”®
districts, 2950 works (estimated cost: = 27.79 crore) included in the approved AAPs
for the years 2007-08 to 2011-12 had not been taken up for execution while as 680
works (estimated cost: हैं 8.39 crore) which were not included in AAPs were taken up
for execution by the BDOs and an expenditure of % 5.24 crore had been incurred on
these unapproved works. The reasons for not taking up the approved works by the
BDOs were attributed (June 2012) to non-allotment of sufficient funds. The
contention of the BDOs is not acceptable as significant funds were available with
them.

2.2.9.8 Abandoned works

Audit analysis of records in nine™ blocks of five districts revealed that 484 works
estimated to cost = 7.40 crore taken up for execution during the period 2007-12
remained incomplete. These works had been abandoned midway after spending
% 2.92 crore on them rendering the expenditure unfruitful.

The BDOs attributed (April-June 2012) abandoning of these works to non-
availability of labour, land disputes, awaited forest clearances etc. The action of the
BDOs in taking up the works on disputed sites or without forest clearance was
irregular.

2.2.9.9 Execution of works without technical sanction

The programme guidelines provide that all works are required to get Administrative
Approval (AA) and Technical Sanction (TS) in advance before work is actually
taken up for execution. It was seen in audit that against 46795 works costing
% 318.75 crore taken up in three® districts during the period 2007-12, technical
sanctions were not accorded in respect of 44932 works (96 per cent) costing
% 297.94 crore. Taking up of works without technical sanction was irregular. The
ACDs/BDOs stated (April-June 2012) that all the works are taken up for execution
after obtaining the necessary AA/TS and that in some cases these might not have
been obtained for which the matter would be taken up with the EEs, Rural
Engineering Wing. The reply of the ACDs/BDOs was not acceptable as the quantum
of works taken up for execution without TS was very high and constituted 96 per
cent of the total works.

2.2.9.10 Unrealistic estimation of works

The framing of estimates before execution of a work is an utter necessity as it gives
an insight into the technical parameters of the work besides the actual idea of the
quantum of funds required for the scheme. The estimates of each work are thus
required to be framed meticulously.

It was, however, seen that huge variations existed between the estimates of works
and the actual spending thereon. In nine” blocks of five’’ districts, 672 works
estimated to cost = 10.45 crore taken up for execution during the period 2007-12 had
been completed at a cost of हैं 3.52 crore which constituted 34 per cent of the
estimated cost. The variation between the estimates and the actual expenditure in
case of individual works ranged between 20 and 73 per cent. This is indicative of the
casual approach of the technical staff of the department in framing of estimates
without verification of the site conditions leading to unrealistic estimation. The
BDOs Mendhar and Poonch attributed (June 2012) this to availability of
construction material near the sites of works executed and also due to some changes
in site conditions. The BDO Udhampur stated (April 2012) that the technical staff
would be asked to explain the position. The replies are not acceptable as framing of
unrealistic estimates lead to misrepresentation of facts in the AAPs with direct
bearing on funding.

2.2.9.11 Procurement of construction material

As per the GOI instructions (November 2010), the State Government had to frame a
procurement policy at the State level for procurement of key construction material
for the works. It was however seen that no uniform procurement policy was in place
in the State and procurement of construction material was being made from different
sources at District/ Block levels. The Director, RDD, Jammu had directed (October
2010) ACDs to procure material from Stores Procurement Department (SPD). The
non-uniformity in procurement of construction material was visible from the fact
that the executing agencies in Kashmir Division had procured material from JK
Cements Limited whereas in Jammu Division, the executing agencies had carried
out procurement from JAKFED and SICOP. The ACD, Poonch stated (June 2012)
that construction material is procured from SICOP/JAKFED which supplies it
within the shortest possible time as compared to the SPD which supplies the
material late. The BDOs, Rajouri and Chenani stated (April 2012) that the material
is procured from other sources when not available with SPD. The fact however
remains that no uniform policy had been framed to ensure procurement in a
transparent manner. The MS (SEGC) informed that the Directors at the divisional
level had been advised (April 2012) to formulate the procurement policy.

2.2.10 Impact assessment

2.2.10.1 Impact assessment by the State

As per the programme guidelines regular evaluations and sample surveys for
specific scheme works was to be conducted to assess the outcomes. For this purpose
broad guidelines for evaluation studies were to be framed by the SEGC. The
technical resource support system was to be set up at the State and District levels to
assist in the planning, designing, monitoring, evaluation and quality audit of various
initiatives. For this purpose, Resource Institutions had to be identified by the State
Government and a panel of institutions/ agencies for technical resource support had
to be prepared. Audit observed that neither had the Technical Resource Institutions
been identified nor any panel of institutions/ agencies for the technical resource
support drawn by the State Government. The SEGC had also not launched
evaluation studies to assess the impact of the scheme.

2.2.11 Maintenance of data and records
2.2111 Maintenance of records

According to the programme guidelines, door-to-door survey had to be conducted by
the panchayats for registration of households willing to register themselves. Records
relating thereto had to be maintained by the Gram Panchayats. In absence of Gram
Panchayats in the State, these aspects were to be dealt by the respective BDOs. It
was however noticed that no door-to-door survey had been conducted by the BDOs
in any of the test-checked blocks.

According to the programme guidelines, records relating to registration of
Households (HHs) seeking job under the programme and those actually demanding
job out of the registered HHs were to be maintained in the shape of job card
application registers, job card registers and employment registers both at the GP and
the Block levels. Audit observed that no such records had been maintained at the
Panchayat level in any of the test-checked Blocks. In absence of these vital records,
Audit could not verify the details of beneficiaries willing to work and the
employment provided to them within the prescribed time limit of 15 days of
registration. Further, some important records like Muster roll receipt and issue
registers, asset registers, works registers, monthly allotment and_ utilization
certificate watch registers and complaint registers had not been maintained at the
Gram Panchayat level. These records had, however, been maintained partially at
Block level in all the test-checked Blocks. As a result of this, proper watch on the
physical and financial aspects of implementation of the scheme could not be kept by
the Gram Panchayat/ Block level functionaries in the areas of their jurisdiction.
After this was pointed out in audit, the BDOs stated (April-June 2012) that the

55


Audit Report on Social, General, Revenue and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs) for the year ended 31 March 2012

records could not be maintained due to shortage of staff. The Member Secretary
(SEGC) stated (October 2012) that all the District Programme officers had been
directed to complete and update all the relevant records relating to the programme.

by the department under the scheme during the year 2011-12. Against the amount of

uploaded as of April 2012. The less uploading of expenditure figures was attributed
by the ACDs/BDOs (April/ June 2012) to dearth of staff, frequent power cuts, poor

2.2.13 Monitoring mechanism

2.2.13.1 Constitution of monitoring agencies

An Ombudsman had to be appointed to deal with the complaints received with
reference to the implementation of the scheme. Besides State and District Quality
Monitors had to be engaged for verification of works undertaken under the
programme. Test-check of records revealed that the Department had, however,
neither appointed Ombudsman for complaints nor engaged District Quality Monitors

for monitoring of the programme.
2.2.13.2 Social Audit

The Act gives a central role to Social Audit. The basic objective of the Social Audit
is to ensure public accountability in the implementation of projects, laws and
policies. The State Government was to constitute an independent organization/

Directorate at the State level for facili

iting Social Audit. Such audits have to be an

ongoing process of participation to ensure that legal guarantees and entitlements
flow to the workers in a legitimate way. Under the programme guidelines, Social
Audit is to be conducted by Gram Sabhas once in every six months and the findings
thereof apart from being made public are to be submitted by the State Government

58



Chapter-2-Audit Findings an Social, General and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs)

to the CAG of India every year in a summarised form. No consolidated data
regarding the conduct of social audits was maintained by the Department. The non-
maintenance of consolidated records at the Divisional or the State level was
indicative of the fact that monitoring of this aspect had not been done at the higher
levels. However, the position of conduct of social audit in the six test-checked
districts was dismal. Against the target of 4528 social audits, only 2577 social audits
had been conducted during the period from 2007-08 to 2011-12 revealing a shortfall
of 43 per cent. Despite conduct of these social audits, none of the blocks could
produce the Social Audit Reports to audit. The Member Secretary (SEGC) stated
(October 2012) that an independent Directorate of Social Audit has been set up
which is responsible for coordinating social audit at Panchayat level with the help of
District Social Coordinators and other stake holders under the programme.

2.2.14 Conclusion

The State Employment Guarantee Fund though established had not been made
operational affecting monitoring of funds at the State level. Perspective plans had
not been formulated in any of the test checked districts in time. The shortfall of 76
per cent of staff (4225 personnel) under the scheme affected smooth implementation
of the scheme. Delay in release and diversion of funds was noticed at all the levels.

Audit Report on Social, General, Revenue and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs) for the year ended 31 March 2012

Unspent balances under the programme had increased from % 7.07crore to = 127.66
crore during the review period. Delay in payment of wages to the workers ranged
between three and 547 days. Records in the shape of job card application registers,
job card registers and employment registers had not been maintained at the Gram
Panchayat level. Details of HHs registered had not been maintained at any level. Out
of the total number of 27.69 lakh Households in the State, number of Households
provided employment ranged between 1.44 lakh (five per cent) and 5.67 lakh
(20 per cent) during 2007-12. Nine percent of HHs who had demanded employment
were not provided employment nor had 100 days of assured employment been
provided to 86 to 98 per cent of the workers during the period 2007-12. While
unapproved works had been executed at a cost रैं 5.24 crore, the works included in
the AAPs had not been taken up for execution. A total number of 484 works had
been abandoned midway after spending % 2.92 crore thereon. Huge number of works
had been executed without the required Technical Sanction. Preparation of
unrealistic estimates of works was rampant. No uniform procurement policy for
procurement of construction material had been framed by the Department. The
mechanism for convergence of MGNREGS with other schemes had not been put in
place. Monitoring mechanism, Inspections and Social Audit were inadequate

2.2.15 Recommendations
The Government may consider to ensure:

¢ scrupulously following up the modalities prescribed in the MGNREG Act
and Guidelines during implementation of the Scheme/Programme.

¢ adequate staff strength in the department) MGNREGS-Cell for
implementation and monitoring of various activities of the programme.

© constitution and strengthening of the Monitoring Committees to monitor
the quality of works and the Social Audit for inspection of works under the
scheme.

POWER DEVELOPMENT DEPARTME

2.3 Power Transmission Utilities
Executive Summary

The National Electricity Policy (NEP) recognized the need for continued
development of National Grid for providing adequate infrastructure for
inter-state transmission of power. Both the Central Transmission Utility
(CTU) and the State Transmission Utility (STU) have the key
responsibility of network planning and development based on the
National Electricity Plan (NEP) in coordination with all the concerned
agencies. The transmission of electricity and Grid operations in the State
are managed and controlled by the Power Development Department.
The Performance Audit of Transmission utilities in the State revealed
under-utilisation of plan funds and non-achievement of targets in
capacity additions in respect of Grid sub-stations and Transmission
lines. Almost all the Grid sub-stations and Transmission line projects
witnessed a time over-run due to inadequate planning, faulty designs etc.
Some of the completed projects could not be operationalised due to
incompletion of related works, with the result the infrastructure created
was lying idle. The transmission losses in the State exceeded the Central
Electricity Authority (CEA) norm of four per cent during the period
2008-12. Due to in-efficient Grid management the State Government had
to pay the Unscheduled Interchange (UI) charges. The disaster
management setup did not exist in the Department for immediate
restoration of transmission system in the event of major failures.

The Department transmitted 8,010 Million Units (MU) of energy during
2007-08 which increased by 29 per cent to 10,367 MU in 2011-12 while as
the transmission network installed capacity increased from 4,489 Mega
Volts Ampere (MVA) in 2007-08 to 7,273 MVA in 2011-12.

(Paragraph: 2.3.6)
The Department had not formulated the State Electricity Policy and
State Electricity Plan.

(Paragraph: 2.3.7)

Although 85 per cent households were electrified as per Census 2011
figures, the number of households covered by properly functional meters
were far less as evidenced by the huge gap between expenditure on
purchase of power by the Department and its receipts.

(Paragraph: 2.3.7)

The time overrun in completion of 40 projects ranged between three and
48 months and the cost overrun of हैं 113.43 crore was noticed in 23
projects.

(Paragraph: 2.3.9.2)
The maintenance of assets was ignored and the expenditure on
maintenance of the infrastructure was very low.

(Paragraph: 2.3.12)

61


Audit Report on Social, General, Revenue and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs) for the year ended 31 March 2012

The transmission and distribution losses were very high at the level of
56.76 per cent (2011-12). The transmission losses ranged between 4.43
and 8.14 per cent against CEA norms of four per cent.

(Paragraphs: 2.3.13 and 2.3.15)

Due to non-maintenance of Grid discipline, the Department had to bear
the burden of payment of Unscheduled Interchange (UI) charges to the
extent of हें 1,405.67 crore and had also to pay the interest of हैं 289.93
crore for delayed payment of these UI charges.

(Paragraph: 2.3.14.2)

Failure in installing Power Factor (PF) correction capacitor banks by

‘Reactive Energy’ charges.

(Paragraph: 2.3.14.3)

Indecision on the part of the Department for evacuation of power from

resulted in non-utilization of 220 Kilo Volts (KV) double circuit Thein-

crore.

(Paragraph: 2.3.15)

The percentage of shortage of power ranged between 34 and 38 per cent
during the period from 2007-08 to 2011-12.

(Paragraph: 2.3.15)

No Disaster Management setup existed in the State for immediate
restoration of transmission system.

(Paragraph: 2.3.16)

2.3.1 Introduction

With a view to ensure availability of reliable and quality power to all by the
year 2012, the Government of India (GOI) notified the National Electricity
Policy (NEP) in February 2005 which inter-alia stated that the Trans1 ion
System required adequate and timely investment besides efficient and
coordinated action to develop a robust and integrated power system for the
country, The NEP also, recognized the need for continued development of
National Grid for providing adequate infrastructure for inter-state
transmission of power. Both the Central Transmission utility (CTU) and the
State Transmission utility (STU) have the key responsibility of network
planning and development based on the National Electricity Plan in
coordination with all the concerned agencies. In the State of Jammu and
Kashmir, transmission of electricity and Grid operations are managed and
controlled by Jammu and Kashmir Power Development Department (PDD)
which is also responsible for planning and development of the intra-state
transmission system.

62


Chapter-2-Audit Findings an Social, General and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs)

2.3.2 Organizational Structure

The State Power Development Department is headed by the Principal
Secretary and the day to day activities of the Department are carried out by
the Development Commissioner, Power (DCP) who is assisted by two Chief
Engineers (CE), System & Operation (S&O) Wings one each for Jammu and
Kashmir besides, one CE, Planning and Design Wing and one CE,
Commercial and Survey Wing. The S&O Wing is responsible for execution
of various transmission projects and the Planning and Design Wing of the
Department studies and finalizes technical parameters of the projects for their
approval besides, planning of infrastructure and procurement of key material
required for the transmission projects. The Commercial and survey wing
deals with the purchase of power and manages operations of the State Load
Despatch Centres of the Department. The four CEs are assisted by seven
Superintending Engineers (SEs), who in tum are assisted by the 19 Executive
Engineers acting as divisional heads

2.3.3. Audit Objectives

The objectives of the performance audit were to assess whether:

e Perspective Plan was prepared in accordance with the guidelines of the
National Electricity Policy/Plan and State Electricity Regulatory
Commission (SERC);

e The transmission system was developed, commissioned and maintained
in an economical, efficient and effective manner;

e Disaster Management System was set up to safeguard its operations
against unforeseen disruptions;

e Effective failure analysis system was set up;
e Effective and efficient Financial Management system existed;

e Efficient and effective system of procurement of material and inventory
control mechanism existed; and

e <A monitoring system was in place to review functioning of the
transmission projects and to take corrective measures wherever
deficiencies existed.

2.3.4 Audit Criteria

The audit criteria were derived from the following sources for achievement
of the audit objectives:

e Provisions of National Electricity Policy and Plan;

© Perspective Plan and Project Reports of the Department;

e Standard procedures for award of contracts with reference to principles of
economy, efficiency, effectiveness, equity and ethics;

e Manual of Transmission Planning Criteria (MTPC);
¢ Code of Technical Interface (CTI)/Grid Code;

63


Audit Report on Social, General, Revenue and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs) for the year ended 31 March 2012

¢ Instructions/directions of the State Government/Ministry of Power (GOI);

e Norms/Guidelines issued by State Electricity Regulatory Commission
(SERC)/Central Electricity Authority (CEA);

¢ Report of the Committee constituted by the Ministry of Power
recommending the ‘Best Practices in Transmission’; and

e Report of the Task force constituted by the Ministry of Power to analyze
critical elements in transmission project implementation.

2.3.5 Scope and methodology of Audit

The Performance Audit of Power transmission utilities in the State for the
period 2007-08 to 2011-12 was conducted during April to July 2012 by test-
check of records of Chief Engineer, System and Operation Wing one each at
Jammu and Srinagar, Chief Engineer, Design and Planning Wing and of 16!
out of 19 utility Divisions of the Department, based on random sampling
technique without replacement.

The methodology adopted consisted of explaining audit objectives and
criteria to top management through an entry conference held on 17 April
2012 with Principal Secretary, Power Development Department. The exit
conference was also held on 12 December 2012 and the views and their
replies were considered in the finalization of this Performance Audit Report.

AUDIT FINDINGS

2.3.6 Power Transmission infrastructure

The transmission of electricity is defined as bulk transfer of power over long
distances at high voltages, generally at 132 Kilo Volts (KV) and above. The
electric power generated at relatively low voltages in power plants is stepped
up to high voltage power before it is transmitted to reduce the loss in
transmission and to increase efficiency in the Grid. Sub-stations (SSs) are
facilities within the high voltage electric system used for stepping-
up/stepping down voltages from one level to another, connecting electric
systems and switching equipment in and out of the system. The step up
transmission SSs at the generating stations use transformers to increase the
voltages for transmission over long distances. Transmission lines carry high
voltage electric power and the step down transmission SSs_ thereafter
decreases voltages to sub transmission voltage levels [66/33/11 Kilo Volts
Ampere (KVA)] for distribution to consumers. The distribution system
includes lines, poles, transformers and other equipment needed to deliver
electricity at specific voltages. Electrical energy cannot be stored; hence
generation of power must be matched to the requirement. Therefore, every
transmission system requires a sophisticated system of control called Grid
management to ensure balancing of power generation closely with demand.
A pictorial representation of the transmission process is given in Chart-1
below:

TLMD-I, Bemina, TLMD-II, Pampore, TLMD-III, Sopore, TLMD-IV, Pampore, TLCD-I,
Bemina, TLCD-Il, Bemina, Grid Construction Division, Srinagar, TLMD-I, Janipur, TLMD-
वा, Gladni, TLMD-II, Udhampur, TLCD-I, Panama Chowk, TLCD-IL, Panama Chowk, Grid

As far as the transmission capacity of the Department is concerned, it
transmitted 8,009.77 Million Units (MU) of energy during 2007-08 which
increased to 10,366.61 MU in 2011-12, i-e., an increase of 29 per cent. The
transmission network of the Department at the beginning of the year 2007-08
consisted of 37 Extra High Tension (EHT) Sub-stations (SSs) with a
transmission capacity of 4,488.64 Mega Volts Ampere (MVA) and 1,879.823
Circuit Kilometer (CKM) of EHT transmission lines. The Department had a
transmission network of 2,516.628 CKM and 54 Sub-stations (SSs) with
installed capacity of 7,273 MVA and capable of transmitting 40,633.32 MUs
annually at 220/132 KV levels as of March 2012. Audit noticed that the
Department erected 18 Grid Sub-Stations (capacity: 2,220 MVA),
10 Transmission lines (265.96 CKM) and augmented existing transformation
capacity by 150 MVA and 230 MVA at 220/132 and 132/33 KV voltage
levels respectively during the review period. In addition, 10 Grid sub-stations
(capacity: 820 MVA), 16 Transmission lines (682.157 CKM) and
augmentation of existing transformation capacity by 120 MVA taken up
during the review period were still incomplete as of March 2012.

2.3.7. Planning and Development

The Central Transmission Utility (CTU) and State Transmission Utility
(STU) have the key responsibility of network planning and development
based on the National Electricity Plan (NEP) in coordination with all
concerned agencies. The Department is responsible for planning and
development of the intra-state transmission system. The assessment of
demand of power is an important pre-requisite for planning ity addition.
The Jammu and Kashmir Electricity Act, 2010 provides for formulation of
State Electricity Policy for development of Power system in the State based
on optimal utilization of resources. The Government was also to prepare the
State Electricity Plan in accordance with the State Electricity Policy and
notify such plan once in five years.

65


Audit Report on Social, General, Revenue and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs) for the year ended 31 March 2012

Audit observed (July 2012) that no State Electricity Policy and State
Electricity Plan had been formulated by the Government as of June 2012.
Non formulation of the Electricity plan for the State led to execution of
works in a standalone mode without taking into account the assessment of
demand and requirements of capacity additions.

Although 85.14 per cent households were electrified as per Census 2011
figures, the number of houscholds covered by properly functional meters was
far less as evidenced by the huge gap between expenditure on purchase of
Power by the Department and its receipts as per details given in Table 2.3.1
below:

The poor execution of transmission projects by the Department was the basic
reason for non-achievement of capacity addition targets. No preparatory
work in advance was taken for survey, design, processing of forest/land and
statutory clearances and tendering activities etc. Resultantly, the completion
of projects got delayed inordinately and there was huge time/cost overrun as

indicated in the succeeding paragraphs 9.1 to 9.5 under sub-head ‘Project
management of transmission network’.

2.3.9 Project management of transmission network

A transmission project involves various activities from concept to
commissioning stage through Project formulation, appraisal/approval phase
and Project execution phase. For reduction in project implementation period,
the Ministry of Power, Government of India (GOI) constituted (February
2005) a Task Force on transmission projects to suggest and recommend
measures to curtail delays in project implementation so that the projects are
completed on schedule and chances of time-over-run and cost-over-run are
eradicated. The Task Force recommended (August 2005) the following
remedial actions:

* to undertake various preparatory activities such as surveys, design &
testing, processing for forest & other statutory clearances, tendering
activities etc. in advance/parallel to project appraisal and approval phase
and go ahead with construction activities once transmission line project
sanction/approval is received;

e to break-down the transmission projects into clearly defined packages
such that the packages can be procured & implemented requiring least
coordination & interfacing and at same time it attracts competition
facilitating cost effective procurement; and

standardize designs prior to floating of tenders for tower fabrication so
that 6-12 months or more time can be saved in project execution.

Though the implementation of transmission projects upto the year 2003
continued mostly through State Plan, the power sector got boost with the
announcement of Prime Minister's Reconstruction Plan (PMRP) in
November 2004 for the State. Audit scrutiny of records (April 2012) of the
Chief Engineer, S&O Wing revealed that for development of transmission
infrastructure in the State a project estimated to cost of = 707 crore was
formulated (2003) by the State Government and sent to the Ministry of
Power, Government of India (501) for approval under 10" five year Plan
period (2002-07). The cost of the project involving 66 works which included
construction of 24 Grid sub-stations (GSS) laying of 37 Transmission lines
(TLs) and augmentation of the existing five sub-stations was revised to
= 1,006.46 crore in September 2004 after its scrutiny by Central Electricity
Authority (CEA). The project cost was further revised (2006) to हैं 1,351
crore due to substantial increase in cost of basic material viz. steel, cement
and transmission equipments. Further, 36 schemes/works of the project
(GSS: 17, TL: 19) were executed on turnkey basis through contractors and 30
schemes/works (GSS: seven, TL: 18, Augmentation works: five) were
executed departmentally.

Audit further observed that though the funds under the PMRP started flowing
in March 2005, the tendering activities in respect of turnkey projects were
commenced after a delay of two years and the letters of award for contracts
were issued from December 2007 onwards by the Department. The
consultancy for the project under PMRP was sought from CEA in January
2005 but the Memorandum of Understanding (MOU) between the

68


Chapter-2-Audit Findings an Social, General and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs)

Department and the CEA for this purpose was signed in July 2006 after
considerable delay. This consequently resulted in delay in finalization of
technical specifications for different equipments/material and _ tender
documents for the project. Thus the Department was not fully geared up to
take up construction of transmission projects in a big way which adversely
impacted the reconstruction plan for development of transmission
infrastructure as brought out in the subsequent paragraphs:

2.3.9.1 Delay in finalization of contracts

The execution of civil works of the projected Grid SSs and transmission lines
under PMRP were started from March 2005 departmentally whereas, the
contracts for execution of works on turnkey basis were awarded after delays
ranging between 21 and 64 months as per Appendix 2.2, due to delay in
signing (July 2006) of Memorandum of Understanding (MOU) with the CEA
and late finalization (January 2007) of technical specifications. This resulted
in revision of cost of the project in the year 2006 before taking up the
projects. The cost escalation of the project by % 287.15 crore was due to
substantial increase in costs of key input material/equipment and labour.

The Chief Engineers, S&O Kashmir as well as Jammu stated (April/July
2012) that initially the works were taken up for execution departmentally.
However, on the advice of the consultant (CEA), the works were allotted on
turnkey basis to various contractors. The whole process consumed substantial
time and resulted in non-completion of schemes as well as shortfall in
achievement of targets. The reply, however, was not tenable as the
department had initiated the proposal for execution of most of these works in
the year 2003 and sufficient ground work was to be carried out to ensure
timely completion of the project.

2.3.9.2 Inordinate delay in completion of transmission infrastructure

Notwithstanding the recommendations of the Task Force for timely
completion of the projects, and further stipulations in the contracts allotted
on turnkey basis for completion of a project within 18 months from the date
of award of contract, the department failed to complete construction of Sub-
stations and Transmission lines within the stipulated period. The time
overrun in completion of 40 projects including Grid sub-stations,
transmission lines, augmentation and other minor works ranged between
three and 48 months is given in Appendix 2.3. Further the cost overrun of
% 113.43 crore was noticed in 23 out of 40 completed projects.

In addition to these 26 projects were incomplete (March 2012) despite time
overrun ranging between 20 and 48 months. Due to delay in completion, the
Department proposed (February/March 2012) another revision in respect of
17 incomplete major projects with cost overrun of € 154.83 crore against the
already revised cost of 559.39 crore in the year 2006. Audit noticed that the
delay in execution of the schemes/works was caused mainly due to Right of
Way (ROW) problems, changes in structural designs caused because of
changed site conditions encountered during execution, litigation problems
due to faulty evaluation of offer bids, non-payment of timely compensation
for land/trees etc. falling within the alignment of sub stations/transmission
lines and awaited clearances from the Forest Department. This indicated that

69


Audit Report on Social, General, Revenue and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs) for the year ended 31 March 2012

the department had failed to take preparatory steps like conducting of
detailed surveys, soil testing and obtaining of statutory clearances which
resulted in delayed and partial execution of transmission infrastructure
besides idle investments.

2.3.9.3 Idle investment

As per the recommendations of the Committee for updating the best practices
in transmission system in the country, project feasibility report is to be
prepared before taking up any project so that the necessary statutory
obligations like issue of notifications as per Electricity Act, forest clearance,
PTCC (Power Telecom Coordination Committee) clearances, etc. are
completed. This project feasibility report consists of details of transmission
system, justification, scope of the project, technical parameters, estimated
cost of the project, details pertaining to project management, environmental
impact, project completion schedule, tariff etc.

Audit observed (March/July 2012) that eight projects targeted to be
completed within 18 months from the date of commencement of the contract,
had not been completed (March 2012) due to Right of Way (ROW) problems
as depicted in Table 2.3.3 below:

simultaneously.

been rendered sub-judice due to failure of the Department to ensure proper

evaluation of bids.

stage. A detailed survey was as such required to be conducted before floating

of tenders

2.3.11 Performance of transmission system

The performance of the Transmission utility depends upon efficient
maintenance of Extra High Voltage Transmission (EHT) network for supply
of quality power with minimum outages. During the course of operation of
network, the supply-demand profile within the constituent sub-systems is
identified and system improvement schemes are planned and undertaken to
reduce line losses and ensure reliability of power by improving voltage
profile. These System Improvement schemes are for augmentation of
existing transformer capacity, installation of additional transformers, laying
of additional lines and installation of capacitor banks. The performance of
the Department with regard to operation and maintenance (O&M) of the
system is discussed in the subsequent paragraphs:

2.3.11.1 Transmission capacity

To evacuate power from the generating stations and to meet the load growth
in different areas of the State, the Department was required to construct
Transmission lines and Sub-stations at different EHT voltage levels. The
evacuation is normally done at 220 KV Sub-stations. The details of
transmission capacity (220 KV) created by the Department vis-a-vis the
transmitted capacity (peak demand) at the end of each year during the last
five years period ended 31March 2012 are given in Table 2.3.8 bellow:

Table-2.3.8
‘Transmission capacity (in MVA)
Year | Installed | After leaving 30 Peak demand Excess
per cent towards Transmission
margin/redundanc capacit

2007-08 1,860 1,302 1,214 [gs
2008-09 | 2,340 1,638 1,422 216
2009-10 | 2,490 1,743 1,503 | 240 |
2010-11 2,490 1,743 1,630 113
2011-12 | 3,570 2,499 1,795 704

From the above table, it could be seen that the overall transmission capacity
in the State was in excess of the requirement during the period from 2007-08
to 2011-12. The transmission capacity after excluding 30 per cent towards
redundancy worked out to 2,499 MVA excess by 704 MVA as of March
2012. Despite excess transmission capacity in the State, it was noticed during
test-check that four Grid sub-stations remained over loaded above their
installed capacity ranging between 12 MVA and 35 MVA. The existence of
idle capacity in the transmission network and prevalence of overloads on
certain Grid sub-stations indicated unscientific planning in creation of
transmission network.

2.3.11.2 Adequacy of Sub-stations

Manual on Transmission Planning Criteria (MTPC) stipulates the permissible
maximum capacity of 320 MVA for 220 KV Grid sub-stations and 150 MVA.
for 132 KV Grid sub-stations.

supply 80 per cent of the load. Audit noticed that twelve’ out of 44 Grid sub-

alternative available with the Department to supply uninterrupted power in
the event of outage of any transformer in these sub-stations indicating lack of
backup planning at transmission network level.

transformers was not financially viable as the actual load at these Sub-

any details of single-transformer Grid sub-stations where load was much
below the installed capacity. The load-capacity gap or financial constraints

2.3.11.3 Voltage management

According to Jammu and Kashmir Electricity Grid code, the utility is
required to maintain Grid voltage of 200-245 KV and 120-145 KV at bus
bars of the Grid sub-station ranging between 360 KV and 420 KV and 400
KV transmission lines between 220 KV and 132 KV transmission lines
respectively.

A test-check of records revealed that out of ten 220/132 KV Grid sub stations
in the State, the minimum bus voltage recorded at Gladni Grid sub-station
was 175 KV below the minimum required voltage limit of 200 KV.
Similarly, 28 Grid sub-stations out of 44 Grid sub-stations (132/33 KV), had
the minimum bus voltage below the required minimum level which ranged
between 96 and 119. This indicated that the grid voltages had not been
maintained as per the prescribed norms by the Department.

2.3.11.4 Extra High Voltage Transmission Lines (EHT)

As per Manual of Transmission Planning Criteria (MTPC), permissible line
loading cannot normally be more than the Thermal Loading Limit (TLL).
These limits restrict sag and loss of tensile strength of the transmission lines
and limit the maximum power flow of the lines. As per MTPC, the TLL of
132 KV line with Aluminum Conductor Steel Reinforced (ACSR) Panther
210 sq.mm conductor should be 366 amperes (amps). A test-check of records
revealed that the thermal loading in respect of 84 outgoing feeders (33KV
voltage level) of 19 Grid sub-stations had exceeded the thermal loading limit
of 366 amps which ranged between 370 amps to 2,210 amps. The loading of
the lines beyond the prescribed limits resulted in voltage fluctuations, higher
transmission losses and frequent interruptions/breakdowns.

2.3.11.5 Bus Bar Protection Panel (BBPP)

Bus bar is used as an application for interconnection of the incoming and
outgoing transmission lines and transformers at an electrical Sub-station. The
Bus bar protection panel (BBPP) limits the impact of the bus bar faults on the
entire power net-work which prevents unnecessary tripping and selective to
trip only those breakers necessary to clear the bus bar fault. As per Grid
norms and Best Practices in Transmission system, BBPP is to be kept in
service for all 220/132 KV Sub-stations to maintain system stability during
Grid disturbances and to provide faster clearance of faults on 220/132 KV
bus bars.

Test-check of records revealed that out of ten 220/132 KV Sub-stations only
two Grid sub-stations at Budgam and Bishnah were provided with BBPP.
The position with regard to the protection given to 44 Sub-stations at voltage
level of 132/33 KV was more dismal because only two* Grid sub-stations
had been provided the protection panel whereas one® Sub-station was not in

Rawalpora and Bari-Brahmna-IL
Bari-Brahmna-II

77

working condition. The position reflected inefficiency of the Department

towards safety of the installed equipment.

2.3.11.6 Performance of Power transformers (PT) and Current
transformers (CT)

The Power and current transformers are most important and cost-intensive
components of the transmission network. These transformers go through
natural ageing under operational stress conditions as well as accelerated
ageing due to contamination and abnormal service conditions and being a
critical link in power systems it is necessary to continuously monitor and
assess the condition of power transformers to prolong their life duration,
avoid unscheduled outages and reduce their maintenance expenditure. In
order to gather detailed information about the operation conditions of CTs
and PTs, various kinds of oil analysis like the standard oil Dissolved Gas

Anal:

is (DGA), Tan Delta tests are generally conducted. For CT and PT

insulation a combination of an insulating liquid and a solid insulation
impregnated therewith are used. For an evaluation of the actual condition of
this insulating system usually a DGA or Tan Delta test is carried out without
needing a shut down, as failures inside the transformers lead to a degradation
of the liquid insulation in such a way that the compound of the gases enables
an identification of cause of the failure

Audit observed that none of the equipment for carrying out diagnostic tests
and preventive maintenance necessary for evaluating residual life of power
transformers as well as detecting critical defects in the installed yard
equipment at Sub-stations was available with the Department. In absence of
these vital equipments, the
transformers could not be carried out. Audit further noticed that 18 power
and 53 current transformers failed within their normal operational life, as per
details given in Table 2.3.10 below:

2.3.12 Maintenance of Transmission system

The regular and periodic maintenance of transmission system is of utmost
importance for its un-interrupted operation. The Report of the Committee for
updating the Best practices of Transmission in the country, apart from
scheduled patrolling of lines, has prescribed various techniques for
maintenance of lines which include hot line maintenance, washing, puncture
detection of insulators, besides preventive maintenance by using portable
carthling hot line tools, vibration measurement of the line, thermo-scanning
and pollution measurement of the equipment. The hot line technique
envisages attending to maintenance works like hot spots, tightening of nuts
and bolts, damages to the conductor, replacement of insulators etc. of lines
without switching off and includes thermo scanning of all the lines for
preventive maintenance.

Audit observed (April 2012) that no hot line maintenance and washing
practice was adopted by the Department in the State. Further during test-
check, it was observed that the yard equipment viz. Power current
transformers, switch gears, circuit breaches installed at three Grid sub-
stations (Janipur, Udhampur and Gladni) had outlived their lives as these
were installed 25-30 years back. No action had been taken to either replace
these equipments or maintain them properly. The Executive Engineers of
concerned Divisions replied (April to August 2012) that the work of change
of obsolete equipment could not be taken up due to non-accord of Techno-
Economic clearance (TEC) to the projects and non-release of funds by the
Department.

The Chief Engineer, S&O Wing, Kashmir stated (August 2012) that against
three per cent of cost of assets required annually for maintenance of assets
only 0.5 per cent was provided for the purpose. The Chief Engineer, S&O
Wing, Jammu stated (July 2012) that requirement of funds for maintenance
of assets was two per cent of the value of assets per annum and stated that
there was shortage of funds for this purpose. In this connection, Audit
noticed that the funds allocated during the years 2007-08 to 2011-12 for
maintenance of assets were below one per cent of the value of assets as
indicated in Table 2.3.11 below:

wing of the Department for further supply to the consumers. The details of

2.3.14 Grid management

Transmission and Grid management are essential functions for smooth
evacuation of power from generating stations to the Distribution
utilities/consumers. Grid management ensures moment-to-moment power
balance in the interconnected power system to take care of reliability,
security, economy and efficiency of the power system. Grid management in
the State is carried out in accordance with the standards/directions given in
the Grid Code issued by SERC. The State Load Despatch Centre (SLDC), a
constituent of Northern Regional Load Despatch Centre (RLDC), New Delhi,
ensures integrated operation of power system in the State. The SLDC,
Jammu i: sted by Sub Load Despatch Centre situated at Bemina, Srinagar
for data acquisition and transfer to SLDC and supervisory control of 132 KV
and 33 KV equipments. The SLDC levies and collect such fees and charges
from the generating companies and licensees engaged in intra-state
transmission of electricity as specified by the SERC.

2.3.14.1 Infrastructure for load monitoring

Remote Terminal Units (RTUs)/Sub-station Management Systems (SMSs)
are essential for monitoring the efficiency of the transmission system and the
power loads during emergency in load dispatch centers as per the Grid norms
for all SSs. Audit observed (June 2012) that only eleven® Sub-stations (20
per cent) out of 54 Sub-stations were provided with RTUs for recording real
time data for efficient Energy Management System.

2.3.14.2. Grid discipline by frequency management

As per Grid Code, the transmission utilities are required to maintain Grid
discipline for efficient functioning of the Grid. All the constituent members
of the Grid are expected to maintain a system frequency between 49 and 50.5
Hertz (Hz).

For maintenance of Grid discipline, the Central Electricity Regulatory
Commission (CERC) takes up suo-motu petition on over drawal of power
from the Grid at a lower frequency thus putting the Grid to the risk.
According to the CERC, regulation (December 2002) on availability based
tariff (ABT) the State overdrawing/under drawing power than their scheduled
quota have to pay revenue charges at rates depending upon frequency. The
Unscheduled Interchange (UI) charges are payable for deviations from the
schedule and are also subject to the grid conditions at that point of time. The
levying of UI charges is a commercial mechanism to maintain grid
discipline.

Bemina, Gladni, Hiranagar, Habak, Janipur, Miransahib, Pampore (220/132 KV), Pampore
(132/33KV),Udhampur, Wanpoh and Zainkote

Northern Regional Electricity Board (NREB) issues bills of Unscheduled
Inter-change (UI) charges in favour of the Department on weekly basis which
are payable within ten days from the date of their issuance. Non-payment of
Ul charges attracts interest from the due date of payment.

Audit observed (June 2012) that UI charges of € 947.15 crore had been paid
between 2002-03 and 2011-12 and there was further an undischarged liability
of हैं 458.52 crore payable to the NREB. These UI charges included an
interest of हैं 303.19 crore due to delayed payment of these charges. This
indicated that the Department had not ensured efficient grid management
which resulted in payment of huge UI charges. This further revealed that
proper mechanism to enforce Grid discipline by way of frequency
management was not in place in the SLDC.

The Executive Engineer, SLDC, Jammu stated (June 2012) that all efforts
were being made to stick to the allocated share and that in compelling
circumstances like technical snag/closure of generating stations under the
control of State Government, the over drawal of power was being made to
fill the gap.

2.3.14.3 Non-installation of capacitors

The power load demand in the State is being met with the availability of
power from self generation share in central sector power projects and power
purchased from the Northern Grid. The State mostly depends upon the
drawal of power from Northern Grid and due to excessive drawal of reactive
power the system voltage is substantially low. Reactive power is a concept to
describe the background energy movement in an alternating current system
arising from production of electric and magnetic fields. These fields store
energy which changes through each AC cycle. Devices which store energy
by virtue of electric fields are said to generate reactive power such as, electric
motors, welding sets, etc. The dissipation of power due to such devices
reduces power factor (ratio of real power to apparent power) and has to be
made up by way of devices which store energy by virtue of magnetic field
and are said to absorb reactive power such as capacitors. Consumers with a
power factor below 96 per cent are assessed with reactive power measured in
Kilo Volt Ampere Reactive (KVAR) charge. In order to improve the power
factor and the system voltage profile and also to reduce the drawal of reactive
current from the Northern Grid, all State Electricity Boards were asked to
install shunt capacitors in their respective networks.

The position of requirement and availability of shunt capacitors during the
period 2007-08 to 2011-12 is given in Table 2.3.13 below:
