Chapter-2-Audit Findings an Social, General and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs)

Authority (CEA). The unmetered supply of electricity was one of the major
factors responsible for such a high power loss. Due to the gap in
infrastructure vis-a-vis energy accounting, the peak demand of the State gets
unrestricted and is presently projected as 2,500 MW which is restricted to
1,750 MW after load curtailments. The ratio of connected load and peak
demand, known as Diversity Factor is always greater than one. Against the
restricted peak demand of 1,750 MW, the registered connected load was
1,708.16 MW which resulted in under recovery of revenue and overload of
transformers leading to large scale transmission failures and disruption of
power supply. This indicated that the procurement of power had not been
planned properly on the basis of realistic inputs from the distribution sector
of the Department.

The State Government had entered into an agreement (January 1979) with
Punjab State Government for sharing power and irrigation water share of
Ranjit Sagar Dam (Thein dam) project on river Ravi. The said agreement was
signed by the then Chief Ministers of the two States. The Thein dam houses
four generating units of 150 MVA each and has a total generating capacity of
600 MVA. The agreement between the two States provided that the State
Government would get share of 20 per cent in the total power generated at
Thien dam by the Punjab Government at the cost of generation at bus bars
and that in case the State Government was unable to utilize the aforesaid
share of power within its territory or was desirous of selling the same, the
surplus power would be sold to Punjab Government at mutually agreed rate.

To evacuate share of power from Thein Dam hydro electric project, the
Department took up construction of 220 KV double circuit, Thein- Hiranagar
transmission line during the period 1992-95. The single circuit of the line
was completed in the year 2005 at a cost of € 20.45 crore. The balance work
for second circuit of the transmission line was got approved under PMRP at a
cost of € 5.71 crore in the year 2006 and the work of 40.35 kms out of 41.54
km of projected length was completed (March 2010) at a cost of € 5.28 crore.
The Punjab Government also agreed to connect this transmission line to
Shahpur Kandi switchyard subject to the condition that the State Government
would bear the cost (% 4.5 crore) of two number 220 KV bays to be utilized
for evacuation of power share from the Thein dam. The Punjab Government
had been insisting that cost of bays be borne by the State Government since
the year 1996 onwards. The stalemate continued and the dispute could not be
settled in-spite of interventions by the CEA and Northern Zonal Council
during 2003-04. Due to failure of the State Government to provide हैं 4.50
crore on account of cost of two 220 KV bays, the State could not draw its
power share from the Thein dam.

Thus, inaction of the department to take decision for evacuation of power
from the Thein dam hydro electric power project for the last three decades
resulted in non utilization of 220 KV double circuit Thein-Hiranagar
transmission line and consequent idle investment of हैं 25.73 crore. The
Government, in reply to audit query forwarded (August 2012) a detailed
background note on Ranjit Sagar Dam wherein the high cost of power tariff
by the Punjab State Electricity Board was attributed to loading of the cost of
execution of irrigation component which was not acceptable to the State

85


Audit Report on Social, General, Revenue and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs) for the year ended 31 March 2012

Government and was not supposed to pay for cost of irrigation of the State of
Punjab.

The fact, however, remained that the Department did not pursue the matter
through appropriate channels and appropriate levels. The Department also
failed to provide a rigorous cost-benefit analysis to conclusively establish
that the acceptance of the deviations in the agreement sought by the Punjab
Government is not in the interest of the Government of Jammu and Kashmir,
considering the overall cost of power procured by the State from alternative
sources.

2.3.16 Disaster Management

Disaster Management (DM) aims at mitigating the impact of a major break
down on the system and restoring it in the shortest possible time. As per the
recommendations of the Committee for updating the Best Practices in
Transmission System, DM system should be set up by all power utilities for
immediate restoration of transmission system in the event of major failures.
The disaster management is carried out by deploying Emergency Restoration
System (ERS), Diesel Generating (DG) sets, skilled and specialized
manpower. The DG sets and synchroscopes'” form part of Disaster
management facilities at EHT sub-stations connecting major generating
stations. Audit noticed that no disaster management setup existed in the State
for immediate restoration of transmission system in the event of any major
failure.

A test-check (April 2012) of records further revealed that out of 54 Grid sub-
stations DG sets were available only in 37 (68 per cent) sub-stations. Further,
snychroscopes were provided in five’! Sub-stations out of which one’? was
non-functional.

The Chief Engineers, S&O wings, Kashmir as well as Jammu admitted
(April/July 2012) that the Disaster management cell had not been setup due
to paucity of funds and that no emergency restoration systems were available
with the Department.

Authority (CEA), late submission of drawings, price variations, revision of
projects due to changes in drawings and site conditions, revision of bill of
quantities and ROW issues. The under utilization of funds adversely affected
the completion of transmission works both under PMRP and T&D State Plan
and resulted in denial of intended benefits to the population of the State.

The Chief Engineers, S&O Wings Kashmir as well as Jammu accepted
(August 2012) the audit contention of under utilization of funds. This
indicated the fact that the Department was not fully geared up for execution
of the projects for which funds were demanded.

2.3.17.1 Non-recovery of liquidated damages

In order to provide quality power to the beneficiaries, the Power
Development Department took up various schemes of construction of
220/132 KV Grid Sub-stations and Transmission lines under PMRP.
Although the works were initially taken up for execution Departmentally,
they were subsequently awarded to the contractors on turn-key basis for
speedy completion. The works were to be executed and the supplies were to
be made within 18 months of the date of issue of Letter of Award (LOA)
According to conditions governing the execution of works on turn-key basis
in case of the failure of the contractor to complete the work within 18
months, a sum equivalent to one per cent of the contract value per week of
delay or part thereof subject to a maximum of 10 per cent of the contract
price was to be recovered from the contractor as liquidated damage. Audit
noticed that the departmental authorities failed to recover liquidated damages
to the extent of हैं 16.48 crore for delayed execution of seven works as
detailed in Appendix 2.4.

87


Audit Report on Social, General, Revenue and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs) for the year ended 31 March 2012

The Executive Engineer, TLCD-II, Srinagar stated (June 2012) that in
respect of two works liquidated damages of € 2.22 crore had been recovered
from the concerned contractor out of liquidated damages of % 2.90 crore.
The fact, however, remained that the amount of liquated damages of % 14.26
crore remained unrecovered

2.3.17.2 Avoidable expenditure

As per the Centrally Electricity Regulatory Commission (terms and
conditions of tariff) Regulation Notification of March 2001 as amended from
January 2008, a rebate of two per cent is allowable for payment of bills of
capacity charges and energy charges through letter of credit (LC). However,
in case the payments are made by a mode other than through the letter of
credit but within a period of one month of presentation of bills by the
generating company, a rebate of one per cent is allowable. Further, in case
the bills are delayed by the beneficiaries beyond a period of one month from
the date of billing, a late payment surcharge at the rate of 1.5 per cent per
month shall be levied by the generating company.

Audit scrutiny (June 2012) of records revealed that despite the fact that State,
being dependent on procurement of power from outside sources, the
Department had not taken benefit of this facility. The system of payment of
power bills by operation of LC though started during the year 2008-09 had
not been adopted by the Department. However as per the procedure in vogue
the bills raised by the power supplier are received by the Executive Engineer,
Load Dispatch and Meter Testing (LD&MT) Division, Jammu and after
verification are forwarded to the Executive Engineer, Commercial Survey
Division, Jammu for onward transmission to the Administrative Department
(Civil Secretariat) and to the State Finance Department for financial
provisions. After passing through all these stages, the bills are then returned
back to the Executive Engineer, LD&MT Division, Jammu for to payment.
The Department had not thus availed the rebate of two per cent for release of
payments though LC. Even the benefit of payment of power bills within one
month for availing one per cent rebate was also not taken by the Department
to the full extent. Audit further noticed that during the period 2007-08 to
2011-12 the Department had availed of rebate of हैं 22.55 crore only against
the allowable rebate of € 190.64 crore resulting in avoidable payment of
र₹ 168.07 crore. Further, due to delayed payment of power bills = 45.90 crore
had been paid by the Department as late payment surcharge during the period
from 2007-08 to 2011-12 resulting in further extra avoidable expenditure to
that extent.

The Executive Engineer, SLDC stated (June 2012) that prior to March 2011
sanctions for opening of LCs for payment to various CPSUs/Power
supplying agencies were accorded and from April 2011 the funds were
placed at the disposal of the Division which was later dispensed with by the
Government. However, the reasons for dispensing with the practice of
payment through LC were not furnished to audit.

88


Chapter-2-Audit Findings an Social, General and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs)

2.3.18 Stores Management

Rule 8.4 of the Jammu and Kashmir Financial code envisaged that stores
should not be held in excess of requirements. Each utility should assess the
requirement of stores for the year and after fulfillment of all the codal
formalities like invitation of tenders, ascertaining reasonability of lowest
market rates, make purchases to ensure procurement of quality material and
economy in Government expenditure.

Audit observed that stores were purchased in excess of the requirements and
in nine’ utility divisions. The surplus stores like tower material, copper
control cables of different core, ACSR conductors, MS Channel/Joints, LA
Sets of different specifications, suspension fittings etc. valued at हैं 11.18
crore were lying in the stores of respective divisions for the last five years,
which tantamount to blockade of Government funds. The prolonged storage
of the material had the chances of deterioration in quality. On being pointed
out in Audit, the Executive Engineers stated that the material as pointed out
by Audit shall be utilized for in hand/other works pertaining to sister
divisions.

2.3.18.1 Non-disposable of unserviceable store/stock

A periodical inspection should be conducted by the Head of Transmission
utility, who must submit a report of surplus, unserviceable and obsolete
stores to the competent authority for issue of orders towards their disposal.

Audit scrutiny of records (April/July 2012) in respect of seven’* utility
Divisions revealed that unserviceable stores consisting of power
transformers, diesel generators, CTs/PTs, insulators, Iron scrap etc. valuing
% 1.27 crore was lying in the stores for a long period of time. The Executive
Engineers of the concerned Utility Divisions accepted (June 2012) the audit
observation.

2.3.18.2 Idling of power transformers

A 15 MVA (GEC Make) Power Transformer was spared (May 2006) at Grid
station Miran Sahib, Jammu and transported for installation at 132/33 Grid
station Jhajjar- Kotli on 10° June 2006. The Sub-station was, however,
provided 31.5 MVA Transformer in May 2009 with the result the 15 MVA
transformer was spared. It was seen that this transformer (15 MVA) had been
kept idle and in uncharged condition for the last three years (July 2012).
Since the transformer was lying in open, the physical condition of the
equipment got detoriated and the manufacturers were approached for over-
hauling/up-rating/re-conditioning of the transformer.

Similarly, 20 MVA capacity power transformer was spared (June 2011) from
Grid station Akhnoor and transported to Grid station Kalakote and was lying

uncharged at that place thereafter. Further no action had been taken by the

admitting (December 2012) the audit contention stated that both transformers
at Grid Sub-Station Jhajjarkotli and Grid sub-station, Kalakote were lying
idle for want of decision to be taken by the Department

2.3.19 Manpower Management

The power utility was required to devise different modules of training for
their staff to update their knowledge based on the latest development in the
respective fields. The Department was also required to have a well defined
HRD policy and systems. Based on requisitions, inputs from various
transmission utility divisions, the staff particularly the technicians and Junior
Engineers entrusted with manning of substations and EHV transmission lines
were required to be got equipped with adequate skills to ensure smooth and
safe operation and maintenance of transmission system of the Department.

Audit observed (April/July 2012) that a strength of 485 technicians was
required in four utility divisions of Jammu region for operationalising and
maintaining 18 Grid sub-stations, against which only 350 technicians were in
position and there was a shortage of 135 (28 per cent) technicians for
manning these Grid sub-stations. Similarly, there was an overall shortage of
technical staff to the extent of 18 per cent in Kashmir region. Audit further
noticed that no trainings were found imparted to the technical staff during the
period 2007-08 to 2011-12. Thus, the manpower management was below par
due to shortage of technicians required for manning transmission
infrastructure and also because of not imparting training to the technicians.

The Chief Engineers, S&O Wing, Kashmir as well as Jammu admitted
(July/September 2012) that no system of trainings and up gradation of skills
existed in the Department because of non availability of funds and
acknowledged the need for adopting the system on the lines of Power Grid
Corporation India Limited (PGCIL) and National Hydro-Electric Power
Corporation (NHPC).

2.3.20 Monitoring and evaluation

The performance of the Sub-stations and transmission lines of 400/220/132
KV on various parameters like maximum and minimum voltage levels,
breakdowns, voltage profiles should be recorded/maintained, as per the Grid
code standards.

Audit observed (July 2012) that the cumulative performance of the Sub-
stations and Transmission lines were neither being maintained nor
consolidated for evaluation of annual performance of the Sub-stations and
Transmission lines. However, the Utility Divisions recorded some
performance data in the log books of respective sub-stations, but no MIS
reports indicating performance of the units as well as equipment installed
were prepared in these Divisions.

The Chief Engineers, S&O Wing, Kashmir as well as Jammu admitted
Guly/August 2012) that year wise cumulative performance of Sub-stations

90


Chapter-2-Audit Findings an Social, General and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs)

and transmission lines is judged from voltage level, flow of current, number
of outages recorded in the log book of the Grid station. This indicated that no
effective monitoring and control system existed in the Department.

2.3.21 Conclusion

The State had not been able to take full advantage of the assistance given by
the Government of India as it had not been able to implement the projects in
a time bound manner. Plan funds to the extent of 49 per cent only were
utilized by the Department during the period 2007-08 to 2011-12. A State
Electricity Plan and State Electricity Policy, as provided in the Jammu and
Kashmir Electricity Act, 2010, to give shape to the future requirements in the
Transmission Sector and to provide a roadmap for development of Power
system in the State, had not been formulated despite lapse of about two years.
The Department had failed to achieve the planned targets during 2007-12 and
almost all the Grid sub-station and Transmission line projects started under
Prime Minister’s Reconstruction Plan (PMRP) had witnessed a time overrun.
Some of the completed projects could not be operationalised due to
incompletion of other related works with the result the infrastructure created
was lying idle. The projects had suffered due to unplanned project
formulations, execution of projects on adhoc basis and execution of projects
without availability of essential pre-planning stage components like land,
Right of Way, well formulated structural designs, site conditions, sound bid
evaluation, etc. The transmission losses exceeded the Central Electricity
Authority (CEA) norm of four per cent and ranged between 5.07 and 8.14
per cent during the period 2008-09 to 2011-12. In-efficient Grid
management had led to payment of Unscheduled Interchage (UD) charges to
the Northern Regional Electricity Board (NREB). The Department failed to
install Power Factor (PF) correction capacitor banks resulted in payment of &
220.65 crore on account of ‘Reactive Energy’ charges during the period
2002-03 to 2011-12. Against requirement of = 160.65 crore for maintenance
of transmission assets, € 36.33 crore was provided during the period 2007-08
to 2011-12 indicating ignorance of the Department in the maintenance of the
assets. The Department did not setup Disaster Management system in all the
power utilities in the State due to paucity of funds.

2.3.22 Recommendations
The Department may consider to ensure:

© efficient implementation of the Electricity Plan/ Policy as laid down in
the Jammu and Kashmir Electricity Act, 2010 for planned development
of power system in the State;

e speedy achievement of the planned targets in capacity additions in
respect of Grid sub-stations and Transmission lines;

e taking full advantage of the assistance of the GOI by ensuring speedy
execution of in-hand projects;

91


Audit Report on Social, General, Revenue and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs) for the year ended 31 March 2012

e close monitoring of the execution of Transmission projects so as to
ensure that execution is not stalled due to Right of Way (ROW)
problems;

© taking effective steps for speedy resolution of encountered problems so
that projects could be completed on schedule to check time and cost
overrun;

e restricting the transmission losses within the Centeral Electricity
Authority (CEA) norms of four per cent;

° efficient grid management to avoid penalty in the form of Unscheduled
Interchange (UI) charges. Power Factor (PF) correction capacitor
banks should be installed to avoid payment ‘Reactive Energy’ charges;

° replacing age old and obsolete equipment installed at various Grid
stations in a phased manner for ensuring system security and
reliability;

© proper maintenance of Yard Equipment so as to avoid expenditure on
occasional tests arranged through Power Grid Corporation India
Limited (PGCIL);

¢ taking expeditious measures to complete the 220KV double circuit
Thein - Hiranagar transmission line project for power evacuation
from Thein dam; and

° to setup a well equipped Disaster Management system.

B. Thematic Audit
LABOUR AND EMPLOYMENT DEPARTMENT

24 Functioning of Jammu and Kashmir Building and Other
Construction Workers Welfare Board

241 Introduction

Government of India (GOI) enacted (August 1996)' the Building and Other
Construction Workers (Regulation of Employment and Conditions of Service) Act,
1996 (BOCW Act) for constitution of a Welfare Board in each State to undertake
social security schemes”. The GOI also enacted (August 1996)? the Building and
Other Construction Workers Welfare Cess Act, 1996 (Cess Act). Section 14 of the
Cess Act stipulates that rule making power under the Act vests only with the GOI.
The GOI framed (March 1998) the Building and other Construction Workers Welfare
Cess Rules, 1998 (Cess Rules). To augment the resources of the Board, Section 3 of
the Cess Act as amended in September 1996 provided for levy and collection of
labour welfare cess at a uniform rate of one percent of the cost of construction
incurred by the employer. For the safety, health and welfare of the workers, the State
Government in pursuance of sections 40 and 62 of the BOCW Act also framed
(uly 2006) the Jammu and Kashmir Building and Other Construction Workers
(Regulation of Employment and Conditions of Service) Rules, 2006 (J&KBOCW
Rules) as amended in November 2012. The State Government constituted (July 2007)
the Jammu and Kashmir Building and Other Construction Workers Welfare Board
(Board) to carry out welfare schemes for construction workers. The provisions of
BOCW Act are applicable to every establishment which employs, or had employed on
any day of the preceding 12 months, 10 or more workers in any building or other
construction works. All such establishments engaged in construction activity are
liable to pay cess under the Cess Act except construction activity by an employer in
relation to his residence costing less than हैं 10 lakh (excluding the cost of land).

2.4.2 Organisational set-up

To oversee the implementation of the BOCW and Cess Acts, the State Government
had appointed (January 2007) the Labour Commissioner of the State as Chief
Inspector and Appellate Authority, two Deputy Labour Commissioners as Assessing
Officers (one each for Kashmir and Jammu province), 22 Assistant Labour
Commissioners as Registering Officers/ Cess Collectors and 24 Labour Officers as
Inspectors for the whole State within their respective jurisdiction.

The Board is headed by the Hon’ble Minister of Labour and Employment as
Chairman. The other members of the Board are Welfare Commissioner (Hgr.),
Ministry of Labour and Employment, GOI and 15 representatives, five each from the
State Government, employers and workers. The Secretary of the Board was an officer
of State Government of the rank of Deputy Secretary (October 2012).

2.4.3 Audit Objectives

The objectives of audit of the Board were to assess whether:

assistance for marriages, personal accident insurance scheme, ete.

. Planning process for implementation of welfare measures was effective;
. Fund management was efficient and effective;

. Monitoring and Internal Control Mechanism was in place and effective
2.4.4 Scope and Methodology of Audit

Section 27 of the BOCW Act read with Section 19 (2) of the CAG’s (Duties, Powers
and Conditions of Services) Act, 1971 provide for audit of accounts of the Board by
the Comptroller and Auditor General of India. The records maintained in the office of
the Board and six* out of 22 Assistant Labour Commissioners for the period from
February 2011 to March 2012 were test-checked in audit during March-April 2012.
The reply of the Secretary/ Chief Executive Officer (CEO) of the Board has been
incorporated at appropriate places in the report.

Audit Findings
2.4.5 Planning for implementation of Act
2.4.5.1 Delay in formation of Jammu and Kashmir Building and Other

Construction Workers Welfare Board

It was noticed that the implementation of the BOCW Act, 1996 was not ensured
expeditiously by the State Government as the Board was constituted (July 2007) after
a delay of 11 years from the date of enactment of BOCW Act and reconstituted in
December 2010. The Board actually started collecting the cess under the Act from
November 2008 and started incurring expenditure only from February 2011. The
delay in constitution and operation of the Board resulted in non-enforcement of
provisions of the Act for the social security and other welfare measures of workers
besides loss of cess to the Board. During test-check of records, Audit noticed non-
recovery of cess of हैं 41.69 lakh from the bills of the contractors by two Executive
Engineers’.

2.4.5.2 Shortfall in conducting meetings of the Board

Schedule for holding meetings by the Board was not prescribed in J&K BOCW Rules,
2006 as required under Section 20 of BOCW (RECS) Act, 1996. The Supreme Court
had also directed (February 2012) that the Board shall meet once in two months. The
rules amended in November 2012, however, provide that the Board shall ordinarily
meet once in two months. It was, however, noticed that the Board had met thrice
(February 2011, July 2011 and February 2012) since its establishment (July 2007)
which resulted in shortfall in conducting meetings and taking policy decisions for the
welfare of the construction workers.

2.4.5.3 Constitution of State Advisory Committee

As per Section 4 of the BOCW Act, 1996 the State Government was to constitute a
State Advisory Committee (SAC) consisting of a Chairperson, two members of the

Srinagar, Bandipora, Baramulla, Jammu, Poonch and Samba

Bandipora: रैं 2.49 lakh,

State Legislature, a member from GOI, Labour Commissioner of the State as Chief
Inspector, seven to eleven nominees of the State Government representing employers,
building workers, etc. to advise the State Government on matters arising out of the
administration of the Act. However, no SAC was constituted by the State
Government. The Secretary/ CEO of the Board stated (August 2012) that the matter
for constitution of State Advisory Committee was taken up (January 2012) with the
Government.

2.4.6 Financial management of the Board

2.4.6.1 Non-preparation of budget estimates resulted in its non-
submission to State Government and Gol

Section 25 of the Act, 1996 provides that the Board shall prepare during each
financial year its budget for the next financial year, showing the estimated receipts
and expenditure of the Board and forward the same to the State Government and GOI.
However, it was noticed that no such Budget had been prepared by the Board from its
inception. The CEO/ Secretary Board stated (August 2012) that steps had been taken
for adoption of Section 25 of the Act for the financial year 2012-13.

2.4.6.2 Resources of the Board

and monthly subscription from the workers were the financial resources (Fund) of the
Board. The fund is required to be applied by the Board for meeting the expenses on
welfare schemes® of workers, salaries, allowances and other remunerations of the
members, officers and other employees and other administrative expenses, etc. of the
Board. The year-wise position of funds received, and expenditure incurred during the

2.4.6.3 Unauthorised expenditure on administrative expenses

As per Section 24(3) of the Act, the Board is authorized to incur administrative
expenditure to the extent of five per cent of the total expenditure during a financial
year. An amount of = 10.21 lakh was incurred towards administrative expenses during
the year 2010-11 despite the fact that no assistance was provided to the beneficiaries
during the year. Further, against admissible administrative expenses of € 18.96 lakh
during the year 2011-12, the Board had incurred हैं 81.97 lakh on this account resulting
in excess expenditure of हैं 63.01 lakh thereby violating the provision of the Act. The
Secretary/ CEO attributed (August 2012) it to non-formulation of scheme and
non-verification of registration of eligible construction workers under the Act and
stated that every effort would be taken to restrict administrative expenditure within
the prescribed ceilings under the Act.

2.4.7 Welfare measures

24.7.1 Implementation of the welfare schemes by the Board

As per the provisions of the BOCW Act, the Board was required to implement
welfare schemes for workers. The Board had in its meetings decided to implement the
welfare schemes regarding medical, disability, death, funeral expenses, maternity
benefits, education assistance for the construction workers.

The Board released an amount of ~ 652.15 lakh to 22 ALCs, on the basis of number
of workers registered with each ALC, for incurring expenditure on the welfare
measures of workers during the year 2011-12 against which the ALCs reported an
expenditure of % 297.25 lakh only during the year for providing assistance to 5105 out
of 57,431 workers registered with the Board. The percentage of utilization of funds by
22 ALCs ranged between 12 and 100. Due to poor utilization of funds the unspent
balance accumulated to = 100.62 crore at the end of March 2012.

It was noticed that 2,400 cases"! amounting to € 85.06 lakh’* were pending with three
ALCs despite = 77.43 lakh remaining un-utilised (March 2012) with these ALCs. The
CEO of the Board stated (August 2012) that seeking of benefit is voluntary in nature
and not mandatory. The reply was not tenable due to delay in constitution of Board
and making it operational after fifteen years from the date of enactment of the Act. All
the workers were not aware of the benefits of the schemes which resulted in availing
of benefits by lesser number of workers during 2011-12.

2.4.7.2 Irregularities in payment of assistance to workers

Some instances of irregular payment of assistance under the scheme noticed during
test-check in audit are produced as follows:

(a) The ALC Poonch released assistance of हैं. 4.62 lakh in favour of 114
beneficiaries without recording details of registration of the workers (112 cases),

Audit Report on Social, General, Revenue and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs) for the year ended 31 March 2012

without obtaining proof of marriage (one case) and marriage assist
ineligible person (one case).

ance to an

(b) Marriage assistance of = 0.25 lakh was released by ALC Poonch in favour of
one construction worker in March 2012 but the amount was found credited to the
bank account of another person.

The Secretary stated (August 2012) that a committee was being constituted to look
into all such loopholes as pointed out by the audit.

2.4.7.3 Registration of establishments

Section 7 of the BOCW Act 1996 provides that every employer is required to make an
application with prescribed fee for the registration of his establishment within a period
of sixty days of the commencement of the Act or within sixty days from the date on
which Act becomes applicable to the establishment. The Board is also required under
Section 43(a) of the Act ibid to identify all the Government agencies and private
builders falling within the purview of the Act and arrange to collect cess at prescribed
rates. The cess is to be collected from every employer in such manner and at such
time including deduction at source in relation to a building or other construction work
of a Government or of a public sector undertaking or advance collection through a
local authority where an approval of such building or other construction work by such
local authority is required. Besides, the Board is required under Cess Act to educate
the employers about penalties, fine and imprisonment for violation and contravention
of the Acts. But it was noticed that neither list of registered employers was available
with the Board nor the Board had conducted any such exercise to identify the
employers as no such consolidated information was available in the Board office.

The State Labour Commissioner (Chief Inspector of Inspection under the Act) had,
however, identified 88 defaulting employers and list thereof was furnished (October
2011) to Commissioner/ Secretary, Labour and Employment Department. Though
notices had been served by the cess collectors of Kashmir division in 40 cases, no
action had been taken in Jammu division in respect of 48 defaulting cases. The
Secretary stated (August 2012) that assessing officers and cess collectors had been
requested to gear up the process of recovery of cess from the defaulters. It was also
stated that an amount of € 4.85 crore was received from an employer but position of
recovery of cess from the 88 cases pointed out in audit was not intimated.

2.4.7.4 Issue of identity cards and renewal of registrations

The identity cards issued were incomplete in as-much-as the space for mentioning the
details of the building and other construction work done by the beneficiary duly
authenticated by the employer was not provided in identity cards as required under
Section 13 of the BOCW Act, 1996. The worker-wise record of annual contribution
received from the workers was not maintained. Therefore, the position of outstanding
contribution from the workers was not ascertainable in audit. The Secretary/ CEO of
the Board stated (August 2012) that new identity card had been designed so that full
information would be available on it as required under the Act.

were engaged on outsourcing basis by the Board. In addition to these, the officers of
Labour Department were also engaged in implementation of the Act.

2.4.9 Internal Control System and Monitoring mechanism

2.4.9.1 Internal Audit and Internal Control Systems

Internal Audit and Internal Control Systems are important mechanisms for ensuring
the smooth working of an organisation. While effective Internal Control Mechanism
helps in exercising check on various activities of the organisation, Internal Audit acts
as an effective tool in exercising check on expenditure. It also ensures that various
systems are functioning properly.

Audit noticed the following deficiencies in the internal control system;

e The Cash Book in the CEO/ Secretary Office had been maintained on the basis of
bank scrolls. However, with effect from February 2011, the cheques of cess
amount were entered in the Cheque Receipt Register instead of Cash Book. The
records indicating details of cheques received, credit afforded on these cheques by
the bank to the account of the Board and cheques dishonoured were not
maintained by the Board.

e  Test-check of records of six ALCs revealed that the Cash Books were not closed
daily/ monthly by three ALCs (Baramulla, Bandipora and Srinagar) and three
ALCs (Poonch, Samba and Jammu) had not maintained Cash Book with the result
that genuineness of balances reflected in the bank statement could not be
reconciled.

The Secretary/ CEO stated (August 2012) that the necessary guidance of Audit has
been noted.

99


Audit Report on Social, General, Revenue and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs) for the year ended 31 March 2012

© The Board had not introduced any Internal Audit in order to ensure effective
control in exercising checks on various activities of the Board including its
finances. In reply to an audit query, the Secretary/ CEO of the Board stated
(July 2012) that the instructions had been noted.

2.4.9.2 Non-preparation of Annual report

Section 26 of the BOCW Act, 1996 envisages that the Board shall prepare, in such
form and at such time of each financial year as may be prescribed, its annual report,
giving a full account of its activities during the previous financial year and submit a
copy thereof to the State Government and Central Government. It was noticed in audit
that the Board had not prepared the requisite annual reports for the financial years
2010-11 and 2011-12.

2.4.10 Monitoring mechanism

The Board had empowered (July 2011) the CEO/ Secretary to take up the process of
implementation of the scheme by constituting committees at district level for
monitoring the implementation of the Act in an effective manner, Such committees
were to meet at regular intervals to give necessary feedback to the Board for removal
of the difficulties in smooth implementation of the scheme. Out of 22 monitoring
committees, only four committees (Reasi, Samba, Ganderbal and Kargil) had
furnished feedback to the Board. Thus, the very purpose of constitution of district
level committees for the better implementation of the Act could not be achieved. The
Secretary stated (August 2012) that the matter had been taken up with all the District
Commissioners and only some of them had shown keen interest in implementing the
Act. Audit further noticed that no meetings were conducted by the ALCs with the
district heads, etc. with regard to the implementation of the Act as no such reports
were on record with the ALCs.

2411 Conclusion

The State Government started implementation of the BOCW and Cess Acts after a
delay of more than 11 years from their enactments. The J&K Building and Other
Construction Workers Welfare Board after its constitution in March 2007 did not take
any initiatives to formulate the modalities for implementing the welfare schemes till
February 2011. The State Advisory Committee was not constituted even after more
than 16 years of enactment of the Act to advice the Board on such matters arising out
of the administration of the Act, as may be referred to it. The crucial aspects of
identification of beneficiaries and registration of establishments and implementation
of welfare schemes for workers were not given due priority. As a result, huge funds to
the extent of हैं 100.62 crore remained unutilised with the Board as of March 2012.
Expenditure to the tune of = 73.22 lakh had been incurred in excess of the permissible
limit during 2010-12 on establishment and other expenditure in contravention of the
Act. The monitoring mechanism was weak and internal audit and internal control
system had also not been instituted by the Board.

100


Chapter-2-Audit Findings an Social, General and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs)

2.4.12 Recommendations

° The Government may consider to ensure effective implementation of the
provisions of the Act.

° The Board may take effective steps to maintain a database with regard to
registration of establishments and conduct of proper survey for identification
of total number of building and other construction workers in the State.

e The State Advisory Committee may be constituted in compliance to BOCW Act,
1996,

¢ Proper mechanism may be evolved for examination of cases before sanctioning
and releasing the financial assistance to beneficiaries.

¢ An effective monitoring mechanism and internal control system may be put in
place for proper implementation of the Act and follow up of the welfare
schemes for the benefit of workers.

९. Audit of Transactions
Fraud/ Embezzlement/ Mis-appropriation/ Loss
Education Department

2.5. Fraudulent drawals

Non-observance of prescribed checks by the Principal, Government Girls
Higher Secondary School and Treasury Officer, Bhaderwah facilitated
fraudulent drawals of % 3.26 lakh.

Rule 2.27 of the Jammu and Kashmir Financial Code provide that the disbursing
officer should enter all bills drawn from the Treasury for departmental expenditure in
a register and get all drawals verified with the registers maintained at the Treasury
once in a month. Further, the rules stipulate that all monetary transactions should be
entered in the cash book as soon as they occur and should be attested by the Head of
the office in token of check.

The procedure for drawal of money from Treasury involves the following steps:

a. preparation and submission of the bills duly attested by the Drawing and
Disbursing Officer (DDO) to the Treasury;

b transferring of money by the Treasury into the official account of the DDO
and recording the entry of payment by the Treasury Officer in treasury records viz.,
Register in Form-3 (Madhwar);

०. recording of the entries of drawal of money by the DDO in his drawal register
and cash book and

व्‌. periodical reconciliation of the cash book balances with the drawal register
and the Bank account statement by the DDO. For this purpose, the DDO is to obtain
from the Treasury Officer by the 15" of every month, a list of bills drawn during the
previous month and trace them in the cash book.

Audit check (December 2010) of records of the Principal, Government Girls Higher
Secondary School, Bhaderwah (DDO) by way of verification of transactions recorded
in the drawal register with the Bank Account statement of the DDO and the records of
Treasury Bhaderwah revealed that three drawals' amounting to हैं 2.46 lakh were
drawn (January 2009 and February 2010) from the Treasury and had not been
recorded in drawal register and cash book of the DDO. These drawals were made on
the basis of fictitious claims of General Provident Fund (GPF) advance (% 1.30 lakh)
of two officials and pay arrears of the staff (¥ 1.16 lakh) from the Treasury. The
entries thereof were not recorded in the drawal register, cash book and GPF advance
register of the office or the personal records of the employees. The amounts after
transfer by the treasury to the official account of the DDO were subsequently credited
to the bank accounts of a fake official (January 2009) and senior assistant of the office
(February 2010) respectively on the basis of authority letters issued by the DDO. A
check of treasury records (Madhwar) and the drawal register revealed that the
Treasury Officer without verifying the drawals made by the DDO during January
2009 and February 2010 from his records had recorded wrong certificates of
verification of drawals on the drawal register.

' January 2009: ₹ 0.70 lakh; January 2009: ₹ 0.60 lakh and February 2010: ₹ 1.16 lakh

102


Chapter-2-Audit Findings an Social, General and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs)

Audit further observed that an amount of & 0.80 lakh was drawn between December
2008 and December 2009 by inflating totals of the pay bills (₹ 0.29 lakh), drawal of
inadmissible pay wages (% 0.10 lakh) of contractual lecturer, charge allowance (% 0.23
lakh) of a lecturer and leave salary (% 0.18 lakh) of a teacher.

These fraudulent drawals were facilitated due to non-observance of the following
prescribed procedures:-

© The DDO had not obtained from the Treasury Officer Bhaderwah, by the 157 of
every month, a list of bills drawn during the previous month and traced them in
the cash book.

¢ The DDO had not ensured entry of all the bills drawn from the Treasury in the
drawal register and cash book of the office besides, proper acquittance of
amounts in favour of genuine officials.

e The DDO had not ensured accuracy of the totals of pay bills and correctness of
the claims of pay and allowances, advances, leave salary, etc.

e The Treasury Officer had not verified individual drawals made by the DDO from
his records while recording the certificate on the drawal register.

The matter was pointed out (December 2010) in Audit to the Principal, Government
Girls Higher Secondary School and the Treasury Officer, Bhaderwah and reported to
the Director School Education, Jammu (January 2011) and the Government
(April 2011). The Treasury Officer stated (December 2010) that verification of the
drawals had been conducted wrongly due to oversight and rush of work.

The Director School Education, Jammu constituted (March 2011) an inquiry
committee to look into the audit observation. The Committee held (November 2011)
the ex-Principal and the senior assistant responsible for financial irregularities. The
Chief Accounts Officer of the Directorate of School Education, Jammu in response to
further audit queries (December 2011) intimated that the senior assistant had been
placed (December 2011) under suspension and the ex-Principal had been charge
sheeted (December 2011) and that the amount of & 3.26 lakh had been remitted into
the Treasury/paid to the concerned officials by the senior assistant.

Thus, non-observance of prescribed checks by the Principal, Government Girls
Higher Secondary School and Treasury Officer, Bhaderwah facilitated fraudulent
drawals of = 3.26 lakh by the DDO. The risk of drawal of GPF advances on fake bills
may be minimised by establishing a system of intimating to the concerned employee
by the Treasury, while moving towards a system of direct electronic credit of
employee’s bank accounts.

The matter was referred to the Government in March 2012. The reply had not been
received (December 2012).

Health and Medical Education Department

2.6 Misappropriation of Government money

Failure to enforce internal controls by the Medical Superintendent, District
Hospital, Baramulla facilitated misappropriation of Government money to the
tune of € 23.27 lakh.

The Government accorded (December 2000) sanction for creation of Hospital
Development Fund (HDF) which was to be credited with user charges realized from

103


Audit Report on Social, General, Revenue and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs) for the year ended 31 March 2012

patients on account of sale of Out Patient Department (OPD) tickets and various
medical investigations/ facilities in Government hospitals. The Government further
directed (October 2001 and February 2008) that these user charges should be retained
and utilized for minor repairs/ maintenance of hospitals, machinery and equipment
including cost of films for providing regular facilities to the patients.

Rule 2.2 and 2.15 of Jammu and Kashmir Financial Code provide that the Drawing
and Disbursing Officer (DDO) should ensure that all the monetary transactions are
entered in the cash book and should also verify cash balances in the cash book at the
end of each month. The DDO should further ensure that a proper receipt is issued on
receipt of Government money.

Test-check (December 2011) of records of the Medical Superintendent, District
Hospital, Baramulla revealed that the user charges collected by the Incharge of
various Sections of the Hospital on account of sale of OPD/ IPD” tickets, medical
facilities/ investigations’, etc. were deposited with the cashier of the Hospital for
onward credit to the HDF Account maintained with Jammu and Kashmir Bank
Limited, Baramulla. Out of % 1.47 crore (including opening balance of 38.74 lakh as
of April 2009) realised from April 2009 to November 2011, an expenditure of हैं 1.03
crore was incurred by the Medical Superintendent leaving balance of हैं 44.42 lakh as
of November 2011 as per cash book of the Hospital. However, there was balance of
र₹ 23.75 lakh* only in the HDF account as of November 2011 revealing
misappropriation of € 20.67 lakh.

In another case, Audit further noticed that out of हैं 43.03 lakh realized from April
2009 to November 2011 on account of sale of OPD tickets by Incharge OPD Section,
only हैं 40.43 lakh were deposited with the Cashier resulting in further
misappropriation of % 2.60 lakh. Thus, total misappropriation amounted to हैं 23.27
lakh.

Audit further noticed that the cash handling was being done at multiple points and
hence codal provisions concerning cash should have been appropriately applied at
each cash collection point. Periodical reconciliation/ verification of receipts collected
by various sections of the Hospital was not conducted with the records of the cashier
and the bank. On being pointed out (December 2011) in Audit, the Medical
Superintendent of the Hospital stated (December 2011) that हैं 11.56 lakh had been
adjusted by admitting vouchers (® 4.10 lakh), recovery of amount from the concerned
(₹ 6.90 lakh), bill variation’ wrong posting (% 0.56 lakh) and accepted
misappropriation of = 9.15 lakh. It was stated (February 2012) that further recoveries
of रं three lakh had been effected from the Cashier and % 2.59 lakh from the concerned
official of OPD Section of the Hospital.

The reply of the Medical Superintendent is not tenable. Out of = 6.90 lakh stated to
have been recovered from the Cashier, credit of रैं 3.30 lakh was already afforded in
the Hospital Development Fund Account in November 2011, which was accounted
for while working out balance at the close of 30 November 2011. Further,
documentary proof of recoveries of हैं three lakh and हैं 2.59 lakh had not been
furnished to audit and no action by way of lodging of first information report/
criminal investigation into the matter was taken by the Department.

Unfruitful/ Unproductive/ Idle expenditure
Agriculture Production Department

Sher-e-Kashmir University of Agricultural Sciences and Technology, Jammu

2.7 Idle expenditure

Failure of the University to ensure installation and operation of equipment
resulted in idle expenditure of % 4.14 crore besides, depriving the
students/ researchers of the intended benefits.

The Sher-e-Kashmir University of Agricultural Sciences and Technology, Jammu
(SKUAST) procured (2005-08) various high-tech equipment at a cost of हैं 6.12 crore
as detailed in Appendix-2.5 for use by different departments of the University. The
equipment were to be used for facilitating research and for imparting practical
trainings to students undergoing various courses in the University.

Audit scrutiny (January 2012) of records of the University revealed that after receipt
(2006-08) of 18 equipment, no serious efforts were made by the University authorities
to install and make them functional with the result that all the equipment were lying
unutilized (June 2012). The equipment were imported by making payments through
letters of credit operated in favour of the firms. The performance guarantee of the
equipment was obtained but the amount of security was released without ensuring
installation and commissioning of the equipment. After this was pointed out in Audit,
the Registrar of the University informed (June 2012) that a committee had been
constituted to suggest solutions to make the equipment functional.

The matter was referred to Government/ University in July 2012. The Registrar
informed (August 2012) that after non-functioning of equipment valuing % 6.12 crore
was pointed out by Audit, the University had succeeded in making eight equipment
costing = 1.98 crore functional. The progress of making the balance 10 equipment
functional was awaited (December 2012).

Failure of the University authorities to ensure installation of equipment resulted in
idle expenditure of हैं 4.14 crore, besides depriving the students of the intended
benefits

Public Works (Roads and Buildings) Department

2.8  Unfruitful expenditure due to non-acquisition of land

Failure of the Department to acquire land for approach roads of the bridge
before allotment of the work resulted in unfruitful expenditure of हैं 73.40 lakh
and locking up of % 90 lakh besides, depriving the people of the benefits of road
connectivity of Katra Town with Domel-Katra road.

To provide road connectivity of Katra town with Domel-Katra road through four?
villages, the Executive Engineer (EE), Roads and Buildings Division (R&B), Katra
proposed (September 2007) construction of motorable bridge over Magal Nallah
along with approaches at an estimated cost of = 1.50 crore to be completed within one

Nangal, Parnote, Sool and Magal

105


Audit Report on Social, General, Revenue and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs) for the year ended 31 March 2012

year. The project included provision of हैं 75 lakh for construction of 25 metre sp:
motorable steel girder bridge and हैं 10 lakh for land compensation. The land for
approach roads was not acquired by the EE before taking up execution of the project.
An amount of € 75 lakh was released under Special Infrastructure Development Fund
(District Plan) during the period 2007-09.

Audit scrutiny (July 2010) of records of the EE, R&B Division, Katra revealed that in
anticipation of Administrative Approval, the work was taken up and construction of
the bridge was completed (March 2009) at a cost of = 73.40 lakh. The cost of the
project was revised (July 2009) to = 1.96 crore for funding its balance works worth
र₹ 1.21 crore. However, the provision for land compensation was not kept in the
revised project. The balance work of the project of approach roads which was allotted
(October 2009) to a contractor at a cost of € 70.26 lakh could not take off due to land
dispute with the locals for non-payment of land compensation. As the balance work
could not be commenced, further = 90 lakh released (November 2009) under the State
Plan for the project was advanced (December 2009 to March 2010) by the EE to the
State Procurement Department for purchase of construction material. However, the
material had not been lifted (February 2012) and the amount continued to remain
locked up.

On this being pointed out in audit, the EE stated (February 2012) that as and when the
land dispute was settled, the work would be completed immediately. The reply was
not acceptable as the EE was required to ensure encumbrance free land before taking
up work of the project.

Thus, failure of the Department to acquire land for approach roads of the bridge
before allotment of the work resulted in unfruitful expenditure of हैं 73.40 lakh
besides, locking up of = 90 lakh. The objective of providing road connectivity of
Katra town with Domel-Katra road could also not be achieved.

The matter was referred to Government/ Department in March 2012; the reply had not
been received (December 2012).

2.9 Unproductive expenditure due to failure to ensure availability of land

Failure of the Departmental authorities to ensure availability of land free from
encumbrance to the contractors before taking up construction of two roads
resulted in unproductive expenditure of < 1.42 crore.

(A) _ To provide road connectivity to village Dhanna in district Kathua with
population of 2,000 souls, the Executive Engineer, R&B Division, Kathua proposed
construction of 2.85 Kms. long fair weather road from point L024 to village Dhanna
under Pradhan Mantri Gram Sadak Yojana (PMGSY) before ensuring availability of
land free from encumbrance. The village was not connected by any road theretofore.
The road was to play an important role in providing basic facilities of life to the
people besides convenient transportation of marketable goods. The project was
sanctioned (May 2008) at a cost of ~ 98.05 lakh. According to the PMGSY
guidelines, the Department is responsible to ensure availability of land for taking up
the proposed road works.

Audit scrutiny (March 2012) of records of the Executive Engineer (EE), PMGSY
Division, Kathua revealed that the work for construction of a fair weather road was
allotted (November 2008) to a contractor at a cost of हैं 96.98 lakh before ensuring
availability of land free from encumbrance. The work was to be completed within a

106


Chapter-2-Audit Findings an Social, General and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs)

period of nine months. The contractor belatedly took up (February 2010) the work and
completed (November 2010) construction of 2.30 Kms. of road at a cost of & 65.46
lakh but could not execute balance work at the starting point (RD 1/0 to 1/425 Kms.)
of road due to dispute over title of land. As this portion of land was not made
available to the contractor, the execution of work on the road remained suspended, as
a result, the road connectivity could not be provided to the village (April 2012).

After this was pointed out in audit, the EE stated (March and May 2012) that the
project could not be completed due to dispute over ownership of land and that the
matter was sub-judice. The reply is not tenable as the Departmental authorities were
required to ensure land free from encumbrance to the contractor before taking up
construction of road.

Thus, failure of the Departmental authorities to ensure availability of land free from
encumbrance to the contractor before taking up construction of road resulted in
unproductive expenditure of रैं 65.46 lakh, besides depriving the populace of benefits
of road connectivity.

The matter was referred to the Government/ Department in May 2012. The Financial
Advisor and Chief Accounts Officer of the Department reported (July 2012) that the
Department was following vigorously for vacation of court stay and execution of
balance work of the Project. Further progress in the matter was awaited (December
2012).

(B) Construction of a two Kms long road, connecting Nagri road (Muthi) Jagir
and Dadoli in Barnoti block of Kathua district, was sanctioned (May 2008) under
Phase-VI of Pradhan Mantri Gram Sadak Yojana (PMGSY) at a cost of हैं 96.99 lakh.
According to PMGSY guidelines, the State Government is responsible for ensuring
availability of land for taking up any road works under the scheme.

Audit scrutiny (March 2012) of records of the Executive Engineer (EE), PMGSY
Division, Kathua revealed that without ensuring the availability of land, free from
encumbrances of disputes, structures and electrical utilities coming in the alignment
of road, the Superintending Engineer, PMGSY, Jammu Circle, Jammu allotted
(September 2008) the work of construction of road to a contractor at a cost of = 95.82
lakh. The work was to be completed within a period of nine months. The contractor
took up (February 2009) execution of road belatedly due to non-clearance of site
because of standing crop in the alignment of road. After construction of road length of
1,425 metres in patches at a cost of = 76.58 lakh the contractor stopped (May 2010)
further execution of work of the road as the possession of land was not made available
to him by the Department due to non-payment of compensation for the structures,
electrical utilities and to the land owners. Though the compensation for structures had
been determined (November 2010) at = 19.54 lakh, the same had not been settled for
electrical utilities (April 2012). Further, possession of land under remaining 575
metres of road length had not been taken over by the Department (April 2012). As a
result of these impediments, the contractor could not resume and complete the work.

After this was pointed out by Audit, the EE stated (April 2012) that compensation for
some areas of land had not been made and the estimates for shifting of electrical
utilities submitted by the Power Development Department were on higher side and as
aresult, the land could not be acquired.

Thus, failure of the Departmental authorities to ensure encumbrance free land to the
contractor before allotment of work for construction of road from Nagri road (Muthi)

107


Audit Report on Social, General, Revenue and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs) for the year ended 31 March 2012

Jagir to Dadoli in Kathua district resulted in unproductive expenditure of हैं 76.58 lakh
besides, denial of intended benefits to the local populace of the area. The
commencement of work in such a manner was also a deviation from the guidelines of
PMGSY.

The matter was referred to the Government/ Department in June 2012. The Financial
Advisor/ Chief Accounts Officer of the Department reported (August 2012) that the
Collector, Land Acquisition, Kathua had issued directions (July 2012) to the
Tehsildar, Kathua to remove obstructions of structures from the alignment of the road.
Further progress in the matter was awaited (December 2012).

Irrigation and Flood Control Department

2.10 Unfruitful expenditure due to non-execution of the project

Failure of the Executive Engineer to provide encumbrance free land before
allotment of work to the contractor for execution of the project resulted in
unfruitful expenditure of = 49.05 lakh and locking up of = 1.32 crore besides,
denial of intended benefits to the farmers of the area.

To provide irrigation facilities to cultivable command area comprising of 160 hectares
of agriculture land of three villages® in Akhnoor, Jammu, the Executive Engineer,
Inigation Division, Akhnoor proposed (July 2008) project ‘construction of Khul
Takwal’ at a cost of हैं 1.89 crore which included provision of हैं 23.44 lakh for
acquisition of land. The project was approved (September 2008) by the State Level
Technical Advisory Committee for funding under the Government of India (GOI)
scheme ‘Accelerated Irrigation Benefit Programme’ and was targeted to be completed
within two years. The project envisaged diversion of water from the creek near village
Sarwal Channi by way of construction of a Chak Dam besides, construction of
rectangular channel, distributory channels, 20 distribution outlets, ete. After its
completion, the project was to provide assured irrigation to agriculture land of the
farmers thereby, improving their socio-economic conditions.

Audit scrutiny (December 2011) of records of the Executive Engineer (EE), Irrigation
Division, Akhnoor, Jammu revealed that the Chief Engineer, Irrigation and Flood
Control Department, Jammu released € 1.81 crore (GOI share: हैं 1.70 crore; State
share: हैं 11.20 lakh) during 2009-10 and 2010-11 for implementation of the project
Instead of taking up execution of the project, the EE incurred expenditure on
procurement of stores (रैं 1.32 crore), improvement of existing channels/ allied works
(₹ 46.25 lakh) and miscellaneous items’ (₹ 2.80 lakh). The EE without acquisition of
land required for construction of dam, allotted its work to the contractor in January
2010 at a cost of हैं 28.69 lakh. As a result, the locals in possession of land did not
allow the contractor to start the work. No action was taken by the Department despite
the position being brought to its notice by the contractor (June 2010). The execution
of project suffered thereby, depriving the farmers of the area of the intended benefits
of the project.

After this was pointed out in audit, the EE stated (March 2012) that the work of the
project could not be executed due to land dispute. The reply is not tenable as the EE
was required to ensure encumbrance free land for execution of the project before

2.12 Unfruitful investment due to failure to finalise design of pump house

Failure to finalise design of the pump house of Lift Irrigation Scheme, Mandla
(Kathua) resulted in unfruitful investment of हैं 3.54 crore besides, denial of
intended benefits of the scheme to the people of the area.

To provide timely and assured irrigation facilities to command cultivable area of 283
hectares of land in village Mandla (Basohli), Kathua, a Lift Irrigation Scheme
estimated to cost हैं 4.44 crore was approved (September 2008) by the Technical
Advisory Committee of the Department for execution under Accelerated Irrigation
Benefit Programme (AIBP). The Project involved construction of pump house (® 1.21
crore), installation of Vertical Turbine Pump Sets (रे 2.29 crore) and other allied
works"! which was to be completed within three years. The project envisaged lifting
of water from Ranjeet Sagar Dam on river Ravi upto delivery tank at hill top where
the main canal was to be fed with water for irrigation purpose. An amount of
% 5.02 crore (Central Share: % five crore; State Share: हैं two lakh) was released during
2009-10 to 2011-12 in favour of the Executive Engineer (EE), Irrigation Division,
Kathua under the scheme.

Test-check (January 2012) of records of the Division revealed that tenders for
construction of reinforced cement concrete (RCC) pump house invited (December
2009) were not responded to despite being extended three times between January and
February 2010. The Jammu and Kashmir Project Construction Corporation Limited
also expressed (March 2010) its inability to execute this work. The construction
through an alternative design (floating structure pump house) was also found (August
2010) un-economical and un-feasible by a Private agency. Consequently, the RCC
pump house was redesigned and the revised design was submitted (November 2011)
by the EE for vetting to the Chief Engineer, Designs, Inspections and Quality Control,
Jammu, which had not been finalized (July 2012). Meanwhile, in anticipation of
finalisation of design and structure of the pump house, the EE had incurred an
expenditure of € 3.54 crore!” during 2009-12 and surrendered & 1.48 crore’ as further
work of the project could not be executed. As the design and structure for the pump
house could not be finalised for about four years, the EE, Mechanical Irrigation
Construction Division, Jammu could also not procure the machinery (April 2012).

Health and Medical Education Department

2.13 Unproductive expenditure due to injudicious action

Injudicious action of the Department to allot construction of hospital building at
Bandipora on the land unsuitable and insufficient for the purpose resulted in
unproductive expenditure of = 2.91 crore.

The construction of 50 bedded hospital at Nasoo, Bandipora was entrusted to Jammu
and Kashmir Project Construction Corporation Limited (Government Company) at a
cost of € 22.77 crore during 2004-05 by the Department. For this purpose, an amount
of = 10.81 crore was released during the period 2004-05 to 2009-10 to the company.

Audit scrutiny (August 2011) of the records of the Director, Health Services, Kashmir
revealed that with the creation (2007-08) of Bandipora as district, the hospital was
proposed to be upgraded to 100 bedded District hospital and, accordingly, the design
of the hospital was changed for which the company submitted (August 2007) the
revised project report for हैं 38.70 crore to the Director Health Services, Kashmir who
in turn forwarded (October 2007) it to the Government for accord of administrative
approval. The work of construction of building was, however, taken up (March 2005)
by the Company in anticipation of the administrative approval and after part’
execution of the project on which an expenditure of % 2.91 crore had been incurred,
further work was stopped (January 2009) as the site was found to be flood prone and
the site of the land for construction of district hospital insufficient. Following this, it
was decided (January 2009) to shift the hospital to an alternate site. Audit further
observed that during the District Development Board meeting (July 2010) followed
by the Board review meeting (December 2010), it was decided to construct the
District Hospital at another site near New Bus Stand, Bandipora. For this purpose,
रू four crore was got transferred (January 2011) to the District Development
Commissioner, Bandipora for acquisition of land out of balance amount of = 7.90
crore available with the Company. The work for construction of district hospital at the
new site was allotted (July 2011) to Jammu and Kashmir Housing Board, Srinagar at
a cost of = 24.33 crore. The Department had intended (February 2009) to utilise the
abandoned structures at Nasoo Bandipora for District Tuberculoses Centre, District
Family Welfare Centre and AMT" School However, the structures continued to
remain abandoned and were not made functional (September 2012), rendering the
expenditure incurred thereon unproductive.

Industries and Commerce (Geology & Mining) Department

2.14 Unproductive expenditure due to non-procurement of Broadband

[Failure of the departmental authorities to finalise process of procurement of

After the earthquake that hit parts of the State in October 2005, an international

observatories at different locations in the State was felt for monitoring of seismic
activities on regular basis.

Audit scrutiny (January 2012) of records of the Director, State Geology and Mining
Department, Jammu revealed that as a follow up to the workshops deliberations, the
Director suggested (November 2008) to the Government for establishment of three
Geo-technical seismological observatories, one each at Tanghdar, Uri and Poonch. In
the first phase, construction of observatory at Tangdhar (Kupwara) was proposed to
be taken up. Accordingly, the administrative approval for construction of
seismological observatory at Tanghdar (Kupwara) at a cost of हैं 71.14 lakh was
accorded (October 2009) by the Director. The project included construction of main
observatory building and one independent room for installation of Broadband
seismograph (BBS) and was to be executed through the Executive Engineer, Jammu
and Kashmir Police Housing Corporation, Srinagar to be completed within one
working season. For this purpose, an amount of = 71.14 lakh’® was advanced to the
Corporation by the Department. Meanwhile, the Director requested (November 2008
and November 2010) the Government to accord approval for importing BBS through
the Department of Geology, University of Jammu, which was also recommended
(February 2010) by the State level purchase committee of the Department. However,
the Government did not agree to this proposal and instead directed (January 2011) the
Director to place orders for procurement of BBS with the approved firm itself at the
latest approved rates. An amount of हैं 25 lakh released (March 2010) by the
Government for procurement of the equipment were drawn and kept in civil deposit
head of account by the Director. However, the Department could not finalise the

we March 2009: ₹ 25 lakh; December 2009: ₹ 24 lakh and June 2010: ₹ 22.14 lakh

Chapter-2-Audit Findings an Social, General and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs)

process of procurement of BBS and the amount of हैं 25 lakh continued to remain
blocked in the civil deposit (March 2012).

Audit check of records further revealed that construction of the building had been
completed by the Corporation at a cost of € 50 lakh except one independent room for
installation of BBS. As the style and design of the equipment which would determine
the exact inner layout of the independent room was not made available by the
Department, the Corporation could not take up execution of this room, as a result,
balance amount of % 21.14 lakh remained unutilised with the Corporation and the
observatory could not be established.

In his reply, the Sr. Drilling Engineer (C) stated (January 2012) that the Department
intended to purchase the equipment at the approved rates of University of Jammu and
as soon as approval was received from the Government, the procurement action
would be initiated. The reply should be viewed in light of the fact that no action had
been taken by the Director on directions of the Government in January 2011 to place
orders for procurement of BBS with the approved firm at the latest approved rates.

Thus, due to the failure of the departmental authorities to finalise process of
procurement of Broadband seismograph for over three years due to which the
seismological observatory at Tanghdar (Kupwara) could not be established resulted in

% 46.14 lakh. The objective of monitoring seismic activity in the State on regular basis
through establishment of the seismological observatory could also not be achieved.

Animal Husbandry Department

2.15 Unplanned implementation of project

‘Unplanned implementation of project for import of semen of elite animals
resulted in blockade of = 50 lakh for over two years besides, objective of the
project could also not be achieved.

To increase productivity potential of bovines and make the State self-sufficient in
milk production, the Department intended to introduce superior germ plasm by using
semen of elite animals. For this purpose, the Director Animal Husbandry Department,
Jammu submitted (January 2010) a proposal to the Government for import of 1.30
lakh doses of Holstein/ Jersey semen of elite bovine animals from a United States
(US) based Company in two lots during the years 2009-10 and 2010-11 for use in the
bovine population of the State at a cost of € two crore. The project was to be taken up
on pilot basis and was also recommended (March 2010) by the expert technical
committee constituted for the purpose.

Test-check (December 2011) of records of the Director, Animal Husbandry
Department, Jammu revealed that the Government sanctioned (March 2010) % 50 lakh
for implementation of the project subject to following of all codal procedures
indicated in Rule 8.5 (1) of Jammu and Kashmir Financial Code and instructions
made thereunder. The amount was kept under deposit head of account by the Director
and was to be operated upon after finalization of arrangement of import. The Director,
without involving the State level purchase committee of the Department, took up the
matter directly with the US based Company for supply of semen and for grant of
licence for its import with the Directorate General of Foreign Trade, Government of
India. Thereafter, the Director, requested (March 2011) the Government for accord of
sanction for purchase of semen from the Company and for opening letter of credit

113


Audit Report on Social, General, Revenue and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs) for the year ended 31 March 2012

thereof which was not accepted. The Joint Director (Planning) instead observed (April
2011) that approval of the firm and rates was the mandate of the State level purchase
committee and that letter of credit was to be opened on the basis of supply order and
agreement executed with the approved firm. Accordingly, the global tendering
process for import of semen was initiated (September 2011) by the State level
purchase committee which had not been finalised (August 2012), as a result of which
¥ 50 lakh could not be utilised and continued to remain blocked. Further funds were
also not released by the Government

On this being pointed out in audit (December 2011), the Director stated (December
2011) that हैं 50 lakh had remained unutilised due to procedural delays like obtaining
of import licence and other allied formalities related to the project. The reply is not
acceptable and should be viewed in light of the fact that the Director was required to
follow the prescribed procedure for import of semen from the firm selected and
approved by the State level purchase committee of the Department immediately after
sanction of the project.

The matter was referred to the Government/ Department in June 2012. The Director
informed (August 2012) that the meeting of the State level purchase committee could
not be convened due to official engagement of both Heads of the Department.

Thus, unplanned implementation of the project for import of semen of elite animals
by the Director resulted in blockade of % 50 lakh for over two years besides, objective
of the project could also not be achieved.

Agriculture Production Department
Sher-e-Kashmir University of Agricultural Sciences and Technology, Kashmir

2.17 Locking up of funds due to non-implementation of research project

Non-implementation of a research project by SKUAST authorities for about
eight years resulted in locking up of & 1.46 crore besides, non-accrual of benefits
of the project to the farmers/ apple traders of the State.

The Sher-e-Kashmir University of Agricultural Sciences and Technology, Kashmir,
Srinagar (SKUAST), a State University, signed (November 2004) a Memorandum of
Understanding (MOU) with the Agricultural and Processed Food Products Export
Development Authority’? (APEDA), New Delhi to undertake a research project titled
‘Improvement of shelf life of apple cultivars Red Delicious and Ambri for export
promotion’. The project was to be funded by the APEDA and was to be completed
within three years from the date of signing of the MOU. The cost of the project
amounting to = 1.61 crore was approved (November 2004) by the APEDA.

The SKUAST was to utilize the results of the research for publication in the form of a
booklet covering the most appropriate protocol for production of Red Delicious and
Ambri varieties of apple to be followed by farmers for pre and post harvest
management practices in future.

Audit scrutiny (December 2011) of records of the Registrar, SKUAST revealed that
% 79.12 lakh were released by the APEDA upto March 2005 out of which हैं 63 lakh
were placed (May 2005) at the disposal of Estates Wing of the University by the
Principal Investigator APEDA project of SKUAST for construction of pack house
@ 18 lakh) and upgradation/ refurbishing of the existing controlled atmospheric (CA)
cold store at the Division of Post Harvest Technology of the University (रे 45 lakh)
Further, हैं 79.32 lakh were released by the APEDA in favour of the University during
the period from April 2005 to August 2008. After receipt of funds, the Estates wing
floated tenders repeatedly between March 2006 and March 2008 for refurbishment/
upgradation of CA store which did not materialize due to poor response to the tender
notices and quoting higher rates by the tenderers. Subsequent re-tendering (March
2009) met with some success and the Works Committee of the University

18 An Autonomous Body under the Ministry of Commerce and Industry, Government of India

recommended (November 2009) grant of work order in favour of the lowest tenderer”

Revenue Department

2.18 Non-utilisation of Central funds

Due to non-identification of sites and non-establishing of basic infrastructure for
setting up computer centres for computerization of land records, the Department
could not utilize the funds rendering the scheme non-functional besides,
depriving the people of the State of the benefits of the scheme for over two
decades.

The proposal for computerization of land records in 257 Niabats (Sub-Tehsils) of the
State at a cost of हैं 20.56 crore” was submitted (August 2006) by the State
Government to the Government of India. The proposal was framed on the basis of a
pilot project taken up after development of a software departmentally for one Niabat
Khanmoh in Srinagar tehsil. For this purpose, the Government of India released

al M/s Voltas Limited New Delhi
a M/s Fresh Food Technologies (I) Limited Noida (UP)
2 At an average cost of & 8 lakh per Niabat
