Chapter-2-Audit Findings an Social, General and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs)

(March 2007) ₹ 15.42 crore” under the Centrally Sponsored Scheme
“Computerisation of Land Records’. The amount was to be spent for creation of
infrastructure of hardware items, server, system software, electrical fitting, furniture,
etc, The amount was credited (April 2007) to the Bank account of the Department by
the Financial Commissioner (Revenue), Jammu and Kashmir.

Audit scrutiny (December 2011) of records of the Financial Commissioner (Revenue)
revealed that = 15 crore was converted (April 2007) into fixed deposit as the scheme
could not be implemented due to the fact that the Department could not identify the
sites and establish basic infrastructure for setting up computer centres at sub-tehsil
offices. The amount which accumulated to हैं 16.95 crore including interest of € 1.53
crore was transferred (September 2008) to Government account. To review the
progress of computerization of land records, an apex level meeting was held (June
2009) under the chairmanship of the Hon’ble Chief Minister of the State wherein it
was decided to depute officers of the Department to other States for understanding the
process for implementation of the scheme of computerization of land records as well
as for adoption of the procedure suitable and in consonance with the geographical
conditions of the State. However, none of the officers was deputed; neither were any
efforts made to utilize the funds to implement the scheme.

Audit further observed that the Department had, without identifying the sites and
establishing basic infrastructure for setting up computer centres at sub-tehsil offices,
submitted proposal to the Government of India for computerization of land records.
After this was pointed out in Audit, the Assistant Director (Planning and Statistics)
also confirmed (January 2012) that infrastructural facilities at Niabat level was
insufficient

The Department had earlier also not implemented the scheme in a proper and planned
manner for which the Central Government had released % 2.86 crore during March
1990 to June 1999 to the State Government rendering the expenditure of @ 2.66 crore
unfruitful. A mention about the same had been made in the Report of the Comptroller
and Auditor General of India for the year ended March 2005. The computer hardware
and software purchased thereunder had become obsolete. Despite the assistance made
available by the Central Government since March 1990 for computerization of land
records, the Department had failed to implement the scheme and provide benefits to
the people of the State for over past two decades. This indicated indifferent approach
of the Department in implementing the scheme.

Thus, due to non-identification of sites and non-establishing of basic infrastructure for
setting up computer centres for computerization of land records, the Department could
not utilize the funds, rendering the scheme non-functional besides, depriving the
people of the State of the benefits of the scheme for over two decades.

The matter was referred to Government/ Department in March 2012; the reply had not
been received (December 2012).

Public Works (Roads and Buildings) Department

2.19 Irregular expenditure

Improper planning by the Departmental authorities in implementation of the
road project from Daresh-Kadal to Qamarwari, Srinagar resulted in irregular
expenditure of € 1.10 crore besides, locking up of € 50 lakh.

The project for ‘Upgradation and widening of 1.5 km road from Daresh-Kadal to
Qamarwari (Srinagar)’ at an estimated cost of € 12.78 crore was conceived (2006-07)
by the Executive Engineer (EE), Roads and Buildings (R&B) Construction Division-I,
Srinagar to be completed in two working seasons. The project provided for acquisition
of land and structures coming in alignment of the road (% 9.38 crore), construction of
four lane bridge at Chattabal (¥ 0.63 crore) and upgradation/ widening of road (% 2.77
crore’), The upgraded road was to serve as a bye-pass for the sector of National
Highway from Batmaloo to Qamarwari.

Audit scrutiny (September 2011) of records of the EE revealed that during the period
of four years from 2006-07 to 2009-10, only an amount of = 1.60 crore was released to
the Division. Instead of taking steps for acquisition of land and structures, which was
pre-requisite for widening and up-gradation of the road, the EE, in anticipation of
administrative approval, incurred an expenditure of % 1.10 crore comprising
maintenance of the existing road (% 54.43 lakh”), procurement of bitumen (% 50.11
lakh) and other works (® 5.49 lakh) during the period 2006-10. A meagre amount of
% 50 lakh was advanced (March 2007) to the Collector, Land Acquisition, Srinagar for
acquisition of land against provision of हैं 9.38 crore in the project. The expenditure on
maintenance of the existing road was clearly beyond the sanctioned scope of the
project and other expenditures were also incurred without proper authority and
synchronization of various activities involved in the project. Clearly the project could
not have moved forward without first completing the acquisition process. As the land
and structures coming in alignment of the road were not acquired, the work on the
project could not be started resulting in its time overrun necessitating revision of cost
of the project which was accordingly revised (December 2010) to ₹ 68.72 crore. After
this was pointed out in audit, the EE stated (January 2012) that the land could not be
acquired due to non-release of adequate funds for the project and that the process of
evaluation of structures could also not be completed. The reply should be viewed in
light of the fact that instead of primarily completing the process of evaluation of
structures and ensuring acquisition of land coming in the alignment of the road, the
Department had incurred हैं 1.10 crore on items not related to the project besides,
locking up of funds with a clear knowledge of the impediments to the acquisition of
land and structures.

Thus, improper planning of the Departmental authorities in implementation of the
project by way of inadequate funding, inadequate provision of funds for acquisition of
land and structures and incurring of expenditure beyond the sanctioned scope of the
project resulted in irregular expenditure of हैं 1.10 crore and locking up of हैं 50 lakh.
The intended benefits of the Project could also not be achieved.

Includes works contingencies of € 37.23 lakh
Construction of retaining walls: र 10 lakh; Laying of bitumen premix: र 37.09 lakh; raising and repair of
U-shaped side drain: & 7.18 lakh and miscellaneous charges: ₹ै 0.16 lakh

properties and land was completed. Further progress in the matter was awaited
(December 2012).

Irrigation and Flood Control Department

2.20 Lack of due diligence in contract management

Failure of the Departmental authorities to ensure completion of allotted work
land subsequent inaction to seize the hypothecated machinery at the time of
abandonment of work by the contractor resulted in non-recovery/ adjustment of
mobilisation advance of % 3.37 crore besides, blocking of € 64.97 lakh.

With a view to provide irrigation facility to 5,500 acres of land of Illaqua Drab in
tehsil Thathri of District Doda, the Chief Engineer, Irrigation and Flood Control
Department, Jammu submitted (February 2006) a detailed project report titled ‘Kandi
Canal Project’ to Central Water Commission (CWC). The project was accorded
(November 2006) techno- economic-viability clearance by the CWC to be funded
under Accelerated Irrigation Benefit Programme. The project envisaged construction
of 38.5 Kms., long irrigation canal from village Kahara to village Bella at an
estimated cost of € 53.70 crore

Audit Scrutiny (March 2012) of records of the Executive Engineer (EE), Irrigation
and Flood Control Division, Bhaderwah showed that the contract for construction of
first ten kilometers of canal length was allotted (June 2007) to a contractor’ on
turnkey basis at a cost of € 23.68 crore, to be completed within 18 months. The terms
of contract provided for payment of mobilisation advance to the contractor against
bank guarantee and/ or hypothecation of machinery to be recovered alongwith interest
from him. The contract provided two alternative modes of securing the mobilisation
advance viz. hypothecation of contractor’s assets or bank guarantee. Hypothecation of
assets involves verification of title to the assets, its current market value,
encumbrances attached to it as well as Departmental vigilance to ensure that the
hypothecated assets are not clandestinely removed/ sold or alienated in any other
manner. Hence, bank guarantee should have been the preferred mode of securing the
advance, which is also provided in Central Vigilance Commission guidelines.
However, the EE paid mobilisation advance of % 3.55 crore to the contractor between
September 2007 to December 2008 against hypothecation of machinery. The
contractor took up execution of work with slow pace and completed less than one
Kilometre out of ten kilometers of canal length and thereafter abandoned the work in
December 2009. Though the Department served several notices (between June 2007
to September 2008) to the contractor for expediting the completion of work but no
action was taken against him in terms of the contract. Against the claim of work done
for & 1.55 crore, the contractor was paid (March 2009) ₹ 1.18 crore” leaving हैं 3.37
crore and interest thereon recoverable on account of outstanding mobilisation
advance from him. No action was taken to seize the hypothecated machinery and the
contractor removed the same from the site of work. Audit further noticed that the EE
without any requirement, advanced % 64.97 lakh to the Stores Procurement
26 M/s Mir Associates Construction Agency, Thathri (Doda)
Includes adjustment of mobilization advance of € 17.76 lakh
Includes work done liability of € 37 lakh

a7

20

Audit Report on Social, General, Revenue and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs) for the year ended 31 March 2012

Department, Jammu for procurement of construction material between December
2006 and May 2008. The material had not been received (March 2012).

After this was pointed out in audit, the EE stated (March 2012) that the matter would
be taken up with the higher authority for recovery of outstanding mobilisation
advance. The reply is not tenable as the Department had failed to take any action
against the contractor for abandoning the work midway and to seize the hypothecated
machinery.

Thus, failure of the Departmental authorities to ensure completion of allotted work
and subsequent inaction to seize the hypothecated machinery at the time of
abandonment of work by the contractor resulted in non-recovery/ adjustment of
mobilisation advance of = 3.37 crore for which the Department holds no security
besides, blocking of € 64.97 lakh.

The matter was referred to Government/ Department in August 2012; the reply had
not been received (December 2012).

Chapter-2-Audit Findings an Social, General and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs)

D. Chief Controlling Officer Based Audit of a Government Department

2.21 INDUSTRIES AND COMMERCE DEPARTMENT

The Industries and Commerce Department is responsible for promotion of
growth of industries, creation of new employment opportunities and
development of new designs in the Handloom and Handicraft sectors in the
State, besides optimum and sustainable usage of State mineral resources. A
Chief Controlling Officer-based audit of the Department brought to fore several
areas of concern: (a) Departmental failure of non-existent oversight resulting in
low industrial output, low employment generation, low-end and low value-
addition manufacturing despite fiscal burden of about € 8340 crore on the State
and Central exchequer during 2007-12, thus, defeating the objectives of the
Central/State Governments’ industrial promotion policies, (b) under-utilisation
of Central assistance, (c) unplanned execution of industrial estates/growth
centres, leading to unproductive investments, (d) inadmissible and irregular
payment of subsidies, VAT reimbursement and irregular exemption of toll tax,
(e) under-utilisation of training centres. Audit also noticed that performance of
implementation of schemes in Handicrafts and Handloom sector was poor and
the purpose of imparting training to unemployed persons for generating
employment through formation of co-operative societies was not achieved. The
Department had no mechanism in place to check the quantities of minerals
extracted and lifted by the lessees from the mineral sites.

Highlights

e Department had not conducted any industrial survey for assessing the
industrial potential including availability of human resources, raw
materials and marketing avenues, etc. It indicated that registration of new
industrial units was not based on any area development plans.

(Paragraph: 2.21.7)
¢ The Department was not exercising any oversight whether the industrial
units set up under the Central/State Governments industrial promotion
policies and provided with substantial tax concessions and subsidies were
indeed meeting the objectives. There is no mechanism in place to monitor
the turnover, value of output, profitability, extent and quality of
employment generation, actual new investment brought in and extent of
value addition in the manufacturing by the units so as to see whether these
are commensurate with the burden of tax concessions and subsidies on the
public exchequer besides, facilitating balanced geographically well-
dispersed growth.
(Paragraphs: 2.21.7 and 2.21.8)

e The performance of the new industrial units set-up under incentive
packages announced by the Central Government in 2002 and the State
Government in 2004 was not commensurate with the burden of fiscal
concessions and subsidies borne by the Central/State exchequer which
ranged between % 1289.89 crore to = 2475.88 crore during the years 2007-
08 to 2011-12. The total employment generated by all the industrial units in
the State was reported to be 1.03 lakh as of 2007-08, which had risen to 1.33
lakh at the close of the year 2011-12 and the 5312 industrial units
registered during 2007-08 to 2011-12 claimed to have provided employment

121


Audit Report on Social, General, Revenue and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs) for the year ended 31 March 2012

to 38,380 persons. The per capita fiscal burden for each worker claimed to
have been employed by all the industrial units (beneficiary or non-
beneficiary units) was between हैं 1.09 lakh and & 2.40 lakh per annum
during the same period clearly indicating near total Government funding
of the claimed payroll costs.
(Paragraph: 2.21.8.1)
e The manufacturing output of the beneficiary units has been low in absolute
terms and almost entirely matching the payroll cost, indicating prevalence
of low-tech manufacturing with low value-addition. The contribution of
beneficiary units to State’s GSDP was clearly less than the fiscal burden of
benefits provided to them. Total contribution to the State’s Gross Domestic
Product (GSDP) during 2007-08 to 2011-12 by all registered manufacturing
units was हैं 7149 crore while total fiscal burden of tax concessions and
subsidies on the Central and State exchequer provided to beneficiary units
was % 8340 crore (= 3118.74 crore on the State exchequer and हैं 5221 crore
on the Central exchequer). The fiscal incentives to the industrial units was
provided in the shape of subsidies, exemptions from payment of VAT and
Toll to the extent of ~ 3119 crore by the State Government and by way of
subsidies, Central Excise refund and income tax exemption amounting to
& 5221.37 crore by the Central Government. The contribution of average
annual workforce size of 1.17 lakh employees to the GSDP during the five
year period was at an average of just हैं 1.22 lakh per annum. These figures
indicated that the value addition by the beneficiary units is extremely low.
Relative share of the contribution of registered manufacturing sector of the
industry in the State’s GSDP has been declining in recent years despite
substantial burden of fiscal concessions on the Central/State exchequer.
The year-to-year growth in the contribution has been erratic.
(Paragraphs: 2.21.8 and 2.21.8.1)
e The utilization of Central funds ranged between 44 and 93 per cent. The
administrative expenditure in Handicraft and Handloom sector was high
and ranged between 83 and 93 per cent of the total expenditure.
An amount of हैं 15 crore released for the project National Institute of
Fashion Technology remained un-utilised since 2009-11 as the project had
not been approved.
(Paragraphs: 2.21.9 and 2.21.9.3)
e The geographical concentration of beneficiary units around Jammu in
close proximity to the railheads adjoining neighbouring States of Himachal
Pradesh and Punjab also indicated that the implementation of the State
Industrial Policy 2004 has not resulted in geographically well-dispersed
Industrial growth within the State. A total of 1779 (47 per cent) industrial
units spanning total land area of 12557 kanals (52 per cent) were located in
and around Jammu. So, besides employment generation, the State
Industrial Policy 2004 had also failed to address the issue of balanced
regional development within the State.
(Paragraph: 2.21.10)
e¢ The Department had not taken any action to retrieve the land from 1329
entrepreneurs (35 per cent) who had not established their industrial units
after the allotment of plots of land in their favour.
(Paragraph: 2.21.10)

2.211 Introduction

The Industries and Commerce Department is responsible for promotion of growth of
industries in the State through sustainable utilization of natural resources,
development of human skills and encouraging new investment in industries. The
creation of new employment opportunities and development of new designs in the
Handloom and Handicrafts wings and to enable the optimum and sustainable usage of
the state mineral resources are other functions of the Department. The Industrial
sector has been declared as main vehicle for generation of employment opportunities
in the State. The Handicrafts and Handloom sectors occupy an important place in the

123


Audit Report on Social, General, Revenue and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs) for the year ended 31 March 2012

socio economic structure of the State and make a significant contribution in sustaining
direct employment to the people of the State. The Geology and Mining sector of the
Department has been assigned the job of investigation of mineral deposits in the State
for their proper utilization, exploration of ground water and construction of tube
wells. Thus, four sectors i.e. Industries and Commerce sector, Handicrafts sector,
Handloom sector and Geology and Mining sector have been selected for the
performance audit.

2.21.3 Audit objectives

The objectives of the audit were to assess the performance of the Department to see
whether:-

e planning for sustainable industrial development in all regions of the State
was efficient;

¢ financial management was efficient and effective;

¢  guidelines/procedures prescribed for disbursement of subsidies to the
industrial units were adhered to;

¢ entrepreneurial friendly environment had been created to invite investors to
the State for setting up industrial ventures;

e action plan had been formulated for revival of potentially viable sick
industrial units;

124


Chapter-2-Audit Findings an Social, General and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs)

e human resource management was efficient and effective;

¢ the monitoring mechanism and internal control/audit mechanism were in
place and effective.

2.21.4 Audit criteria

Audit criteria for the CCO based audit of the Department has been derived from the
following sources:

¢ State Industrial Policy-2004 and packages of incentives of the Government
of India sanctioned in the year 2002.
e Guidelines of various Centrally Sponsored Schemes;

e J&K Financial Code and Book of Financial powers;

¢  Circulars/notifications issued by the Government from time to time.
2.21.5 Scope of Audit

Audit was conducted by test-check of records of the Commissioner/Secretary
Industries and Commerce Department; Directors, Industries and Commerce
(Kashmir and Jammu); Director, Handloom Development Department; Director
Handicrafts Development Department; Director, Geology and Mining Department,
Joint Director, Handicrafts, Jammu; General Managers of seven District Industries
Centres'; Assistant Directors, Handicraft, in eight districts’; Assistant Directors,
Handloom in nine districts, Coordinator, Geology and Mining, Kashmir; Sr. Drilling
Engineers, Kashmir and Jammu; Geologists (Grade-II) Kashmir as well as Jammu
and Assistant Mining Engineer, Jammu during the period from March to August
2012 covering the period from 2007-08 to 2011-12. The working of Autonomous
Bodies and Public Sector Undertakings (PSUs) under the administrative control of
the Chief Controlling Officer (Commissioner/Secretary Industries and Commerce
Department) had not been covered in the current review. The significant audit
findings in respect of Industries and Commerce, Handicrafts, Handloom and
Geology and Mining Wings of the Department have been brought out separately in
the succeeding paragraphs:

2.21.6 Audit methodology

An entry conference was held with the Commissioner/Secretary, Industries and
Commerce Department on 26 April 2012 wherein objectives, scope and criteria of
audit were discussed. The Audit findings and recommendations were discussed with
the Department representatives led by the Joint Director (Planning) of the Industries
and Commerce Department on 7 December 2012. The views and replies of the
Government have been incorporated in the report.

Industries and Commerce sector

The Industries and Commerce sector of the Department is responsible for creating
infrastructural facilities in the shape of fully developed industrial estates and growth
centres to encourage prospective entrepreneurs for establishing their industrial
ventures by allotting land in their favour. The organisation, in addition, provides the
facilities of registrations, training to entrepreneurs, liasioning with different sister

हि Selected on the basis of growth of industries and implementation of policy/programme in the respective

districts.
Assistant Directors, Handicrafts: Anantnag, Baramulla, Jammu, Kathua, Pulwama, Rajouri,
Srinagar and Udhampur

125


Audit Report on Social, General, Revenue and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs) for the year ended 31 March 2012

agencies, granting of different Central/ State Government incentives, etc. to the
industrial units in the State.

2.21.7 Planning

Proper planning is essential for success of the programme. The District Industries
Centres (DICs) were required to conduct and update the industrial survey of the
districts for assessing the industrial potential including availability of human
resources and raw material, marketing avenues and other factors for preparation of
Perspective Plan and Annual Plans at periodical intervals. Audit scrutiny revealed
that no such survey was conducted by the DICs of the Department. The Director,
Industries and Commerce, Jammu stated (July 2012) that the General Managers of
respective DICs would be asked to conduct such surveys. The Joint Director
Development, Directorate of Industries and Commerce, Kashmir, however, stated
(August 2012) that such surveys were conducted while identifying land for industrial
estates through field agencies. The Commissioner/Secretary reported (December
2012) that fresh potential survey had been conducted and communicated to the
Government of India by the District Industries Centres of the Kashmir valley. The
reply should be viewed in light of the fact that General Managers of three test-
checked DICs (Srinagar, Baramulla and Anantnag) had stated that no such survey
was conducted in the districts.

To accelerate industrial development of the State and boost investor-confidence, the
Government of India sanctioned special package of incentives in June 2002. To
supplement this, the State Government had also sanctioned (January 2004) its own
package of incentives under the State Industrial Policy 2004. One of the objectives
of State Industrial Policy 2004 was to generate 90 per cent employment in the
industrial units. The Department was not monitoring whether the industrial units set
up under the Central/State Government’ s industrial promotion policies and provided
with fiscal incentives and subsidies were indeed meeting the objective of
employment generation as laid down in the State Industrial Policy 2004. The
employment generated data was accepted as claimed by the industrial units without
any mechanism for verification being in place. There is no mechanism in place to
monitor the turnover, value of output, profitability, extent and quality of
employment generation, actual new investment brought in and extent of value
addition in the manufacturing by the units so as to see whether these are
commensurate with the burden of tax concessions and subsidies on the public
exchequer besides, facilitating balanced geographically well-dispersed growth as
evident from the succeeding paragraphs

2.21.81 Employment generation

For any Government, the first priority is to ensure that the population within its
jurisdiction has access to quality goods and services at reasonable prices. In
addition, it may try to seek production of such goods and services within its
jurisdiction as would provide jobs to locals. Hence, the sole yardstick to assess the
impact of Industrial Policy being pursued by the Central and State Government
involving fiscal concessions to industrial units is the job creation. The employment
generation was the basic objective of both the GOI package of incentives-2002 as
well as the State Industrial Policy 2004. The position of units registered and
employment generated during the period from 2007-08 to 2011-12 is given in Table
2.21.2 below:

year 2011-12 while as the financial incentives provided to the industrial units during
the years 2007-08 to 2011-12 ranged between % 1289.89 crore to % 2475.88 crore.
Thus, the per capita fiscal burden for each worker employed by the industrial units
(including those industrial units not covered by the State Industrial Policy 2004 and
packages of incentives) was between हैं 1.09 lakh and हैं 2.40 lakh per annum during
the same period.

2.21.10.1 Incomplete industrial growth centres/estates

¢ Audit scrutiny of records revealed that the Department had taken up execution of
IID Centre Govindsar (Kathua), Industrial estate, Ghatti (Kathua), Industrial
estate Lassipora (Pulwama), Food Park Doabgah (Sopore) and Common facility
centre Sather (Anantnag) in an unplanned manner viz. non-provision of electric
sub-station, not obtaining environmental clearance, non-providing of electric and
water supply facilities and non-installation of entire plant and machinery. This
resulted in non-completion of all the five projects, rendering the expenditure of
₹ 56.65 crore incurred thereon unproductive as detailed in Appendix-2.6. The
unplanned execution and consequent incompletion of these industrial estates and
growth centres deprived the prospective investors the opportunity to invest in the
projects for setting up industrial units in these centres/ estates.

¢ The indent for the acquisition of land measuring about 2763 kanals for phase-IIT
of the Industrial Growth Centre, Samba was placed in September 2000 by the
Director, Industries and Commerce to the Collector (Land acquisition). For this
purpose हैं 36.61 crore had been placed (up to March 2006: ₹ 5.18 crore;
2007-12: € 31.43 crore) at the disposal of Collector Land Acquisition, Samba
against the estimated cost of compensation of हैं 9.16 crore. The land had not
been acquired and the Additional Deputy Commissioner Samba, meanwhile,
revised (July 2011) the compensation amount required for land acquisition to
% 66.87 crore. As a result of non-acquisition of land, the Growth centre could not
be developed and हैं 36.61 crore continued to remain locked up. The Director,
Industries and Commerce, Jammu stated (July 2012) that the sub-committee of
MLCs had been constituted for settlement of the issue. Further progress in the
matter was awaited (December 2012).

© Seven industrial estates (Lamberi, Surankote, Beoli, Bidda, Majalta, Sunderbani
and Mandipora) could not be established despite delay ranging between five to
eight years from the date of placement of indents due to non-acquisition of
identified land by the Revenue Department. As a result, = 88.20 lakh advanced

Land Acquisition, Doda: हैं 50 lakh) during the years from 2003-04 to 2007-08
remained unutilized as of March 2012. The Directors, Industries and Commerce,
Kashmir as well as Jammu stated (August/July 2012) that steps had been taken
to expedite the process of acquisition of land. The reply should be seen in light
of the fact that despite delay of five to eight years, land had not been acquired by
the Department

2.21.11 Implementation of packages of incentives

The packages of incentive of the Government of India (GOI) provide for 15 per cent
capital investment subsidy on plant and machinery; three per cent capital subsidy on
working capital loan; 90 per cent transport subsidy from designated railhead; 100
per cent insurance premium on capital investment and income tax exemption upto
the year 2012 while as the incentive packages by the State Government provide for
100 per cent subsidy on pre-investment studies/preparation of feasibility reports
with maximum limit of हैं two lakh; toll tax exemptions on import of raw material
and export of finished goods; procurement of plant and machinery; building material
and other equipments for a period of five years from the date of registration;
exemption from earnest money/security deposits to the prescribed extent; exemption
from the levy of General Sales Tax/Value Added Tax; 100 per cent subsidy for
purchase of new Diesel Generator sets, etc.

2.21111 Inadmissible payment of transport subsidy

© To promote growth of industries in the State, the GOI had launched (July 1971)
a scheme for grant of subsidy in the shape of transport cost of raw material and
finished goods between locations of industrial units and rail heads of Jammu or
Pathankot whichever was nearer to the site of industrial unit. The scheme had
however, been closed by the GOI during 2010-11. With the establishment of
Kathua railway station in the year 1980, this station became nearer to the
industrial sites in Kathua by 5 kms as compared to the Pathankot railhead which
was 25 kms from these location. Accordingly, the name of Pathankot railhead
was to be got replaced by the Kathua railhead by issuance of necessary
amendments/notification to the scheme. Inaction of the Director, Industries and

135


Audit Report on Social, General, Revenue and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs) for the year ended 31 March 2012

Commerce, Jammu led to avoidable extra payment of transport subsidy of हें 3.17
crore to 12 industrial units located in Kathua during the years 2008-09 and
2009-10. The Director, Industries and Commerce, Jammu stated (June 2012) that
re-designation of railhead, Kathua in place of Pathankot had been taken up with
the GOI. The reply should be seen in light of the fact that the matter was taken
up with the GOI in December 2011 after the closure of the scheme.

e The guidelines of transport subsidy scheme envisaged that the subsidy on
account of transport cost was to be allowed to the industrial units engaged in
manufacturing activity. The Special Bench of Income Tax Appellate Tribunal
(ITAT) Pune, had held (June 2008) that the process of pasteurization and
standardization of milk amounts to processing but do not amount to manufacture
as it did not alter the nature and character of milk. Test-check of records showed
that the Director, Industries and Commerce, Jammu wrongly allowed transport
subsidy of हैं 95.73 lakh to M/S Aneja Dairy, Kathua engaged in processing of
raw milk into skimmed milk during the period 2008-12. The Director, Industries
and Commerce Department, Jammu stated (June 2012) that it was not possible to
segregate the utilization of raw material for manufacturing different items of
finished goods. The reply should be seen in light of the fact that consumption
statement submitted by the General Manager, DIC, Kathua clearly indicated
consumption of raw material and production of finished goods.

e M/S Aneja Food Products, Kathua was registered for manufacture of skimmed
milk powder, whole milk powder and Dairy whitener from skimmed milk. It was
seen in audit that M/S Aneja Dairy, Kathua, a sister concern of M/S Aneja Food
Products, Kathua did not export any quantity of skimmed milk outside the State
and when sufficient quantity of skimmed milk was available with this unit, under
such situation, the import of skimmed milk from outside state by M/S Aneja
Food Products, Kathua was not justifiable. This fact was not kept in view by the
Director Industries and Commerce, Jammu which resulted in avoidable payment
of subsidy of = 70.50 lakh to M/S Aneja Food Products, Kathua on transport cost
of skimmed milk during the years from 2008-09 to 2009-10. The Director,
Industries and Commerce, Jammu stated (June 2012) that there was no
stipulation in the guidelines that a unit cannot import raw material when its sister
unit was producing it. The reply was not acceptable as the transport subsidy was
to be paid judiciously and that the quantity of skimmed milk processed by sister
concern was more than the quantity of requirement of the unit.

2.21,11.2 Irregular payment of interest and capital investment subsidies

e According to the Government instructions (October 1998), the change of
manufacturing activities by the entrepreneurs at the site of their allotted land was
to be considered only in unavoidable circumstances due to valid reasons. The
Directorate of Industries and Commerce further directed (December 2006) that
change of constitution or change of line of activity should not be accorded to any
industrial unit except in unavoidable circumstances for valid reasons and that too
with the prior approval of the Director. The change of manufacturing activity
would render such industrial unit ineligible for financial assistance of any type.
Test-check of records, however, showed that the Director, Industries and
Commerce, Jammu allowed change of manufacturing activities in favour of 7
industrial units without any valid reasons and allowed subsidies of % 3.39 crore
in the shape of capital investment, interest and subsidies on purchase of DG sets
and testing laboratory equipments during the years from 2006-07 to 2011-12.

136


Chapter-2-Audit Findings an Social, General and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs)

The General Manager, DIC, Jammu stated (August 2012) that the unit holders
were within their rights to change line of activity and were entitled to all
incentives, The General Manager, DIC, Kathua stated (July 2012) that the
change of activity was accorded by the Directorate. The replies were indicative
of the fact that departmental authorities were not aware of the instructions issued
by the Government.

The Ministry of Industry, Government of India re-classified (September 1991)
various activities into Small Scale Industry (SSI) and Small Scale Service
Business Enterprise (SSSBE) to be eligible for benefits/concessions admissible
to Small Scale Sector. Accordingly, the activities like diagnostic health centre,
restaurants, contractors, retail/wholesale establishments which were not
classified as SSSBE were not eligible for benefits/concessions admissible to
Small Scale Sector. It was, however, seen in audit that the Department wrongly
allowed subsidies of हैं 83.07 lakh on DG sets and quality control equipment to
18 SSSBEs during the period 2007-12. After this was pointed out by Audit, the
Joint Director, Industries (Development), Kashmir stated (August 2012) that all
such activities fall within the category of SSSBE and qualified for incentives.
The reply was contrary to the GOI instructions of September 1991

The GOI package of incentives provide for 15 per cent capital investment
subsidy on plant and machinery subject to ceiling of हैं 30 lakh to newly
established industrial units provided that the unit had not taken any of the
effective steps’ prior to 14 June 2002. Test-check of records revealed that seven
industrial units had taken up effective steps prior to 14 June 2002 and were not
entitled for capital investment subsidy. The Director, Industries and Commerce,
Jammu, however, paid capital investment subsidy of % 47.49 lakh to these seven
ineligible units during September 2006 to March 2008 in violation of the scheme
guidelines. The Director, Industries and Commerce, Jammu stated (July 2012)
that subsidy was paid to these units keeping in view the date of commencement
of production after 14 June 2002. The reply was not acceptable in view of the
guidelines of package of incentives of the GOI.

As per State Industrial Policy 2004, the Capital Investment Subsidy (CIS)
scheme of the State Government was to be replaced by the Government of India
scheme for CIS. The GOI scheme was applicable to the industrial units situated
in the notified areas of the State. The State Government was to extend the
benefits to the industrial units located in un-notified areas where the CIS scheme
of GOT was not applicable. Test-check of records revealed that three industrial
units operating in notified areas of industrial estates of Gangyal and Kathua were
irregularly paid = 45.11 lakh on account of capital investment subsidy under the
State policy during 2009-10 to 2011-12 by the General Managers, DIC Jammu
and Kathua. The General Manager, DIC, Jammu stated (August 2012) that the
units in question do not fall in the notified area and as such were entitled for
capital investment subsidy under the State policy. The reply was not acceptable
as these units had been operating in industrial estate, Gangyal which was notified
by the Government of India. The General Manager, DIC, Kathua stated (July
2012) that matter would be looked into and necessary action would be taken
accordingly.

¢ The guidelines envisage that if the industrial unit had gone out of production
within five years after its commencement of production or after availing interest
subsidy, the capital investment and interest subsidies paid were to be recovered
from such unit. Test-check of records of DICs, Samba and Udhampur, however,
showed that the Director, Industries and Commerce, Jammu had not taken any
action to recover the subsidies to the tune of हैं 40.79 lakh paid to three industrial

e The State Industrial Policy 2004 envisages 100 per cent capital subsidy on
installation of testing equipment subject to maximum of हैं 25 lakh and the
subsidy amount was to be disbursed to the industrial units only after obtaining
National/International quality marks from Bureau of Indian Standards (BIS) and
Indian Standard Institution (ISI). Test-check of records showed that the
Department had disbursed capital subsidy of रैं 2.41 crore to 23 industrial units as
detailed in Appendix -2.7 during January 2008 to January 2012 in violation of
the State industrial Policy 2004 as these units had not obtained
National/International quality mark from these Institutions. The General
Manager, DIC Kathua accepted (July 2012) Audit contention and stated that
procedure would be followed in future, while the General Manger, DIC,
Udhampur issued (August 2012) notices to the defaulting industrial units to
submit the requisite certificates.

2.21.11.3 Inadmissible VAT reimbursements

As per the Government order (June 2005), the Small Scale Industrial (SSI) units
were to be provided relief under the Value Added Tax (VAT) regime on purchase of
raw materials/inputs through SICOP/J&K Minerals Limited/any other Public Sector
Undertakings of the State Government. Such corporations were not to charge VAT
from the SST units and instead claim financial support to the extent of VAT paid on
such products by them from the Department.

© The ceiling’ limit on investment in Plant and Machinery was pre-requisite for the
purpose of registration of unit as SSI unit. Test-check of records showed that
three SSI units had undervalued their plant and machinery and restricted their
value within the prescribed ceiling limits by misclassification in their
accounts/Balance sheets. As a result of misrepresentation of facts by these unit
holders, the Administrative Department had to reimburse % 26.56 crore °
including unpaid liability of € 12.41 crore”” on account of VAT on purchase of
raw material through SICOP during the period from April 2005 to March 2012.
The General Manager, DIC, Samba stated (August 2012) that the value of plant
and machinery of M/S Ritzy Polymers was certified by the Chartered
Accountant. The reply was not acceptable as the Project Manager was to

1996: ₹ 0.60 crore; 1997: र Three crore; 1999: ₹ one crore

M/S Ritzy Polymers, Samba: हैं 23.84 crore; M/S Yamuna Alloys Limited; Jammu: हैं 2.38 crore and
M/S Komal Paper Mills Limited: % 0.34 crore

For the period from February 2011 to March 2012

138


Chapter-2-Audit Findings an Social, General and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs)

physically verify the installation of plant and machinery and certify its value
before formal registration of the industrial unit. The General Manager (DIC),
Kathua stated (July 2012) that appropriate action would be taken after detailed
investigation of the case. Further progress in the matter was awaited (December
2012).

e Audit noticed that M/S J&K Pulp and Paper Private Limited, Jammu registered
as medium and large scale industrial unit during the year 1996-97, made
investment of हैं 4.41 crore in plant and machinery up to March 2003. The
General Manager, DIC, Jammu changed (March 2009) the status of the unit to
SSI unit in terms of the Micro, Small and Medium Enterprises (MSME) Act
2006. This Act, however, does not provide for conversion of already registered
medium and large industry into SSI unit. The change of status of the SST unit by
incorrect application of provisions of MSME Act, 2006 by the Director
Industries and Commerce, Jammu resulted in inadmissible reimbursement of
VAT of & 21.22 lakh"! on purchase of raw material through SICOP to the
industrial unit during August 2011 to May 2012. The General Manager, District
Industries Centre, Jammu stated (August 2012) that such conversion of industrial
unit was accorded by the Director, Industries and Commerce, Jammu.

2.21.11.4 Irregular toll tax exemption

e¢ The Government by a notification dated 31 January 2004 exempted registered
industrial units from the payment of additional toll on raw material/ consumables
procured from outside the State and on finished goods manufactured/exported by
these units and sent outside, except for items included in the list, henceforth,
called negative list, which included all kinds of oils (edible and non-edible),
excluding oil seeds. Audit noticed that eight industrial units were irregularly
allowed exemption from the payment of additional toll to the extent of = 1.07
crore on import of 24193.17 MTs of Mentha oil being non-edible oil during
January 2006 to March 2012. The General Manager, DIC, Jammu stated (August
2012) that Mentha oil is a medicinal produce and used in pharma industry,
cosmetic industry and health care products. The reply should be seen in light of
the fact that Mentha oil being non-edible oil was not eligible for exemption from
payment of toll tax as per notification of the Government. The General Manager,
DIC, Kathua stated (July 2012) that matter would be taken up with the Deputy
Excise Commissioner, Toll Post Lakhanpur, Jammu.

e The term ‘manufacture’ as defined in the Government notification of August
1998 means a process by which raw material is converted into an entirely
different commodity. M/S Aneja Dairy, Kathua engaged in manufacture of desi
ghee, butter, cream and skimmed milk was wrongly allowed exemption of toll
tax of हैं 4.02 crore on import of 88484 MTs of raw milk used for processing it
into skimmed milk during the period from January 2007 to December 2011.This
resulted in inadmissible exemption from payment of additional toll of = 4.02
crore to the industrial unit. The General Manager, DIC, Kathua stated (July
2012) that matter would be taken up with the Deputy Excise Commissioner, Toll
Post, Lakhanpur, Jammu.

e The Madras High court in the case of Computer Graphics Pvt. Ltd. versus Union
of India had held (November 1990) that making out photographic flats or films
from jumbo rolls by cutting it into smaller pieces is not a manufacturing process.

” Including unpaid liability of € 5.05 lakh from January 2012 to March 2012

139


Audit Report on Social, General, Revenue and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs) for the year ended 31 March 2012

The slitting of Jumbo rolls of photographic paper, X-ray films into different sizes
did not alter the nature and character of paper and therefore did not fall within
the purview of manufacturing activity. M/S Jindal Photo Limited, Samba was
registered as a manufacturing unit for slitting of Jumbo rolls of photographic
paper into different sizes and as such its activity was not a manufacturing
activity in light of the above referred judgment. The General Manager, DIC,
Samba, however, wrongly allowed exemption of % 49.08 lakh on account of toll
tax on import of 4850.80 MTs of raw material and export of 7419.87 MTs of
finished goods during the period from January 2005 to December 2008 in favour
of M/S Jindal Photo Limited Samba.

2.21.12 Lack of environmental concerns

2.21.12.1 The State Government adopted policy for establishment of
insecticides/ pesticides industrial units in the State on the recommendation (February
2005) of the State level Apex Projects Clearance Committee. The policy provisions
provide to make mandatory for the insecticides/pesticides industrial units to obtain
ISO 14000 ” certification within a period of two years from the date of
commencement of commercial production and in the event of their failure to do so,
the registration of such units was to be cancelled. Further, on the instructions
(December 2006) of the Hon'ble Chief Minister of the State, the Director of
Industries and Commerce placed (January 2007) activity of manufacturing of
pesticides, insecticides and weedicides on the restricted list in view of the hazardous
nature of these units for health of the people. This activity was banned (2003) by the
State Government of neighboring State Himachal Pradesh. Audit scrutiny of records,
however, showed that pesticides/insecticides/weedicides industrial units established
in the State during the years from 2006-07 to 2010-11 had not obtained ISO 14000
certification even after completion of period ranging from two to six years from the
date of commencement of production of these industrial units. The Director
Industries and Commerce, Jammu instead of taking action against such industrial
units provided incentives in the form of various subsidies to the extent of = 9.47
crore to 15 industrial units during the period 2008-12. This indicated casual
approach of the Department towards health of the people. The Director, Industries
and Commerce Department stated (September 2012) that it was not clear whether
any formal order was issued. However, all the concerned General Managers had
been instructed to obtain requisite ISI-14000 certificates.

2.21.12.2 The J&K State Pollution Control Board granted (March 2008)
consent to operate in favour of M/S Cecil Pharmaceuticals Private Limited valid up
to November 2008. The Director, Industries and Commerce, Jammu and General
Manager, DIC, Jammu did not demand renewed consent to operate from the
industrial unit and instead paid subsidies of = 65.67 lakh on account of capital
investment, generator set and testing equipment in favour of the unit from December
2008 to March 2010. The J&K State Pollution Control Board ordered (February
2011) closure of the unit in view of its unauthorized operation since December 2008
The General Manager, DIC, Jammu stated (August 2012) that a notice had been
issued to the unit holder for refund of subsidy. The reply should be seen in light of
non-fulfillment of required formalities by the Director, Industries and Commerce,
Jammu and General Manager, DIC, Jammu before sanctioning of subsidies.

2.21.13 Poor performance of training programme

2.21.13.1 The Department was to organize training programmes for creating
trained and skilled class of personnel for absorption by self employment. The
Department has 62 training centres with capacity to train 1860 personnel in a year.
The position of number of persons trained and the industrial units set up by trained
personnel in the State during the period from 2007-08 to 2011-12 is detailed in
Table 2.21.7 below:

During the period from 2007-08 to 2011-12 total number of 7433 personnel (80 per
cent) were trained against the capacity for training 9300 personnel revealing under-
utilization of the capacity of the training centres. The position was no different in the
six test-checked DICs where 3708 personnel were trained in 31 training centres
against their capacity to trained 5150 personnel. Audit further noticed that none of
the trained personnel had set up their own units or adopted the trade as profession,
rendering expenditure of % 9.01 crore incurred on training of these 3778 personnel
unproductive. Thus, the objective to create skilled entrepreneurs for self
employment had not been achieved. The Director, Industries and Commerce, Jammu
stated (July 2012) that training programme was primarily for skill development and
not for setting up of units. The reply was not tenable as the purpose of training was
to create skilled class of personnel for absorption mostly by self employment.

2.21.13.2 The Footwear Demonstration Training Centre, Srinagar remained
non-functional since inception as no training had been imparted in this centre. Due
to the training centre remaining non-functional, the General Manager, DIC, Srinagar
had paid idle wages of हैं 96.29 lakh to the staff of the centre during the period 2007-
12. The General Manager, DIC, Srinagar stated (August 2012) that the matter had
been taken up with the Director, Industries and Commerce, Kashmir.

2.21.14 Poor follow up for revival of sick units

The State Industrial Policy 2004 envisaged rehabilitation of sick industrial units. For
this purpose, the soft loans were to be provided to the sick industrial units to meet
the liabilities on account of power, sales tax, etc. The total amount of soft loan
sanctioned was not to exceed 30 per cent of total loan requirement of the sick unit.
The loan was to be disbursed through the SIDCO. Further, to reduce interest liability
of unit holders, the Government was to pay interest subsidy to the extent of rates of
interest prescribed by the SIDCO reduced by one per cent thereof. The position of
units approved for revival, funds released, soft loan disbursed, loan sanctioned by
banks and outstanding repayment of loan and interest as of March 2012 is given in
Table 2.21.8 below:

2.21.15 Non-constitution of Permanent Grievance Redressal Committee

According to the State Industrial Policy 2004, the Finance Department was to issue
an order to the effect that no consignment of goods meant for industries was to be
detained at the entry point of the State for more than 24 hours. A notice was to be
issued by the concerned authority to the firm outlining the objections in the event of
any doubt but the consignment of goods was to be released instantly. For this
purpose, a Permanent Grievance Redressal Committee consisting of Director
Industries and Commerce, Commissioner Commercial Taxes and Commissioner
Excise was to be set up by the Finance Department and the Committee was to meet
once every fortnight to consider all such notices and pass orders which were final
and binding on all concerned. It was, however, seen in audit that no such committee
had been set up. The Commissioner/ Secretary reported (December 2012) that the
Grievance Redressal Committee had been constituted in March 2012 by the
Government.

Handicrafts Sector

Handicraft activities occupy an important position in the economic structure of the
State. The Handicraft Department is mandated with the task of imparting training to
unemployed persons in various crafts and to organize these trainees into co-
operative societies, increase production capacity of artisans by introducing new
techniques and designs and providing marketing facilities for the products of these
societies through organization of exhibitions, expos, crafts bazaars, etc.

2.21.16 Planning

Proper planning is essential for success of the programme. A long term Perspective
Plan is an appropriate framework to prioritize funds and serve both as a roadmap
and a long term Action Plan against which actual transfer of intended benefits of the
programme can flow and be consistent with the prioritization and goals set forth in
the Perspective Plan. It was, however, seen that no long term Perspective Plan as
well as Annual Action Plans had been formulated by the Department. The Director,
Handicrafts, J&K stated (August 2012) that the Department prepared Annual Action
Plan as per requirement and as per funds allocated by the Planning and Development
Department. The reply should be seen in the light of fact that no instructions had
been issued to the Assistant Directors, Handicrafts for preparation of District Annual
Plans.

Chapter-2-Audit Findings an Social, General and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs)

2.21.17 Poor performance of trainings

The Department runs elementary and advanced training centres wherein unemployed
personnel are imparted training and after completion of training courses, the trainees
are to be organized into cooperative societies through industrial co-operative wing of
the Department. The Department runs 464 training centres (Jammu Province: 145;
Kashmir Province: 319) for imparting training in 33 crafts. The position of intake
capacity of these training centres, number of persons trained and formation of
cooperative societies during the years from 2007-08 to 2011-12 is given in Table

Directors, Handicrafts” stated (March/April/June 2012) that it was not possible to
recover the stipend from the drop out trainees or trainees who had not established
their units.

2.21.17.1 Non-ayailability of raw material for trainings

For smooth running of training programmes, the Department was to ensure
availability of raw material viz. woolen and cotton yarn, sheesham wood, etc. in
advance of the commencement of trainings. Test-check of records revealed that
during the years 2008-09 to 2011-12, raw materials were purchased at the fag end of
each year and as such could not be made available to the training centres in time.
Further, raw material such as woolen, cotton yarn and sheesham wood were not
procured during 2007-08 to 2011-12. Audit further noticed that in 57 training centres
(carpet weaving centres: 17, leather cum lacquer centres: 7, paper mashie: 19 and
wood carving: 14), the raw material was not made available during the years 2007-
08 to 2011-12. The imparting of training to the trainees without raw materials was
doubtful. The Joint Director, Handicrafts, Jammu stated (April 2012) that raw
material could not be procured in time due to non- finalization of rate contract by
State Level Purchase Committee and due to delayed release of funds. The Joint
Director, Handicrafts, Kashmir stated (September 2012) that the raw material
procured during previous years were provided to the training centres. The reply was
not acceptable as raw material for paper machine and wood carving craft were not
procured during 2007-08 to 2010-11.

2.21.17.2 Non-disposal of finished goods

The finished goods valuing % 63.27 lakh manufactured by trainees in eight test-
checked districts were lying in the stores as of March 2012 and out of this the
finished goods valuing = 25.62 lakh got damaged due to prolonged storage. The
Department had not taken steps for disposal of these finished goods. The Assistant
Directors, Handicrafts stated (March and June 2012) that the accumulation of
finished goods was due to lack of demand from buyers for these goods and the
matter regarding damaged goods had already been taken up with the higher
authorities for writing off the value of these damaged goods.

The industrial cooperative wing of the Department is mandated with the task of
organizing successful trainees into co-operative societies to generate employment in
Handicraft sector. For the growth of these co-operative societies, the Department
provides assistance in the shape of managerial subsidy and interest subsidy on
institutional finance. The status of Co-operative Societies registered with the

2.21.18 Poor performance of credit plan scheme

To arrest extinction of Handicraft industry in the State and to boost economic
conditions of craftsmen and artisans, the State Government had formulated a special
credit plan to provide financial assistance to them for setting up of self employment
generating units at affordable terms. The scheme envisages 90 per cent financial
assistance in the form of loan from banks and 10 per cent promoters’ contribution for
setting up of units. Interest subsidy to the extent of five per cent on the loan
component was to be provided by the State Government.

The position of number of cases sponsored for loan, cases sanctioned and loan
disbursed in eight test-checked districts under the credit plan scheme during the

2.21.19 Improper implementation of Central schemes

To provide the artisans community access to the best of healthcare facilities and to
provide life insurance protection to them, the GOI launched ‘Rajiv Gandhi Shilpi
Swastha Bima Yojana’ and ‘Bima Yojana for Handicraft Artisans’ for implementation
during the 11" five year plan period (2007-12). The Scheme ‘Rajiv Gandhi Shilpi
Swastha Bima Yojana’ envisaged that expenses incurred by the artisans for medical
treatment availed of in any hospital or nursing home within the country would be
paid/reimbursed by the Insurance Company. For this purpose, the GOI was to
contribute हैं 650 per annum in case of General Category artisans and हैं 725 per annum
in case of artisans belonging to SC/ST/BPL and NER. The General Category artisans
were to contribute हैं 150 per annum and the artisans belonging to SC/ST / BPL and
NER categories were to contribute हैं 75 in each case. The Department was to
implement the scheme by way of sensitizing the handicraft artisans to join the
scheme. The position of coverage of artisans was very poor in test-checked districts of
Kashmir Province as only 2771 artisans (19 per cent) were covered under the scheme
against the target of 14875 artisans during the period 2007-12. Further, only 246
artisans were covered under the scheme during the period 2010-12 in the districts of
Jammu, Kathua and Udhampur and not even a single artisan was covered in these
districts during the period 2007-10 under the scheme. The Assistant Directors
Handicraft attributed (March/April/June 2012) poor implementation of the scheme to
lack of interest shown by the artisans.

Another scheme ‘Bima Yojana for Handicraft Artisans’ envisages life insurance
coverage against natural death (% 30,000), accidental death or permanent total
disability (र 75,000) and permanent partial disability (€ 37500). The additional
benefits of an associated scheme of LIC ‘Shiksha Sahyog Yojana’ which envisages
education allowance of हैं 300 per quarter per child studying in classes 9" to 12" upto
maximum of two children for a maximum period of four years or till they completed
12" standard whichever event occurs earlier were to be provided to these artisans.
Under the scheme, the annual premium charged was to be हैं 200 per annum per person
of which हैं 100 was to be borne by LIC from its Social Security Fund, € 60 were to be
borne by the GOI and remaining = 40 were to be borne by the beneficiary. For
successful implementation of the scheme, the State Government was to motivate
artisans to join the scheme. It was, however, noticed that the scheme had not been
implemented in four test-checked districts of Jammu Province while as the position of
implementation of the scheme was very poor in four test-checked districts of Kashmir
province as only 1011 artisans (seven per cent) were covered against the target of
14875 artisans during the period 2007-12. The Department had not taken any steps to
make the artisans aware about the benefits of the scheme. The Assistant Directors,
Handicrafts attributed (March/April 2012) poor implementation of the scheme to lack
of interest shown by the artisans.

Handloom sector

The Handloom sector occupies a significant place in the socio-economic structure of
the State and is known for weaving of fabrics like Pashmina, Raffel, silk sarees and
cotton items. It plays a crucial role in employment generation for weaker sections of
society. The Handloom Development Department plays a catalyst role in
modernization of Handloom sector in the State through skill up-gradation of weavers

146


Chapter-2-Audit Findings an Social, General and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs)

by providing modernized looms, training in designs and weaving, marketing of
handloom products through market incentives and by fostering participation of
weavers in National, State and District level handloom expos and melas.

2.21.20 Planning

To promote handloom activities by way of imparting trainings to unemployed youth
in various crafts and forming co-operative societies to assess the latest trends in the
market, the Department was to organize its activities through formulation of
Perspective Plan out of which the Annual Action Plans were to be carved out. It was,
however, seen in audit that the Department had not formulated any Perspective Plan.
The baseline survey for preparing the profile of the cluster i.e. number of active
handlooms, type of handlooms, number of weavers, type of yarn used, product range
and average weaver income, etc. was required to be conducted. Audit noticed that the
Annual Action Plans (AAPs) had not been formulated and no base line survey was
conducted in any of the test-checked districts. The Deputy Director, Planning,
Handloom, attributed (September 2012) non-formulation of long term Perspective
Plan to availability of limited resources of infrastructure and manpower with the
Department,

2.21.21. Poor performance of schemes
2.21.21.1 Integrated Handloom Development Scheme

To facilitate the sustainable development of handloom weavers located in or outside
identified clusters into a cohesive, self-managing and competitive socio-economic
unit, the Government of India had launched a Centrally sponsored scheme ‘Integrated
Handloom Development Scheme (IHDS)’ to be implemented during the 117 Five
Year Plan period (2007-12). Under this scheme, 10 cluster projects (estimated cost:
% 5.91 crore) and 17 Group Approach Projects (estimated cost: % 1.21 crore) were
sanctioned by the GOI during 2007-08 and 2009-11 respectively. For the cluster
projects of the scheme, the guidelines envisage formation of consortium involving
stake holders from the Self Help Groups (SHGs), co-operative societies, master
weavers, private entrepreneurs, NGOs for interaction with the weavers, etc.

To ensure regular availability of yarn of requisite count and to avoid delay in
production process, one time assistance of & three lakh per cluster was to be provided
as a corpus fund to National Handloom Development Corporation (NHDC) to ensure
supply of yarn to the weavers against payment through yarn depots at the cluster.
Further, to provide facilities of pre-loom and post-loom operations, quality control
laboratory, common work shed, water treatment and effluent treatment plants to
weavers, common facility centre was to be set up in each cluster.

It was noticed that consortium was formed from weavers of the area only and did not
involve SHGs, private entrepreneurs, cooperative societies and NGOs. The Assistant
Director, Handlooms Kathua stated (July 2012) that necessary steps would be taken to
include SHGs, NGOs, private entrepreneurs, etc. in the consortium.

Further, no yarn depot was set up and no corpus fund was maintained in any of the
test-checked clusters as yarn was supplied to weavers free of cost. The yarn supplied
was also utilized for imparting training and for making samples which defeated the
purpose of creation of corpus fund.

147


Audit Report on Social, General, Revenue and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs) for the year ended 31 March 2012

Audit also noticed that no common facility centre was set up. For cluster Samba, the
building (estimated cost: हैं 24.90 lakh) was constructed only upto slab level at a cost
of € 6.50 lakh. The remaining portion of the building could not be completed due to
non-release of balance funds. Further, for cluster Billawar, the execution of building
could not be taken up due to non-release of funds. The guidelines of the scheme
further envisage setting up of Computer Aided Textile Design (CATD) System,
Colour Forecast and Trend Forecast in each cluster to develop designs for weavers.
For this purpose, a qualified designer was to be engaged in each cluster. Though the
Computer system was procured in each cluster, its utilization could not be confirmed
as records of designs developed had not been maintained. The guidelines further
envisage providing of assistance for individual weavers for basic inputs such as
margin money, looms and construction of work sheds. Records showed that the
margin money of % 8.73 lakh released between December 2009 to March 2012 in
respect of two clusters (Samba and Billawar) remained unutilized in bank accounts as
the working capital loans had not been sanctioned by the banks. Further, the
utilization of हैं 16.56 lakh meant for purchase of 88 looms and construction of 18
work sheds in these two clusters was not ensured by the Department. Test-check of
records showed that in Billawar Cluster, the raw material required for imparting
training to weavers in weaving, dyeing and designing was procured after the
completion of trainings. Imparting of training to weavers in the absence of raw
material was thus, doubtful. Monitoring report of the Government of India also
indicated that the training in weaving was imparted for 15 days only against
prescribed 60 days. The Assistant Director, Handlooms, Kathua stated (June 2012)
that the matter would be got investigated

For Group Approach Projects (GAPs), the guidelines of the scheme envisage
providing of assistance for individual weavers for basic inputs such as margin money,
purchase of looms and construction of work sheds. Test-check of records showed that
margin money of © 7.75 lakh had not been released in favour of the weavers and
remain unutilized, as working capital loans had not been sanctioned by the banks in
respect of eight Group approach projects. Similarly, proper utilization of = 30.44 lakh
meant for purchase of looms and construction of work sheds was not ensured by the
Assistant Directors (Jammu, Udhampur, Kathua and Rajouri) as would be revealed
from the position of these GAPs given in Appendix -2.8. Three GAPs (Nagrota Preta,
Parnalla and Dadwara) had procured raw material required for imparting training to
weavers after the completion of trainings. As such, the imparting of training in
absence of raw material and payment of stipend of हैं 2.57 lakh to weavers was
doubtful.

The Assistant Directors, (Jammu, Udhampur, Kathua and Rajouri) had not maintained
any record of pre and post cluster interventions such as average increase in number of
working days, average increase in wages of weavers per month, total turnover of the
cluster, increase in productivity per weaver, etc. in any of the test-checked cluster
projects as well as GAPs. Due to non-maintenance of these vital records, the
achievement made by these clusters could not be ascertained. The Assistant Directors,
Handlooms (Jammu, Udhampur, Kathua and Rajouri) accepted (June/July 2012) the
audit observations and stated that needful would be done to address the issue.

2.21.21.2 Handloom weavers welfare scheme

To provide enhanced insurance cover to the handloom weavers in case of natural as
well as accidental death and in case of total or partial disability, the GOI launched

148


Chapter-2-Audit Findings an Social, General and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs)

‘Mahatma Gandhi Bunkar Bima Yojana’ (MGBBY) for implementation during 117
five year Plan period. The scheme was to be administered by the Life Insurance
Corporation of India and the authority in charge of Handloom in the State was to
finalize insurance coverage of weavers with the LIC of India. Under the scheme, the
annual premium of हैं 330 per member was to be shared by GOI (® 150), weaver
(₹ 80) and LIC (₹ 100). It was, however, observed that the coverage of weavers in the
State under the scheme was very poor and ranged between four and 20 per cent of the
targets fixed. Against the target of 26000 weavers to be covered under the scheme
during the period 2007-12, only 2546 (10 per cent) weavers were covered by the
Department. The Director, Handloom stated (September 2012) that the scheme was
implemented by the LIC which was to organize awareness camps to educate
beneficiaries. This indicated that the Department had not sensitized the weavers to
join the scheme through public awareness programmes.

2.21.22 Non-monitoring of scheme of modernization of looms

The scheme of ‘Modernization of looms’ envisage that assistance in the shape of loan,
subsidy to modernize the looms and for purchase of accessories for looms was to be
provided to weavers of primary Handloom societies. The assistance was to be given in
the shape of loan and subsidy in the ratio of 50:50 basis and the loan amount was
recoverable in 10 equal yearly installments. Test-check of records revealed that loan
of & 45.20 lakh was provided to 229 societies of the State for purchase, modernization
and renovation of looms during the period 2007-12. The Department had not ensured
that loan released to societies was actually utilized on procurement and renovation of
looms. The Assistant Directors, Handlooms stated (June/July 2012) that installation of
looms by weavers could not be verified due to acute shortage of staff in the
Department.

2.21.23 Non-achievement of objective of training programme

For achieving the objective of employment generation in Handloom Sector, the
Department imparts training in various crafts to the unemployed. For this purpose,
the Department runs training centres wherein the unemployed are imparted training
and after completion of training courses, the trainees are to be organized into co-

2.21.24 Non-verification of physical existence of units

To check the traditional industry of Handloom from extinction and to boost
economic conditions of craftsmen and artisans, the State Government formulated a
special credit plan to provide financial assistance to weavers for setting up their self
employment generating units at affordable terms. The scheme envisaged 90 per cent
financial assistance in the form of loan from banks and 10 per cent promoter’s
contribution for setting up of units. Interest subsidy to the extent of five per cent on
the loan component was to be provided by the State Government. The position of
number of cases sponsored for loan, cases sanctioned by the banks and number of
cases where loan was disbursed in respect of test-checked districts of Kashmir
province is given in Table 2.21.13 below

of the units had never been ascertained, as a result, the actual setting up of units by the
beneficiaries could not be confirmed. In Jammu province, the position could not be

General Manager, DIC, representatives of two leading banks in the district, District
Employment Officer and District Social Welfare Officer as its members was to be
constituted. Audit noticed that no such task force had been constituted in any of the
test-checked districts.

Geology and Mining sector

The main objectives of the Geology and Mining wing of the Department is to carry
out geological mapping for ascertaining mineral wealth of the State and to carry out
drilling for groundwater exploration. The State is endowed with rich and wide variety
of minerals spread over an area of 13334 square kilometers. Around 60 per cent of
these minerals were reported to be commercially viable for mining.

150


Chapter-2-Audit Findings an Social, General and Economic Sectors (Non-PSUs)

2.21.25 Planning

For exploitation of mineral and natural resources, the Department was to formulate a
long term policy to plan and exploit mineral wealth of the State. The Mineral
Concession Rules, 1960 provides for approval of mining plan indicating plan of lease
hold area showing nature and extent of mineral body, spots where mining operations
are proposed, extent of manual mining or mining by use of machinery and mechanical
devices, details of scheme of restoration of the area by afforestation, land reclamation
and such other measures as directed by the Government, annual programme and plan
for excavation during five years. The mining operations are required to be carried out
in accordance with the duly approved mining plan. Test-check of records showed that
the Department had not formulated any such policy and that out of 17 minerals
explored in the State, the Department had exploited only five minerals in the State
(August 2012).

2.21.26 Collection of Revenue

The position of targets of revenue on account of royalty fixed by the Department and
actual revenue realised thereagainst during the period 2007-08 to 2011-12 is given in
Table 2.21.14 below:

Table-2.21.14

(@& In crore)

Year Targets fixed Achievements
